  • All questions carry equal marks.
  • Neatness in handwriting and clarity in expression carries weightage
  • Blank pages if any, to be struck by (X) at the end of each question.

Instruction 2 on the paper states that all questions carry equal marks, and six answered questions are required against a printed 'Total Marks - 100'. Every question is therefore recorded at 16 marks, so six answered questions total 96 against the printed 100. The discrepancy is printed on the source copy and is reproduced, not corrected.

Understand this first

This chapter exists because offshore wind created a category of person the 1974 Convention had no box for. A technician riding out to a turbine is plainly not crew - they are not there to work the ship. But if you call them a passenger, the ship becomes a passenger ship, and the whole passenger regime - subdivision, damage stability, life-saving scale, passenger ship certification - descends on a vessel that was built as a workboat and is carrying twenty-five people in survival suits who are trained, briefed and fit. That is disproportionate, and before chapter XV the industry ran on a patchwork of national authorisations under interim IMO recommendations. Chapter XV's answer is to invent a third category: industrial personnel, expressly not passengers (regulation 2.2), carried on a ship that stays a cargo ship (regulation 4) but must additionally meet the IP Code and hold its own certificate (regulation 5). Everything else in the chapter - the thresholds, the counting rule, the retrofit ladder - is bookkeeping around that single structural decision. Get that decision and the chapter reads itself.

Memory cue. Two questions answer the whole chapter. Who is on board? — a third category, industrial personnel, expressly not passengers (steps 1 and 3). What does the ship have to do about it? — stay a cargo ship, add the IP Code, add a certificate (steps 4 and 5). Application (step 2) is the bridge: 500, 12, and 1 July 2024.

Model written answer

(a) Objectives, scope, application and the meaning of “industrial personnel” — 8 marks

1. What chapter XV is, and the problem it was written to solve

SOLAS chapter XV, “Safety measures for ships carrying industrial personnel”, was adopted by resolution MSC.521(106) and entered into force on 1 July 2024. At this examination it is nine months old and fully operative law. Its technical content sits in the IP Code — the International Code of Safety for Ships Carrying Industrial Personnel, resolution MSC.527(106) — which regulation 1.2 defines and regulation 5 makes mandatory.

The objective is to close a gap the Convention had never addressed. The growth of offshore wind, and of offshore construction and servicing generally, created large-scale carriage of workers who are plainly not crew but who, if treated as passengers, would turn every workboat into a passenger ship. Before this chapter the trade ran on national authorisations issued under the interim recommendations in resolution MSC.418(97) — a patchwork with no common standard. Chapter XV replaces it with a mandatory international regime, and does so by creating a third category of person on board: the ship remains a cargo ship, and carries industrial personnel under an additional Code and an additional certificate.

The objectives can therefore be stated as four:

  1. To provide a mandatory, uniform international safety standard for the carriage of persons engaged in offshore industrial work, in place of divergent national authorisations;
  2. To recognise industrial personnel as a distinct class — trained, briefed, medically fit and equipped, and therefore not requiring the protection the Convention affords the travelling public;
  3. To keep the regulatory burden proportionate, by leaving the vessel within the cargo ship regime and adding only what the carriage of these persons genuinely requires; and
  4. To make the standard enforceable, by attaching a certificate to it and bringing that certificate within the ordinary port State control machinery.

The scope is correspondingly narrow and deliberate. Chapter XV regulates the ship — its construction, equipment, survey and certification when carrying these persons. It does not regulate the offshore facility, and it does not regulate the industrial work itself; that remains a matter for the coastal State and the operator.

2. Application - the ships, the operations and the four trigger points

Regulation 3.1 is the main rule: the chapter applies to cargo ships and high-speed cargo craft, of 500 gross tonnage and upwards, constructed on or after 1 July 2024, which carry more than 12 industrial personnel. Note what is absent — passenger ships are not in scope, and the chapter is not tonnage-blind. Regulation 3.5 fixes what “constructed” means by reference to regulation II-2/1.1.2.1 (with II-2/1.1.3) for cargo ships and X/1.4 (with X/1.5) for high-speed craft.

Existing tonnage is caught by three further paragraphs, and they are not the same rule:

ShipTriggerWhat is required
Reg 3.1 — built on/after 1 July 2024, ≥500 GT, >12 IPon entry into serviceFull compliance and certification
Reg 3.2 — cargo ship built before 1 July 2024, already authorizedfirst intermediate or renewal survey after 1 July 2024IP Code III/1, III/2 (except 2.1.7), IV/7 and IV/8
Reg 3.3 — HS cargo craft built before 1 July 2024, already authorizedthird periodical or first renewal survey after 1 July 2024IP Code III/1, III/2 (except 2.1.7), V/7 and V/8
Reg 3.4 — never authorized, any date of constructionbefore carrying more than 12 IPFull compliance and certification — no window

At this sitting the regulation 3.2 and 3.3 windows are open and running: part of the existing fleet has passed its qualifying survey and complies, and part has not yet reached it. Regulation 3.4 has no window at all — that is the distinction to state.

The operations are defined by regulation 1.3: offshore industrial activities are the construction, maintenance, decommissioning, operation or servicing of offshore facilities, related to — but not limited to — exploration and exploitation of resources by the renewable or hydrocarbon energy sectors, aquaculture, ocean mining or similar activities. Two features of that definition are worth stating. It covers the whole life cycle of a facility, from construction through operation to decommissioning — a growing trade in its own right. And it is technology-neutral and open-ended: “related, but not limited to” and “or similar activities” mean the chapter is not confined to wind and oil, and will catch sectors that did not exist when it was drafted. In practice the ships engaged are crew transfer vessels (CTVs), service operation vessels (SOVs) with walk-to-work gangways, and construction and accommodation support vessels.

3. Industrial personnel - the definition, and the counting rule that catches candidates

Regulation 1.1: industrial personnel (IP) means all persons transported or accommodated on board for the purpose of offshore industrial activities performed on board other ships and/or offshore facilities. The definition is purposive — it turns on why the person is on board, not on their trade, their employer or their qualifications.

So the term captures the wind-farm technician, the subsea engineer, the commissioning specialist and the survey party alike; and it excludes, by the same test, the ship’s own crew, who are on board to work the ship, and the genuine fare-paying or non-working passenger, who is on board for neither reason.

Three consequences must be stated. First, regulation 2.2: industrial personnel shall not be treated or considered as passengers. That single sentence is what keeps the vessel a cargo ship, and with it the whole proportionality of the regime. Second, regulation 2.3: wherever a number of industrial personnel appears as a parameter, it is the aggregate of industrial personnel, special personnel and passengers carried on board, and within that aggregate the number of passengers shall not exceed 12. So “more than 12 industrial personnel” is not a headcount of technicians: a ship carrying 8 technicians, 3 special personnel and 4 passengers is over the threshold, and the chapter contains its own internal ceiling — carry more than twelve genuine passengers and the vessel is a passenger ship and outside chapter XV altogether. Special personnel is the term the chapter borrows, by footnote, from the 2008 SPS Code (resolution MSC.266(84)).

Third, regulation 2.1 completes the picture and prevents the chapter from importing the passenger regime through the back door: where the IP Code refers to passenger ship requirements, the corresponding cargo ship requirements are deemed to be complied with. Without that rule, every passenger-ship cross-reference inside the Code would drag the vessel back towards the standard chapter XV exists to avoid.

(b) Safety measures, certification and the role of the flag Administration — 8 marks

4. The safety measures - what regulation 5 actually requires

Regulation 5.1 is the operative provision, and its three items each do different work. A ship to which the chapter applies shall:

  1. be certified as a cargo ship or high-speed cargo craft in accordance with chapter I, chapter VIII or chapter X, as applicable — the ordinary status is preserved, not replaced;
  2. meet the requirements of the IP Code — the additional technical standard for accommodation, means of escape, life-saving, personnel transfer and safety training appropriate to industrial personnel; and
  3. in addition to regulations I/8, I/9 and I/10 (or sections 1.5 to 1.9 of the HSC Code), be surveyed and certified as provided for in the IP Code.

The words “in addition to” carry the answer to “what safety measures”. Regulation 4 confirms it: the cargo ship regulations in the other chapters of SOLAS continue to apply, except as modified by chapter XV, and for HSC Code craft the cargo craft regulations of that Code apply on the same basis. The chapter is therefore additive, not substitutional — construction and subdivision, fire protection, life-saving, radio and safety of navigation are untouched, and the IP Code is layered on top to deal with the people.

That layering is the honest way to answer “what are the key safety measures”. They fall into two groups. The first group is unchanged and must not be omitted from the answer merely because it is not new: the ship carries its full Cargo Ship Safety Construction, Safety Equipment and Safety Radio certification (or the High-Speed Craft Safety Certificate), is surveyed under regulations I/8 to I/10, and complies with SOLAS chapters II-1, II-2, III, IV and V as any cargo ship must. The second group is what chapter XV adds, through the IP Code: the standards appropriate to carrying a large complement of non-crew — their accommodation and means of escape, life-saving appliances scaled to the total persons on board, safety briefing, familiarisation and training before they sail, and the arrangements for transferring them to and from the offshore facility, which is where the real hazard of this trade lives.

Regulation 2.4 gives the one significant deeming rule in the other direction: a high-speed craft certified under chapter XV and the IP Code is deemed to comply with chapters 2 to 12 and 18 of the HSC Code — so the chapter avoids double regulation of the same subject matter for high-speed tonnage rather than stacking two regimes on it.

5. Certification, survey and the role of the flag Administration

The document issued is the Industrial Personnel Safety Certificate. It is issued on an initial survey and maintained by surveys aligned to the ship’s existing harmonized survey cycle — for a cargo ship, to the maintenance and renewal surveys of the Cargo Ship Safety Certificate or the Cargo Ship Safety Construction Certificate; for high-speed cargo craft, to the periodical and renewal surveys of the High-Speed Craft Safety Certificate. The certificate is endorsed at maintenance survey and issued at renewal.

The alignment is deliberate and is the practical answer to “what are the certification requirements”: chapter XV does not create a separate survey cycle. For a ship already on the harmonized system, the initial survey is the first Safety Construction intermediate or renewal survey required by regulation I/10, whichever occurs first after 1 July 2024; for high-speed cargo craft it is the third periodical or first renewal survey under HSC Code paragraph 1.5 after that date. Thereafter the Industrial Personnel Safety Certificate rides on the same surveys as the certificates the ship already holds, which is why the retrofit dates in regulations 3.2 and 3.3 are expressed in survey events rather than in calendar dates.

The flag Administration is central at every stage:

  • Authorisation — it is the Administration that authorises a ship to carry more than 12 industrial personnel, and regulations 3.2 and 3.3 turn on whether it had already done so before 1 July 2024;
  • Survey — it surveys the ship against the IP Code, or delegates survey and certification to a recognized organization under the ordinary SOLAS arrangements, the RO acting on its behalf and to its standards;
  • Certification — it issues and endorses the Industrial Personnel Safety Certificate, and determines which paragraph of regulation 3 catches each ship on its register;
  • Interpretation and equivalents — it applies the chapter's own definitions of “constructed” and may accept equivalents under the general SOLAS machinery; and
  • Implementation in national law — in India the Convention takes effect through the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 and the rules made under it, the Directorate General of Shipping acting as the Administration.

The chapter has changed the character of that role. Before 1 July 2024 the Administration granted a discretionary national authorisation under interim recommendations that other States were free to read differently; it now certifies against a mandatory international Code, and the certificate it issues is recognised, and challengeable, everywhere.

Finally, regulation 5.2 closes the enforcement loop. A ship holding a certificate issued under regulation 5.1 is subject to the control established in regulation I/19 or XI-1/4 (and HSC Code 1.10), and for that purpose the certificate is treated as a certificate issued under regulation I/12 or I/13. That is what makes chapter XV enforceable by a port State at the ship’s side, and not merely by the flag on the register.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The paper prints 8 + 8, and the two limbs ask genuinely different things. Limb (a) is a reading test — objectives, scope, application, definition — and every one of those four is a separately markable object, which is why the answer gives it three sections rather than one. Limb (b) is a system test: it wants the candidate to see that regulation 5 is three items, that regulation 4 preserves the ordinary regime, and that the Administration sits at the centre of all of it.

The application ladder goes in a table deliberately. Four different trigger points written as prose are the single easiest place on this question to lose marks through sheer unreadability, and an examiner scanning for “first intermediate or renewal survey” will find it in a table and may not find it in a paragraph.

The one structural idea that unlocks the chapter

Industrial personnel are a third category of person, and the chapter exists to keep the ship out of the passenger regime. If you write nothing else, write that.

A technician riding to a wind turbine is not crew: they are not employed on the business of the ship. If the Convention called them a passenger, the vessel would become a passenger ship and would attract passenger subdivision and damage stability, passenger-scale life-saving, and passenger ship certification — on a hull built as a workboat, for a complement that is trained, briefed, medically fit and equipped with survival suits. That is disproportionate to the risk, and it is why regulation 2.2 says in terms that industrial personnel shall not be treated or considered as passengers.

Everything else in the chapter is bookkeeping around that decision. The ship stays a cargo ship (regulation 4); it picks up an extra Code and an extra certificate (regulation 5); and the thresholds and counting rule exist to say when.

At this sitting: nine months in force, and the transition is still running

Chapter XV and the IP Code entered into force on 1 July 2024. At an April 2025 sitting the chapter is therefore in force and being applied — not a draft, not “shortly to enter into force”, and not “recently adopted”. Say in force since 1 July 2024 and the currency mark is banked.

But the transitional ladder is live. Regulation 3.2 gives a previously authorized cargo ship until its first intermediate or renewal survey after 1 July 2024, and regulation 3.3 gives high-speed cargo craft until the third periodical or first renewal survey. Nine months into that window, some ships have complied and some have not yet reached the survey that obliges them. An answer copied from a later sitting will describe the retrofit as finished; here it is in progress, and saying so is worth a mark.

The counting rule — regulation 2.3

This is the most commonly missed provision in the chapter. “More than 12 industrial personnel” does not mean twelve technicians. Regulation 2.3: wherever the number of industrial personnel appears as a parameter, it is the aggregate of industrial personnel, special personnel and passengers, and the passengers within that aggregate may not exceed 12.

Two things follow. First, a ship can cross the threshold without carrying thirteen technicians — 8 IP + 3 special personnel + 4 passengers is 15 and the chapter bites. Second, the chapter contains its own internal cap: carry more than twelve genuine passengers and you are outside chapter XV altogether, because you are running a passenger ship. Special personnel is the 2008 SPS Code term, which the chapter cites by footnote.

Two resolutions, and they are not the same one

Candidates routinely attribute both the chapter and the Code to MSC.527(106). The chapter itself was adopted by resolution MSC.521(106); MSC.527(106) adopted the IP Code. Regulation 1.2 names only the second, because a chapter does not cite its own adopting resolution — which is exactly why the pairing is easy to get wrong. Getting both right, and in the right order, is a cheap discriminator.

Common mistakes

  • Calling industrial personnel a kind of passenger, or saying the ship becomes a passenger ship. Regulation 2.2 says the opposite, and the whole chapter depends on it.
  • Saying the chapter applies to passenger ships. It applies to cargo ships and high-speed cargo craft.
  • Omitting the 500 gross tonnage threshold, or the 1 July 2024 construction date, from the regulation 3.1 rule.
  • Reading “more than 12 industrial personnel” as a headcount of technicians, and missing the aggregate rule at regulation 2.3.
  • Treating regulations 3.2, 3.3 and 3.4 as one transitional provision. They are three, and 3.4 has no window.
  • Saying chapter XV replaces the ordinary cargo ship requirements. Regulation 4 preserves them; the chapter is additive.
  • Describing the IP Code's internal chapters in detail from memory. Name the Code and its role, and quote the regulation references chapter XV actually prints.
  • Forgetting the certificate's name, or inventing one. It is the Industrial Personnel Safety Certificate.
  • Leaving the Administration's role at “issues the certificate”. It authorises, surveys or delegates, issues, endorses, and applies the transitional ladder.

Examiner traps

  • “Is an industrial person a passenger?” No — regulation 2.2, expressly. That is the point of the chapter.
  • “A vessel carries 11 industrial personnel and 3 passengers. Does chapter XV apply?” The aggregate is 14, so yes on that limb — subject to the ship also being a cargo ship or high-speed cargo craft of 500 GT and upwards, and to which paragraph of regulation 3 catches it.
  • “Can such a ship carry 20 passengers as well?” No. Regulation 2.3 caps passengers within the aggregate at 12.
  • “A 2019-built CTV was authorized by its flag in 2021 under the interim recommendations. What must it do now?” Regulation 3.2 — a defined subset of the IP Code by the first intermediate or renewal survey after 1 July 2024, not the whole Code and not immediately.
  • “A 2019-built ship was never authorized and now wants to carry 20 technicians.” Regulation 3.4 — full compliance and certification first. Its date of construction buys it nothing.
  • “Which certificate, and how is it kept alive?” The Industrial Personnel Safety Certificate, surveyed in step with the existing Cargo Ship Safety or High-Speed Craft Safety cycle.
  • “Who can detain the ship for a chapter XV deficiency?” The port State. Regulation 5.2 subjects the certificate to control under regulation I/19 or XI-1/4 and treats it as an I/12 or I/13 certificate.

Likely oral follow-up

  • When did SOLAS chapter XV enter into force? 1 July 2024.
  • Which resolution adopted the IP Code? MSC.527(106). And the chapter itself? MSC.521(106).
  • Define industrial personnel. Persons transported or accommodated on board for the purpose of offshore industrial activities performed on board other ships and/or offshore facilities.
  • Give three offshore industrial activities. Construction, maintenance, decommissioning, operation or servicing of offshore facilities — renewables, hydrocarbons, aquaculture, ocean mining.
  • What is the tonnage threshold, and the personnel threshold? 500 GT and upwards; more than 12, counted as an aggregate.
  • Are they passengers? No — regulation 2.2.
  • Name the certificate. The Industrial Personnel Safety Certificate.
  • What did ships do before this chapter? Operated under national authorisations issued under the interim recommendations in resolution MSC.418(97).

Memory framework

Hold the chapter as five regulations in five words, in their printed order: DEFINE, GENERAL, APPLY, OTHERS, REQUIRE. Regulation 1 defines, 2 sets the general rules including “not passengers”, 3 applies, 4 preserves the other chapters, 5 requires.

For the thresholds, three numbers and one date: 500 · 12 · 1 July 2024. Five hundred gross tonnage, more than twelve people counted in aggregate, built on or after 1 July 2024.

For the transitional ladder, remember that the two already-authorized classes get a survey window and the never-authorized class gets nothing: authorized buys you time; unauthorized buys you nothing.

For limb (b), regulation 5.1 is three items and they run outward: certify as what you are → meet the Code → be surveyed and certified under the Code.

Regulation and source map

  • SOLAS chapter XV — adopted by resolution MSC.521(106), in force 1 July 2024.
  • Reg XV/1.1 — definition of industrial personnel. Reg XV/1.2 — the IP Code, resolution MSC.527(106). Reg XV/1.3 — offshore industrial activities. Reg XV/1.4 — the HSC Code, MSC.97(73).
  • Reg XV/2.1 — passenger ship references read as cargo ship requirements. Reg XV/2.2 — not passengers. Reg XV/2.3 — the aggregate counting rule, passengers capped at 12. Reg XV/2.4 — HSC Code chapters 2 to 12 and 18 deemed met.
  • Reg XV/3.1 — cargo ships and high-speed cargo craft, 500 GT and upwards, built on or after 1 July 2024, more than 12 IP. 3.2, 3.3 — retrofit windows for previously authorized ships. 3.4 — no window for the never-authorized. 3.5 — meaning of “constructed”.
  • Reg XV/4 — the other chapters continue to apply except as modified.
  • Reg XV/5.1 — certify as cargo ship or HS cargo craft; meet the IP Code; be surveyed and certified under the Code in addition to regulations I/8, I/9 and I/10. Reg XV/5.2 — control under I/19 or XI-1/4; treated as an I/12 or I/13 certificate.
  • Resolution MSC.418(97) — the interim recommendations chapter XV replaced. Resolution MSC.266(84) — the 2008 SPS Code, source of “special personnel”.
  • MSC.1/Circ.1680 — unified interpretation for chapter XV; names the Industrial Personnel Safety Certificate and aligns its surveys to the HSSC cycle.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

The IP Code itself is not held by the True Source corpus. Every statement above about the Code is taken from what chapter XV says about it — its identity, its mandatory status, the specific regulation references the chapter cites in the transitional paragraphs, and the survey and certification hook in regulation 5.1.3. The internal content of parts I to V of the Code is deliberately not asserted, and no IP Code provision is paraphrased. That is an accepted limitation recorded in the verification file, not an omission: the question asks for the safety measures and certification requirements under SOLAS chapter XV, with reference to the IP Code, and the chapter supplies that.

“Special personnel” is used exactly as chapter XV uses it. Its scope is fixed by the 2008 SPS Code, which the chapter cites by footnote and which is not held, so it is not defined here from any other source.

No commercial figures are given for offshore wind activity or for the number of ships certified. Those are market statistics, not regulatory facts.

Currency. The edition used is the SOLAS consolidated edition 2024, which incorporates all amendments in effect on 1 July 2024 — the chapter's own entry-into-force date, so there is no gap between the edition and the sitting. No amendment adopted after that date operates on chapter XV, and none is cited. Instruments postdating April 2025 are absent.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 5 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a What chapter XV is, and the problem it was written to solve
  2. __________a Application - the ships, the operations and the four trigger points
  3. __________a Industrial personnel - the definition, and the counting rule that catches candidates
  4. __________b The safety measures - what regulation 5 actually requires
  5. __________b Certification, survey and the role of the flag Administration
Flashcards 10

Regulation XV/1.1: all persons transported or accommodated on board for the purpose of offshore industrial activities performed on board other ships and/or offshore facilities. The test is purposive - why the person is on board, not their trade or employer.

The definition is a printed limb of the question and the word 'purpose' is what makes it work.

Chapter XV by resolution MSC.521(106); the IP Code by resolution MSC.527(106). Both entered into force on 1 July 2024. Regulation 1.2 names only MSC.527(106), because a chapter does not cite its own adopting resolution.

Attributing both to MSC.527(106) is the standard error, and the pairing is a cheap discriminator.

No. Regulation XV/2.2 says they shall not be treated or considered as passengers. It matters because if they were, the vessel would become a passenger ship and attract passenger subdivision, damage stability, life-saving scale and certification - on a hull built as a workboat. Regulation 4 keeps it a cargo ship.

This single provision is the structural decision the whole chapter is built on.

Cargo ships and high-speed cargo craft, of 500 gross tonnage and upwards, constructed on or after 1 July 2024, which carry more than 12 industrial personnel. Passenger ships are not in scope.

Three conditions plus a ship-type limit; candidates routinely drop the tonnage or the construction date.

Regulation XV/2.3: as the aggregate of industrial personnel, special personnel and passengers carried on board, where the number of passengers shall not exceed 12. So 8 technicians, 3 special personnel and 4 passengers is over the threshold.

The single most commonly missed provision in the chapter.

If already authorized under the interim recommendations, regulation 3.2 gives it until the first intermediate or renewal survey after 1 July 2024 to comply with IP Code III/1, III/2 (except 2.1.7), IV/7 and IV/8. If never authorized, regulation 3.4 applies and it must comply and be certified in full BEFORE carrying more than 12 - no window at all.

Three transitional paragraphs are routinely collapsed into one, and the no-window case is the distinction that scores.

Be certified as a cargo ship or high-speed cargo craft under chapter I, VIII or X as applicable; meet the requirements of the IP Code; and, in addition to regulations I/8, I/9 and I/10 or HSC Code sections 1.5 to 1.9, be surveyed and certified as provided for in the IP Code.

This is the whole of limb (b)'s legal content, and the words 'in addition to' are what make the regime additive.

The Industrial Personnel Safety Certificate. It is issued on an initial survey and maintained by surveys aligned to the ship's existing harmonized cycle - the Cargo Ship Safety or Safety Construction surveys for a cargo ship, the High-Speed Craft Safety Certificate surveys for high-speed cargo craft - being endorsed at maintenance survey and issued at renewal.

Regulation 5 does not print the name; it comes from the unified interpretation, so candidates who only read the regulation cannot supply it.

Regulation XV/5.2: a ship holding a certificate issued under regulation 5.1 is subject to the control established in regulation I/19 or XI-1/4, and in HSC Code 1.10, and for that purpose the certificate is treated as one issued under regulation I/12 or I/13.

It converts the chapter from a flag-side certification exercise into something enforceable at the ship's side.

National authorisations granted by individual Administrations under the interim recommendations in resolution MSC.418(97) - a patchwork with no common standard. Chapter XV replaced that discretionary authorisation with certification against a mandatory international Code.

It is the 'objectives' half of limb (a) and explains why the chapter exists at all.

Quick revision
15-second recall
SOLAS chapter XV, MSC.521(106), in force 1 July 2024. It creates a THIRD category of person — industrial personnel — who are expressly not passengers (reg 2.2), so the ship stays a cargo ship. Applies to cargo ships and high-speed cargo craft, 500 GT and upwards, built on or after 1 July 2024, carrying more than 12 IP — counted as the aggregate of IP + special personnel + passengers, passengers capped at 12 (reg 2.3). IP means persons carried or accommodated for offshore industrial activities on other ships or offshore facilities (reg 1.1). Older ships: previously authorized ones get a survey window (regs 3.2, 3.3); never-authorized ones must comply first (reg 3.4). Requirements are reg 5.1: certify as a cargo ship, meet the IP Code (MSC.527(106)), and be surveyed and certified under it in addition to regs I/8–I/10. Reg 4 keeps the other chapters alive. The document is the Industrial Personnel Safety Certificate, surveyed in step with the HSSC cycle, and reg 5.2 subjects it to port State control under I/19 or XI-1/4.
Keywords
SOLAS chapter XVMSC.521(106)IP Code MSC.527(106)in force 1 July 2024industrial personnelnot passengers - reg 2.2offshore industrial activities500 GT and upwardsmore than 12, aggregatespecial personnelIndustrial Personnel Safety Certificatereg 5.1 three itemsreg 4 - other chapters preservedreg 3.2/3.3 survey windowreg 3.4 no windowMSC.418(97) interim recommendationsport State control I/19, XI-1/4
Critical numbers
  • SOLAS chapter XV and the IP Code entered into force on 1 July 2024
  • Applies to cargo ships and high-speed cargo craft of 500 gross tonnage and upwards
  • The threshold is MORE THAN 12 industrial personnel, counted as an aggregate
  • Within that aggregate the number of passengers shall not exceed 12
  • Reg 3.2 - first intermediate or renewal survey after 1 July 2024
  • Reg 3.3 - third periodical or first renewal survey after 1 July 2024
  • Chapter XV has five regulations
Critical regulation
SOLAS regulation XV/2.2 — industrial personnel shall not be treated or considered as passengers — read with regulation XV/5.1, which requires the ship to be certified as a cargo ship or high-speed cargo craft, to meet the requirements of the IP Code, and to be surveyed and certified as provided for in the Code in addition to regulations I/8, I/9 and I/10.
Major trap
Reading “more than 12 industrial personnel” as a headcount of technicians. Regulation 2.3 makes it the aggregate of industrial personnel, special personnel and passengers, with passengers capped at 12 inside that aggregate — so 8 technicians, 3 special personnel and 4 passengers crosses the threshold. The paired trap is treating industrial personnel as passengers, which regulation 2.2 expressly forbids and which would defeat the entire purpose of the chapter.

Also on the platform: QP2504 Q2 - port State control, the enforcement route by which a chapter XV certificate is actually checked at the ship's side

  • PRIMARY: SOLAS regulation XV/1.2 - the IP Code is the International Code of Safety for Ships Carrying Industrial Personnel as adopted by the Maritime Safety Committee by resolution MSC.527(106)
  • PRIMARY: SOLAS regulation XV/1.3 - offshore industrial activities are the construction, maintenance, decommissioning, operation or servicing of offshore facilities related, but not limited to, exploration and exploitation of resources by the renewable or hydrocarbon energy sectors, aquaculture, ocean mining or similar activities
  • PRIMARY: SOLAS regulation XV/2.1 - where the IP Code refers to passenger ship requirements, the corresponding cargo ship requirements are deemed complied with
  • PRIMARY: SOLAS regulation XV/2.2 - for the purpose of the chapter, industrial personnel shall not be treated or considered as passengers
  • PRIMARY: SOLAS regulation XV/2.3 - where a number of industrial personnel appears as a parameter it is the AGGREGATE of industrial personnel, special personnel and passengers, the passengers not exceeding 12
  • PRIMARY: SOLAS regulation XV/2.4 - a high-speed craft certified under this chapter and the IP Code is deemed to comply with chapters 2 to 12 and 18 of the HSC Code

  • PRIMARY: SOLAS regulation XV/3.3 - high-speed cargo craft in the same position comply with IP Code III/1, III/2 (except 2.1.7), V/7 and V/8 by the third periodical or first renewal survey, whichever occurs first, after 1 July 2024

  • PRIMARY: SOLAS regulation XV/5.1 - the ship shall be certified as a cargo ship or high-speed cargo craft under chapter I, VIII or X, shall meet the requirements of the IP Code, and shall in addition to regulations I/8, I/9 and I/10 or HSC Code sections 1.5 to 1.9 be surveyed and certified as provided for in the IP Code
  • PRIMARY: SOLAS regulation XV/5.2 - certificates issued under 5.1 are subject to control under regulation I/19 or XI-1/4 and HSC Code 1.10, and are treated as certificates issued under regulation I/12 or I/13

Unresolved
  • The IP Code (resolution MSC.527(106)) is NOT held by the True Source corpus. Every statement in this answer about the Code is drawn from what SOLAS chapter XV itself says about it - the regulation numbers chapter XV cites, the deeming rules and the survey and certification hook. No IP Code chapter is summarised or quoted, and the detailed content of parts I to V of the Code is deliberately not asserted.
  • The precise scope of the phrase 'special personnel' is fixed by the 2008 SPS Code (resolution MSC.266(84)), which the chapter cites by footnote. The SPS Code is not held, so the answer uses the term as chapter XV uses it and does not define it from any other source.
  • No figure is given for the number of ships certified under chapter XV, or for offshore wind installation activity. Those are commercial statistics, not regulatory facts, and are not held.

It is correct at April 2025 - nine months into a window that runs to the first intermediate or renewal survey after 1 July 2024 - but it is precisely the kind of statement a later editor would modernise into the past tense. Confirm it is still framed as at the sitting before publication. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK)
  • No IP Code provision is stated beyond the regulation references SOLAS chapter XV itself prints — The IP Code (MSC.527(106)) is not held by the corpus. Recording this as an accepted limitation is a decision to publish an answer that explains the Code's ROLE without asserting its CONTENT, not a promotion of secondary material to primary. If the Code is later acquired, the answer can be deepened; it is not wrong without it. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • The certificate is named the Industrial Personnel Safety Certificate — Regulation 5 does not print the certificate's name; it is taken from the unified interpretation for chapter XV (MSC.1/Circ.1680) which is printed in the held consolidated edition. That is primary and sufficient, but the derivation is recorded so a reviewer does not look for the name in regulation 5 and fail to find it. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
Understand this first

This question is about a threshold and what happens when people disagree about whether it was crossed. Port State control is an intrusion on a foreign flag ship, so the Procedures constrain it: without clear grounds, the officer may look at the certificates and form a general impression, and that is all. Clear grounds is the key that unlocks the more detailed inspection, and from that inspection a detention may follow. So when a Chief Engineer disputes a detention, the dispute is almost always really about one of two things — whether the grounds were clear, or whether the deficiency found is genuinely detainable. And here is the part candidates get wrong: the system gives you a right of appeal but no stay. The ship does not sail while you argue. That single fact determines the whole of the correct behaviour in limbs (b) and (c) — you work the deficiency and build the record at the same time, because the fastest route to sea is rectification, and the record is what wins the review afterwards.

      • absence of principal equipment or arrangements required by the conventions

    2. 4Putting the disagreement on the record while the detention stands

      • the flag State may consult the port State, and paragraph 2.3.8 records that involving flag, RO and owner avoids later arguments about the circumstances of the detention

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

Memory cue. Two thresholds and one hard rule. The thresholds are CLEAR GROUNDS to look deeper (1) and DETAINABLE to hold the ship (2). The hard rule is APPEAL WITHOUT STAY — which is why the on-board answer is cooperate, record, rectify (3, 4) and the shore answer is company applies, flag speaks, class proves (5, 6).

Remember6 route headings
Cover52 core points beneath them

Memorise the 6 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 52 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Clear grounds - the definition, and why the term matters
  • A.1185(33) paragraph 1.7.2 - evidence that the ship, its equipment or its crew do not correspond substantially with the requirements of the relevant conventions
  • or that the master or crew members are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures relating to the safety of ships or the prevention of pollution
  • paragraph 2.4.1 supplies the force of the term - WITHOUT clear grounds the inspection is limited to valid certificates and documentation plus an overall impression
  • so clear grounds are not needed to board; they are needed to go further than the paperwork
  • paragraph 2.5.1 - clear grounds, or the absence of valid certificates, trigger the more detailed inspection
  • it is an evidence standard, not a suspicion - the PSCO must be able to point at something
2. The circumstances that establish clear grounds
  • paragraph 2.4.2 lists nine, and says expressly that they include but are not limited to
  • absence of principal equipment or arrangements required by the conventions
  • evidence from a review of the certificates that a certificate is invalid
  • required certificates or documents not on board, incomplete, not maintained or falsely maintained
  • serious hull or structural deterioration risking structural, watertight or weathertight integrity
  • serious deficiencies in safety, pollution prevention or navigational equipment
  • master or crew not familiar with essential shipboard operations, or those operations not carried out
  • key crew members unable to communicate with each other or with others on board
  • false distress alerts not followed by proper cancellation
  • a report or complaint that the ship appears to be substandard
3. Immediate actions on board - what a Chief Engineer does in the first hours
  • cooperate fully; a detention is not suspended by argument and obstruction converts a technical dispute into a conduct problem
  • attend the closing meeting with the master and ask the PSCO to identify, for each deficiency, the CONVENTION REFERENCE and the detainable ground relied on
  • obtain the inspection report - Form A and Form B - and check every entry against the machinery, the certificates and the records
  • sign as acknowledging receipt, not as agreeing; record disagreement in writing on the face of the report where the form allows
  • assemble the counter-evidence at once - certificates, survey reports, PMS records, alarm and test logs, spare-part records, photographs, parameter printouts
  • check paragraph 1.2.6 - an Exemption Certificate correctly referenced, or approved alternative design and arrangements documentation, means the ship is to be interpreted as complying
  • notify the Company and the DPA immediately; the company, not the ship, is the party with the right of appeal
  • raise it in the SMS as a non-conformity under ISM element 9, with root-cause analysis - this is required whether or not the detention is disputed
  • start rectification in parallel with the dispute; the fastest route to sea is a rectified deficiency, not a won argument
4. Putting the disagreement on the record while the detention stands
  • request the flag Administration's involvement at once - paragraph 3.5.2 requires the PSCO to notify the flag State in writing with the inspection report
  • the flag State may consult the port State, and paragraph 2.3.8 records that involving flag, RO and owner avoids later arguments about the circumstances of the detention
  • call the recognized organization that issued the certificate in question - the deficiency may impugn its survey and it has both standing and expertise
  • ask for re-inspection by, or in the presence of, a senior officer of the authority where the technical point is genuinely arguable
  • where a deficiency cannot be rectified at the port, paragraph 3.7.3 allows the authority to permit passage to the nearest appropriate repair yard chosen by the master and agreed by the authority
  • that route runs on conditions agreed between port State and flag State, which may include flag State confirmation that remedial action has been taken
  • keep a contemporaneous factual log of every exchange - times, names, what was said, what was produced
  • avoid asserting undue delay early; paragraph 2.3.8 shows the port State is already alive to it, and the claim is stronger made afterwards on a complete record
5. Reviewing the detention - what IMO provides, and what the Panel is
  • start with what A.1185(33) actually gives - paragraph 2.3.11: the company or its representative has a right of appeal, the appeal does not suspend the detention, and the PSCO must properly inform the master of the right
  • chapter 5 of the Procedures is Review Procedures but it is NOT an appeal body - it is a reporting and evaluation mechanism
  • 5.1.1 - the Organization summarises deficiency reports and disseminates them, indicating flag State action or an outstanding flag State comment
  • 5.1.2 and 5.1.3 - the appropriate committee periodically evaluates the summary and recommendations feed back into the instruments
  • so IMO reviews the SYSTEM; the individual detention is reviewed regionally
  • the DETENTION REVIEW PANEL is a mechanism of the regional port State control memoranda of understanding, not of IMO - India participates in the Indian Ocean MOU
  • the shape of the process: a written application by the company or the flag State that the detention was improperly imposed, within the time the MOU allows
  • the application is supported by the inspection report, the certificates, the technical evidence and the flag State's position
  • the Panel reviews the record and the parties' submissions and reports its findings to the detaining authority and the MOU committee
  • the outcome is the correction of the detention record - the detention is not undone in time, which is why the record matters commercially
6. The roles of the company, the flag State and class
  • the COMPANY holds the right of appeal under 2.3.11 and is the applicant - it funds, instructs and assembles the case
  • the company also owns the ISM consequence: the non-conformity, the root cause and the corrective action, which the flag State will look for
  • the FLAG STATE is the ship's principal advocate and the only party in a State-to-State relationship with the detaining authority
  • it receives the written detention report under 3.5.2, may seek and contest particulars, and agrees the conditions for any repair-yard passage under 3.7.3
  • the CLASSIFICATION SOCIETY appears in two capacities and they must be kept apart
  • as CLASS it certifies against its own rules; as RECOGNIZED ORGANIZATION it acted for the flag State in issuing the statutory certificate now challenged
  • 3.3.2 and 3.5.2 require the RO to be notified where appropriate, and its survey record is often the decisive technical evidence
  • its own performance is engaged - a detention against a certificate it issued is a matter for the flag State's oversight of it
  • the three roles are complementary, not alternative: the company applies, the flag State speaks, class proves
Model written answer

(a) “Clear grounds” and the circumstances that establish them — 4 marks

1. Clear grounds - the definition, and why the term matters

The operative instrument at this examination is resolution A.1185(33), Procedures for Port State Control, 2023, adopted by the 33rd Assembly on 6 December 2023.

Paragraph 1.7.2 defines the term: “Clear grounds: Evidence that the ship, its equipment, or its crew do not correspond substantially with the requirements of the relevant conventions or that the master or crew members are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures relating to the safety of ships or the prevention of pollution.”

The force of the term comes from paragraph 2.4.1. Where a PSCO inspects a foreign ship required to hold a convention certificate, the inspection should be limited to verifying that valid certificates and other relevant documentation are on board, and to the PSCO forming an impression of the overall condition of the ship, its equipment and its crew — unless there are clear grounds for believing that the condition of the ship or its equipment does not correspond substantially with the particulars of the certificates.

Two consequences follow, and stating them is what turns a definition into an answer. First, clear grounds are not needed to board; they are needed to go beyond the paperwork and the walk-round. Second, it is an evidence standard, not a suspicion — the officer must be able to point at something. Paragraph 2.5.1 then makes the consequence explicit: if the ship does not carry valid certificates, or the PSCO has clear grounds of the kind described, a more detailed inspection should be carried out.

2. The circumstances that establish clear grounds

Paragraph 2.4.2 enumerates nine, and says expressly that clear grounds “include but are not limited to” them — the list is a floor, not a closed set:

  1. absence of principal equipment or arrangements required by the relevant conventions, taking account of paragraph 1.2.6;
  2. evidence from a review of the ship’s certificates that a certificate or certificates are invalid;
  3. evidence that required certificates and documents are not on board, incomplete, not maintained or falsely maintained;
  4. evidence from general impressions and observations of serious hull or structural deterioration that may place at risk the structural, watertight or weathertight integrity of the ship;
  5. evidence of serious deficiencies in the safety, pollution prevention or navigational equipment;
  6. information or evidence that the master or crew are not familiar with essential shipboard operations relating to safety or pollution prevention, or that such operations have not been carried out;
  7. indications that key crew members may not be able to communicate with each other or with other persons on board;
  8. the emission of false distress alerts not followed by proper cancellation procedures; and
  9. receipt of a report or complaint containing information that a ship appears to be substandard.

Note the balance of the list: five of the nine are about documents or hardware, four are about people — familiarity, communication, operations not carried out, and a complaint. In practice it is the human limb that most often converts a routine visit into a detailed inspection of the engine room. Note also paragraph 1.2.6, which works the other way: where an Exemption Certificate carrying the correct reference is held, or approved alternative design and arrangements documentation is carried, the port State authority should interpret this as meaning that the ship complies.

(b) Immediate actions on disagreeing with a detention for engine room deficiencies — 6 marks

3. Immediate actions on board - what a Chief Engineer does in the first hours

Begin from the rule that governs everything else. Under paragraph 2.3.11 the company has a right of appeal, but “the appeal should not cause the detention to be suspended”. The ship does not sail while the argument runs. It follows that the correct conduct is to work the deficiency and build the record at the same time, and that obstruction achieves nothing except to convert a technical dispute into a conduct problem.

The immediate actions are therefore:

  1. Cooperate fully and remain professional. Do not refuse access, do not argue on the plates, and do not attempt to sail.
  2. Attend the closing meeting with the master and ask the PSCO to identify, for each deficiency, the convention reference relied on and the ground on which it is treated as detainable. A deficiency and a detainable deficiency are not the same thing, and this is where a disputed detention is usually won or lost.
  3. Obtain and check the inspection report (Form A and Form B) line by line against the machinery, the certificates and the records.
  4. Sign as acknowledging receipt, not as agreeing, and record the disagreement in writing on the face of the report where the form permits. Refusing to sign gains nothing and looks evasive.
  5. Assemble the counter-evidence at once — statutory and class certificates and survey reports, planned maintenance records, alarm and trip test logs, running hours, spare-gear inventory, photographs, and parameter printouts. Evidence gathered on the day is worth far more than evidence reconstructed a month later.
  6. Check paragraph 1.2.6. If the arrangement objected to is covered by an Exemption Certificate with the correct reference, or by approved alternative design and arrangements documentation, that is a complete answer and should be produced immediately.
  7. Notify the Company and the DPA without delay. The right of appeal belongs to the company or its representative, not to the ship, so the company must be brought in from the first hour.
  8. Raise it in the safety management system as a non-conformity under ISM Code element 9, with root-cause analysis and corrective action. This is required whether or not the detention is disputed, and its absence is itself a finding.
  9. Begin rectification in parallel. The fastest route to sea is a rectified deficiency, not a won argument — and under paragraph 3.7.1 the PSCO is in any event to endeavour to secure rectification of all deficiencies detected.

4. Putting the disagreement on the record while the detention stands

Bring the flag State in immediately. Under paragraph 3.5.2 the PSCO must, on detention, immediately notify the flag Administration in writing and include the report of inspection, and must likewise notify the recognized organization that issued the relevant certificates where appropriate; both are also notified in writing of the release. The flag State is the only party in a State-to-State relationship with the detaining authority, and paragraph 2.3.8 records the Organization’s own view that involving the flag State, the RO and the owner can produce a safer ship, avoid subsequent arguments relating to the circumstances of the detention, and prove advantageous in litigation involving “undue delay”.

Call the recognized organization. Where the deficiency concerns machinery covered by a certificate it issued, the RO’s survey record is frequently the decisive technical evidence, and its own position is engaged by the finding.

Ask for re-inspection by, or in the presence of, a senior officer of the authority where the technical point is genuinely arguable — and be prepared to demonstrate the machinery rather than to describe it.

If it cannot be fixed here, use paragraph 3.7.3. Where the deficiencies that caused the detention cannot be remedied at the port of inspection, the port State authority may allow the ship to proceed to the nearest appropriate repair yard, chosen by the master and agreed by that authority, provided the conditions agreed between the port State authority and the flag State are complied with — which may include flag State confirmation that remedial action has been taken. This is the practical escape from an unrepairable detention and candidates routinely omit it.

Finally, keep a contemporaneous factual log — times, names, what was asked, what was produced, what was said. And do not lead with an allegation of undue delay: the claim is real, but it is far stronger made afterwards on a complete record than asserted in the first hour.

(c) Requesting a review through the Detention Review Panel, and the roles of the parties — 6 marks

5. Reviewing the detention - what IMO provides, and what the Panel is

The distinction to draw first is the one most answers miss. A.1185(33) itself does not create a body that reviews an individual detention. What it gives is paragraph 2.3.11 — the company or its representative has a right of appeal against a detention, the appeal does not suspend it, and the PSCO must properly inform the master of that right. Chapter 5 of the Procedures is headed Review Procedures, but it is a systemic mechanism, not an appellate one: under 5.1.1 the Organization summarises deficiency reports and disseminates them, indicating flag State action or an outstanding flag State comment; under 5.1.2 the appropriate committee periodically evaluates the summary to identify measures for more consistent and effective application of IMO instruments; and under 5.1.3 resulting recommendations are fed back into the instruments themselves.

So IMO reviews the system; the individual detention is reviewed regionally. The Detention Review Panel is a mechanism of the regional port State control memoranda of understanding — for a detention in an Indian port, the Indian Ocean MOU, of which India is a member; in the Asia-Pacific region, the Tokyo MOU. Its rules are made by the MOU committee, not by the Organization, and they are not IMO instruments.

The steps to be followed are:

  1. Exhaust the local route first. Raise the disagreement with the detaining authority and, where the MOU provides for it, with the authority’s own internal review, before escalating.
  2. The company or the flag State submits a written application to the MOU secretariat contending that the detention was improperly imposed — that the deficiency was not detainable, or that it was wrongly attributed, or that the clear-grounds threshold was not met. The applicable MOU fixes the time limit and the form.
  3. Support it with the record: the inspection report, the certificates and exemption documentation, the survey and maintenance evidence, photographs, the RO’s position and the flag State’s own assessment.
  4. The detaining authority responds with its account and the PSCO’s justification.
  5. The Panel reviews the inspection record and the parties’ submissions and reports its findings to the detaining authority and to the MOU committee.
  6. The outcome is correction of the record — where the Panel finds against the detention, the detention is deleted or amended in the regional database. It does not restore the time lost, and it does not of itself award compensation.

That last point explains why the process is worth pursuing at all. A detention entry drives the ship’s and the company’s targeting factor, affects the flag’s and the RO’s regional performance, and is read by charterers and vetting inspectors for years. The commercial harm is in the record, so the remedy is to the record.

6. The roles of the company, the flag State and class

The three parties are complementary, not alternative: the company applies, the flag State speaks, and class proves.

PartyStandingWhat it actually does
CompanyHolds the right of appeal under paragraph 2.3.11Instructs and funds the challenge; assembles the technical case; owns the ISM non-conformity, root cause and corrective action under element 9; carries the commercial consequence
Flag StateThe ship’s principal advocate; the only party in a State-to-State relationship with the detaining authorityReceives the written detention report and inspection report under 3.5.2; seeks and contests particulars; agrees the conditions for any repair-yard passage under 3.7.3; supports or itself brings the review application; is notified in writing of the release
Classification societyTwo distinct capacities, which must be kept apartAs class, certifies the machinery against its own rules and attends to survey the disputed item. As recognized organization, it issued the statutory certificate now challenged, is notified under 3.3.2 and 3.5.2, and supplies the survey record that is often the decisive evidence — while its own performance is engaged before the flag State that authorised it

The practical sequence is therefore: the Chief Engineer secures the facts and the evidence on board; the company converts them into an application and into a corrective action; the flag State puts the case to the port State as one Administration to another; and the classification society or RO supplies the technical proof that the item complied when surveyed. Omit any one of the three and the case is incomplete — the company alone has no standing before the port State, the flag State alone has no evidence, and class alone has no locus to challenge a detention.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The printed split is 4 + 6 + 6, and the commonest failure is to write a beautiful four-mark answer worth twelve minutes. Limb (a) is a definition plus a list: state the definition close to the Procedures’ own words, then enumerate. The marks in limb (a) are in the enumeration, not in the prose around it, which is why the answer numbers the nine grounds instead of describing them.

Limbs (b) and (c) are both procedural and it is essential to keep them apart. (b) is on board and immediate; (c) is ashore and afterwards. Candidates who start the appeal in limb (b) find they have nothing left for limb (c) and lose six marks to repetition. The route puts the flag State notification in limb (b) because it happens immediately — but the review application in limb (c), because that is a later and separate act.

Limb (c) prints three actors and asks what each does. That is a table, and an examiner looking for three roles will find them in a table in five seconds.

The one rule that determines the whole answer: appeal without stay

Paragraph 2.3.11: the appeal should not cause the detention to be suspended. Everything sensible in limbs (b) and (c) follows from that sentence.

If arguing did stop the clock, the rational response would be to argue. Because it does not, the rational response is to rectify and dispute simultaneously — get the ship released by fixing the deficiency, and preserve the record so the detention entry can be challenged afterwards. A candidate who writes “I would refuse to accept the detention and demand the appeal be heard before the ship is held” has misunderstood the system and will be marked down for it.

The same paragraph adds a duty on the other side that is worth quoting: the PSCO should properly inform the master of the right of appeal. If that was not done, it is itself a point for the review.

Deficiency is not the same as detainable deficiency

This is the technical heart of a disputed detention and it is where the six marks in limb (b) actually are. A ship can leave port with recorded deficiencies; it cannot leave with a detainable one. Under paragraph 3.7.2 the trigger for detention is deficiencies clearly hazardous to safety or the environment, and paragraph 3.5.1 concedes that it is impracticable to define a ship as substandard solely by reference to a list of qualifying defects — guidance is given in appendix 2, but judgement remains.

So the right question at the closing meeting is not “is this a deficiency?” but “on what basis is this deficiency detainable?”, asked deficiency by deficiency, with the convention reference. That question, politely put and recorded, is the single most useful thing a Chief Engineer can do in the first hour.

Chapter 5 is not an appeal body - and knowing that is worth a mark

The stem names the Detention Review Panel, and a candidate who assumes it must be somewhere in A.1185(33) will look in chapter 5, “Review Procedures”, and misread it. Chapter 5 is about the Organization summarising deficiency reports, the appropriate committee periodically evaluating them, and recommendations being fed back into the instruments. It reviews the system, over years, in aggregate. It has nothing to do with one ship’s detention.

The Detention Review Panel belongs to the regional memoranda of understanding. India participates in the Indian Ocean MOU; the Tokyo MOU operates the equivalent mechanism in the Asia-Pacific. Drawing that boundary expressly — IMO sets the procedures, the regional MOU operates the review — shows the candidate knows how port State control is actually organised, and it is exactly the kind of distinction that separates a pass from a good mark.

The repair-yard route - paragraph 3.7.3

A detained ship is not necessarily stuck where it lies. Under paragraph 3.7.3, where the deficiencies that caused the detention cannot be remedied in the port of inspection, the authority may allow the ship to proceed to the nearest appropriate repair yard available, as chosen by the master and agreed to by that authority, provided the conditions agreed between the port State and the flag State are met — conditions that may include flag State confirmation that remedial action has been taken. The next port of call and the other parties are notified.

Two enforcement provisions sit behind it: under 3.7.5, if the ship sails without complying with the agreed conditions, the port State immediately alerts the next port, the flag State and others; under 3.7.6, if the ship does not call at the nominated repair port, the repair port’s authority alerts the flag State and the detaining port State. Mentioning the route and its conditions is worth more than mentioning either alone.

Common mistakes

  • Saying clear grounds are needed before a PSCO can board or inspect at all. They are needed to go beyond certificates, documents and an overall impression — paragraph 2.4.1.
  • Treating the nine grounds as exhaustive. Paragraph 2.4.2 says “include but are not limited to”.
  • Saying an appeal suspends the detention. Paragraph 2.3.11 says the opposite in terms.
  • Refusing to sign the inspection report, or refusing cooperation. Signature acknowledges receipt, not agreement; obstruction converts a technical dispute into a conduct finding.
  • Locating the Detention Review Panel inside A.1185(33). It is a regional MOU mechanism; chapter 5 of the Procedures is a systemic reporting and evaluation process.
  • Saying the company appeals to IMO. IMO operates no appellate jurisdiction over a detention.
  • Omitting the flag State from limb (b). Paragraph 3.5.2 puts it in the process from the moment of detention, in writing.
  • Confusing the classification society’s two hats — class under its own rules, and recognized organization acting for the flag State.
  • Quoting a specific time limit or panel composition for the review. Those are MOU-specific and differ between regimes.
  • Forgetting the ISM element 9 non-conformity, which is required regardless of whether the detention is disputed.
  • Missing paragraph 3.7.3, the route that lets an unrepairable ship move to a yard.

Examiner traps

  • “Define clear grounds.” Paragraph 1.7.2 — evidence that the ship, its equipment or its crew do not correspond substantially with the requirements of the relevant conventions, or that the master or crew are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures relating to safety or pollution prevention.
  • “The PSCO finds the oily water separator alarm bypassed. Clear grounds?” Yes — on at least two heads: serious deficiency in pollution prevention equipment (2.4.2.5), and arguably falsely maintained records (2.4.2.3). Expect the ORB to be examined next.
  • “Your ship holds an Exemption Certificate for the arrangement objected to. What follows?” Paragraph 1.2.6 — provided it carries the correct reference to the exemption provision and the requirement it relates to, the port State should interpret this as meaning the ship complies. Produce it immediately.
  • “You disagree. Can you sail while you appeal?” No. Paragraph 2.3.11 — the appeal does not suspend the detention.
  • “Who has the right of appeal — you, the master, or the company?” The company or its representative. The PSCO must inform the master of the right, but the right is the company’s.
  • “The main engine cannot be repaired in this port. Is the ship stuck?” Not necessarily — paragraph 3.7.3, the nearest appropriate repair yard, chosen by the master, agreed by the authority, on conditions agreed with the flag State.
  • “Which IMO body hears your appeal?” None. IMO reviews the system under chapter 5; the individual detention is reviewed by the regional MOU’s Detention Review Panel.
  • “What does winning the review get you?” Correction of the record — deletion or amendment of the detention entry. Not the lost time, and not compensation as such.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which resolution contains the Procedures for Port State Control, and when was it adopted? A.1185(33), 6 December 2023.
  • What is a PSCO limited to without clear grounds? Valid certificates and documentation, and an impression of the overall condition of ship, equipment and crew.
  • Give five clear grounds. Any five of the nine in paragraph 2.4.2.
  • Define detention. Intervention action to ensure the ship will not sail until it can proceed without danger to the ship or persons on board, or unreasonable threat of harm to the marine environment.
  • Does an appeal suspend a detention? No — paragraph 2.3.11.
  • Who must the PSCO notify on detention, and in what form? The flag Administration, immediately and in writing, with the report of inspection; and the RO that issued the certificates, where appropriate.
  • Where does the Detention Review Panel come from? The regional memorandum of understanding, not IMO.
  • What does the classification society do here? As class, surveys against its rules; as RO, it issued the statutory certificate and supplies the survey evidence.

Memory framework

Hold the threshold ladder in four rungs, in order: CERTIFICATES → IMPRESSION → CLEAR GROUNDS → MORE DETAILED INSPECTION, and then, only if what is found is hazardous, DETENTION. Every part of limb (a) is somewhere on that ladder.

For the nine grounds, group them 5 and 4: five about things — equipment absent, certificate invalid, documents bad, structure bad, equipment bad; four about people and reports — not familiar, cannot communicate, false alerts, complaint received.

For limb (b), the four verbs in order: COOPERATE, RECORD, NOTIFY, RECTIFY. For limb (c), the three actors in one line: company applies, flag speaks, class proves.

And carry one sentence for the whole question: you get an appeal, not a stay.

Regulation and source map

  • Resolution A.1185(33) — Procedures for Port State Control, 2023, adopted 6 December 2023 by the 33rd Assembly. The operative edition at this sitting.
  • 1.7.2 — definition of clear grounds. 1.7.3 — deficiency. 1.7.4 — detention. 1.7.5 — initial inspection.
  • 1.2.4 — apply only conventions in force and accepted. 1.2.6 — exemption certificates and approved alternative design and arrangements are to be read as compliance.
  • 2.3.8 — involving flag, RO and owner avoids later argument about the circumstances of detention and helps in undue-delay litigation. 2.3.10 — detention reports in sufficient detail. 2.3.11 — right of appeal; no suspension; master to be informed.
  • 2.4.1 — the limiting rule. 2.4.2 — the nine clear grounds, not exhaustive. 2.5.1 — more detailed inspection.
  • 3.4 — corrective action before permitting the ship to sail. 3.5.1 — detention guidance, appendix 2. 3.5.2 — immediate written notification of flag Administration and RO, and of release. 3.6 — suspension of inspection.
  • 3.7.1 to 3.7.6 — rectification and release, including the nearest appropriate repair yard at 3.7.3 and the alerts at 3.7.5 and 3.7.6.
  • Chapter 5, 5.1.1 to 5.1.3 — Review Procedures: summary of deficiency reports, periodic committee evaluation, feedback into instruments. Not an appeal body.
  • Treaty powers: SOLAS I/19 and XI-1/4; MARPOL articles 5 and 6; UNCLOS article 226 on avoiding undue delay to a foreign vessel.
  • ISM Code element 9 — reports and analysis of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences.
  • India: the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 and the DG Shipping circulars made under it are the domestic vehicle at this sitting.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

The whole IMO layer of this answer is primary and verified. A.1185(33) is held in full and every paragraph cited above was read in the held text.

The Detention Review Panel is not. No regional memorandum of understanding instrument is held by the corpus, and no DG Shipping circular is held either. What is established rigorously is the negative: A.1185(33) was read in full and contains no individual-detention review body, so the Panel cannot be an IMO creature. The positive account of the Panel — written application, supporting record, response by the detaining authority, findings reported to the authority and the MOU committee, correction of the record — is carried at authoritative-secondary level, and the answer says so in the text rather than hiding it.

No time limit, filing formality or panel composition is quoted. Those differ between MOUs and inventing one would be exactly the false precision the production protocol forbids. The applicable MOU fixes them.

No detention statistics, targeting-factor formulae or MOU list positions are given. They are regional performance data, they change annually, and none is held.

Currency. A.1185(33) is the edition in force at this sitting. The 34th Assembly adopted its resolutions on 3 December 2025 — eight months after this examination — so no A.1xxx(34) instrument appears in this answer, and its absence is deliberate rather than an oversight.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a Clear grounds - the definition, and why the term matters
  2. __________a The circumstances that establish clear grounds
  3. __________b Immediate actions on board - what a Chief Engineer does in the first hours
  4. __________b Putting the disagreement on the record while the detention stands
  5. __________c Reviewing the detention - what IMO provides, and what the Panel is
  6. __________c The roles of the company, the flag State and class
Flashcards 11

A.1185(33) paragraph 1.7.2: evidence that the ship, its equipment or its crew do not correspond substantially with the requirements of the relevant conventions, or that the master or crew members are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures relating to the safety of ships or the prevention of pollution.

It is a printed four-mark limb and the definition is worth stating close to the text.

Paragraph 2.4.1: verifying that valid certificates and other relevant documentation are on board, and forming an impression of the overall condition of the ship, its equipment and its crew. Clear grounds are not needed to board - they are needed to go beyond that.

This is what gives the term its force, and it is the half of limb (a) most candidates omit.

Absence of principal equipment or arrangements; evidence a certificate is invalid; required documents absent, incomplete, not maintained or falsely maintained; serious hull or structural deterioration risking structural, watertight or weathertight integrity; serious deficiencies in safety, pollution prevention or navigational equipment; master or crew not familiar with essential shipboard operations. Also: key crew unable to communicate; uncancelled false distress alerts; a report or complaint that the ship appears substandard. The list is expressly not exhaustive.

The marks in limb (a) are in the enumeration, and five of the nine are about hardware while four are about people.

No. Paragraph 2.3.11: the company or its representative has a right of appeal, the appeal should not cause the detention to be suspended, and the PSCO should properly inform the master of the right. The right belongs to the company, not to the master or the ship.

It is the single rule that determines the whole of the correct conduct - rectify and dispute in parallel.

A ship may sail with recorded deficiencies; it may not sail with a detainable one. Under 3.7.2 detention attaches to deficiencies clearly hazardous to safety or the environment, and 3.5.1 concedes that a ship cannot be defined as substandard solely by a list of defects. So the question to ask at the closing meeting is not 'is this a deficiency?' but 'on what basis is this deficiency detainable?', item by item, with the convention reference.

It is where a disputed detention is actually won, and it turns a complaint into a case.

Cooperate; attend the closing meeting and ask for the convention reference and the detainable ground for each item; obtain and check Forms A and B; sign for receipt not agreement and record the disagreement in writing; gather certificates, survey reports, PMS records, test logs and photographs at once; produce any Exemption Certificate or approved alternative design documentation under 1.2.6; notify the company and DPA; raise an ISM element 9 non-conformity; and begin rectification in parallel.

Cooperate, record, notify, rectify - four verbs that carry the six marks of limb (b).

Paragraph 3.5.2: the flag Administration immediately and in writing, including the report of inspection; and the recognized organization that issued the relevant certificates on behalf of the flag State, where appropriate. Both are also notified in writing of the release of detention.

It puts the flag State and the RO into the process from the first moment, which is what makes the later challenge possible.

Paragraph 3.7.3: the port State authority may allow the ship to proceed to the nearest appropriate repair yard available, chosen by the master and agreed by that authority, provided the conditions agreed between the port State and the flag State are complied with - which may include flag State confirmation that remedial action has been taken. The next port and other authorities are notified.

It is the practical escape from an unrepairable detention and is routinely omitted.

No. A.1185(33) chapter 5 is headed 'Review Procedures' but is a systemic mechanism - the Organization summarises deficiency reports, the appropriate committee periodically evaluates them, and recommendations feed back into the instruments. The Detention Review Panel is a mechanism of the regional port State control memoranda of understanding; India participates in the Indian Ocean MOU.

The stem invites the assumption that it is an IMO body, and drawing the IMO/MOU boundary is what distinguishes a good limb (c).

The company holds the right of appeal, instructs and funds the challenge, assembles the technical case and owns the ISM corrective action. The flag State is the ship's principal advocate and the only party in a State-to-State relationship with the detaining authority; it receives the detention report, contests particulars and agrees any repair-yard conditions. The classification society acts in two capacities - as class against its own rules, and as recognized organization which issued the statutory certificate now challenged and supplies the decisive survey evidence.

Company applies, flag speaks, class proves - and the two hats of the society are a standing distinction.

Correction of the record - deletion or amendment of the detention entry in the regional database. It does not restore the lost time and does not of itself award compensation. That matters because the detention entry drives the ship's and company's targeting factor and is read by charterers and vetting inspectors for years afterwards.

It explains why the process is pursued at all, and stops the answer promising a remedy the system does not give.

Quick revision
15-second recall
A.1185(33), Procedures for Port State Control, 2023, adopted 6 December 2023. Clear grounds (1.7.2) = evidence that ship, equipment or crew do not correspond substantially with the conventions, or that master or crew are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures. Without them, inspection is limited to certificates, documents and an overall impression (2.4.1); with them, a more detailed inspection follows (2.5.1). Nine grounds at 2.4.2, expressly not exhaustive — five about things, four about people. On a disputed detention: you get an appeal but no stay (2.3.11), and the right is the company's. So cooperate, ask for the convention reference and the detainable ground for each item, sign for receipt not agreement, gather evidence, produce any Exemption Certificate (1.2.6), notify company and DPA, raise an ISM element 9 non-conformity, and rectify in parallel. Flag State and RO are notified in writing by the PSCO on detention (3.5.2). If it cannot be fixed here, 3.7.3 allows passage to the nearest appropriate repair yard on agreed conditions. The Detention Review Panel is a regional MOU body, not IMO — chapter 5 of the Procedures reviews the system, not the ship. Company applies, flag State speaks, class proves; the remedy is correction of the record.
