Critical regulation
Resolution A.1185(33) paragraph 2.4.1 read with 1.7.2 and 2.5.1 — absent clear grounds an inspection is limited to verifying valid certificates and documentation and forming an impression of overall condition; clear grounds, being evidence of substantial non-correspondence or of unfamiliarity with essential shipboard procedures, unlock the more detailed inspection. Paragraph 2.3.11 then governs any dispute: the company has a right of appeal and the appeal does not suspend the detention.
Major trap
Believing that disputing a detention holds it in abeyance. Paragraph 2.3.11 states expressly that the appeal should not cause the detention to be suspended, so the ship stays where it is while the argument runs — which is why the correct conduct is to rectify and dispute in parallel rather than to refuse cooperation. The paired trap is looking for the Detention Review Panel inside A.1185(33): chapter 5 is headed 'Review Procedures' but reviews the SYSTEM through summarised deficiency reports and periodic committee evaluation, while the Panel that reviews an individual detention is a regional memorandum of understanding mechanism.

Also on the platform: QP2504 Q1 - SOLAS chapter XV, whose Industrial Personnel Safety Certificate is expressly brought within the regulation I/19 control machinery this question describes · QP2504 Q7 - vetting inspection, the commercial inspection regime that runs alongside port State control and answers a different question about the same ship

Unresolved
  • THE CENTRAL EVIDENCE GAP OF THIS QUESTION. The Detention Review Panel is not created by A.1185(33) and the corpus holds no regional memorandum of understanding instrument and no DG Shipping circular (the corpus records DGS-circulars as a placeholder). The Panel's composition, its filing deadlines, its procedural rules and the form of its decision are therefore NOT asserted from any held source. The answer explains the Panel's function and the parties' roles, states expressly that the detailed procedure is set by the regional MOU rather than by IMO, and quotes no time limit or membership rule it cannot source.
  • No numeric time limit is given for lodging a review application. Those limits are MOU-specific and differ between regimes, and quoting one wrongly is worse than stating that the applicable MOU fixes it.
  • The Indian implementation - the DG Shipping circulars and the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 rules governing PSC in Indian ports - is named as the vehicle but not quoted, because no DG Shipping instrument is held.
  • Detention statistics, target factors and MOU blacklist or greylist positions are commercial and regional performance data, not regulatory facts, and are not held or quoted.
Re-verify before publication
  • The Detention Review Panel is a regional memorandum of understanding mechanism and is not created by resolution A.1185(33) — Established negatively but rigorously: A.1185(33) was read in full and its only review provision, chapter 5, is an Organization-level summary-and-evaluation mechanism which does not review an individual detention. The positive account of the Panel's own procedure rests on authoritative secondary material because no MOU instrument and no DG Shipping circular is held. Recorded as an accepted limitation, with the boundary stated in the answer itself rather than hidden. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • No time limit, panel composition or filing formality is quoted for a detention review application — Those rules are MOU-specific and differ between regimes. Quoting one from memory would be false precision of exactly the kind the production protocol forbids. Deliberate omission, not a gap to be filled from an unheld source. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • A.1185(33), adopted 6 December 2023, is the operative edition of the Procedures for Port State Control at this sitting — Date-driven and known to have moved. The 34th Assembly adopted its resolutions on 3 December 2025, after this sitting. Any future editing must not modernise this citation to an A.1xxx(34) edition, which would be a post-sitting import. Re-check before publication and before the paper is placed alongside later sittings. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK)
Understand this first

The examinable idea is that a ship is an acoustic source whether anyone intended it or not, and that almost everything which makes it loud also makes it inefficient. Underwater radiated noise is not an emission in the MARPOL sense — nothing is discharged — but international law can still treat it as pollution, because UNCLOS defines pollution of the marine environment to include the introduction of energy. The mechanism is dominated by one thing: propeller cavitation. Below the speed at which cavitation begins, a ship is a relatively quiet machinery-noise source; above it, collapsing vapour bubbles generate broadband noise that rises steeply with speed and swamps everything else. That single fact organises the whole answer — it explains why the sources rank as they do, why slowing down is the most powerful single measure, why hull and propeller condition matter as much as design, and why the measures overlap so heavily with fuel-efficiency measures. And the regulatory point to hold on to is that none of it is mandatory at this sitting: the guidance is recommendatory, which changes the Chief Engineer's role from compliance to management-system governance.

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

Model written answer

(a) The concept of URN, its primary sources and its effects on marine life — 8 marks (the source copy prints no limb letter here)

1. What underwater radiated noise is, and how it is described

Underwater radiated noise (URN) is the sound a ship puts into the sea as an unintended by-product of propulsion, machinery operation and motion through the water. It is not deliberately produced and, critically, nothing is discharged — which is why it falls entirely outside the MARPOL discharge model and had to be approached through guidelines rather than through an Annex.

It is nonetheless capable of being pollution in international law, because UNCLOS article 1(1)(4) defines pollution of the marine environment as the introduction of substances or energy into the marine environment resulting in deleterious effects. Sound is energy, and article 194 obliges States to prevent, reduce and control pollution from vessels.

URN is described as a source level — in decibels referred to one micropascal at one metre — expressed as a spectrum across frequency. Three properties matter:

  • shipping noise is overwhelmingly low frequency, and low frequency propagates enormous distances, particularly in the deep sound channel — so a ship is heard far beyond the horizon;
  • the signature has two components: broadband noise from cavitation and turbulence, and discrete tonal peaks at shaft rate, blade rate and engine firing orders; and
  • measurement is standardised — ISO 17208-1 and 17208-2 — so that ships can be compared at all.

The concern is not one loud ship. It is the cumulative raising of the ambient noise floor across whole ocean basins by continuous merchant traffic, sustained over decades.

2. The primary sources on a merchant ship

There are three source groups, and which one dominates depends on the operating condition:

SourceMechanismWhen it dominates
Propeller cavitationVapour cavities form in low-pressure regions on the blade and collapse violently, radiating broadband noise. Tip vortex cavitation is usually first to appear and often the loudest single mechanismAbove cavitation inception speed — i.e. at normal service speed, where it swamps everything else
Machinery-borne noiseMain engine, gearbox, generators, pumps and compressors; structure-borne vibration transmitted through mounts, seatings and shell plating and re-radiated into the water. Carries the tonal contentBelow cavitation inception, at low speed, at anchor and in dynamic positioning
Hydrodynamic flow noiseTurbulent boundary layer over hull and appendages, flow over sea chests, openings and the bow waveThird in magnitude for most merchant ships — but it creates the wake the propeller works in, so it feeds source one

Cavitation is worsened by a non-uniform wake, by blade damage, roughness and fouling, and by operating off the design point — heavy loading, unusual trim, poor immersion. And the property that governs the whole of limb (b) is its strong non-linearity: cavitation noise rises very steeply with speed, so a modest reduction in speed produces a large reduction in radiated noise.

3. The effects on marine life

The harm is not a single phenomenon, and separating the mechanisms is what earns the marks here:

  1. Masking. Shipping noise occupies the same low frequencies that baleen whales and many fish use for communication, navigation and prey detection. It drowns out biologically important sound, shrinking the range over which animals can hear one another — a measurable loss of acoustic habitat. This is the principal chronic effect.
  2. Behavioural disturbance. Avoidance of shipping lanes and displacement from preferred habitat; interrupted feeding; disrupted breeding, nursing and mother–calf contact; altered diving and vocalisation.
  3. Physiological stress. Chronic exposure is associated with elevated stress responses, with downstream consequences for immune function, health and reproductive success.
  4. Auditory injury. Temporary or permanent threshold shift close to a powerful source. This is a proximity effect and is not the principal shipping concern, which is chronic and diffuse.

The range of affected animals is broad: marine mammals, which are the usual focus; fish, including commercially important species whose spawning aggregations are noise-sensitive; and invertebrates, many of which sense particle motion rather than pressure. The defining characteristic of the problem is that it is cumulative and chronic rather than acute.

(b) International guidelines, mitigation measures and the Chief Engineer’s role — 8 marks

4. The international framework - and the fact that it is entirely recommendatory

State the status first, because it governs everything else: there is no mandatory IMO requirement on underwater radiated noise at this sitting. MARPOL Annex VI contains no provision on it. This is a guidelines regime, addressed to designers, shipbuilders and operators, not a compliance regime with a limit to be met and a certificate to be held.

The printed question names MEPC.1/Circ.833, the 2014 Guidelines for the reduction of underwater noise from commercial shipping to address adverse impacts on marine life. That circular established the framework: it identified propeller cavitation as the dominant source, and directed designers and operators to hull form and wake field, propeller design, and onboard machinery, together with operational and maintenance considerations.

The currency point must be made. Circ.833 was superseded by the Revised guidelines, MEPC.1/Circ.906, approved at MEPC 80 in 2023 — roughly two years before this examination, so the revised circular is the operative edition at this sitting. The revision restructures the guidance around a URN Management Plan, and gives greater attention to measurement, establishing a baseline and setting reduction targets. It is accompanied by an Action Plan and a three-year Experience Building Phase intended to gather data and practical experience before any further step is considered. That phase is running at this sitting and has not reported, so nothing can be said about what may follow it.

Around the IMO layer sit UNCLOS article 194; the ISO 17208 measurement standards; voluntary class notations for underwater radiated noise; and port and coastal incentive schemes that reward quiet ships in sensitive waters.

5. Design measures

These are newbuilding and major-conversion measures. A serving Chief Engineer influences them chiefly through feedback and specification, but they must be known.

Propeller — the most productive single target, because it is the dominant source. Increased blade area ratio and skew; optimised pitch distribution and blade section; control of tip loading to delay cavitation inception. The design objective is to raise cavitation inception speed above the ship’s normal service speed, so that the loudest mechanism does not occur in normal operation at all.

Hull form — give the propeller clean water. Aftbody lines optimised for wake-field uniformity; wake-equalising ducts, pre-swirl stators and fin arrangements; and adequate propeller–hull clearance. A propeller in a badly disturbed wake cavitates unsteadily and is loud however well it is designed.

Machinery — break the structure-borne path. Resilient mounting of main and auxiliary machinery, including double-resilient arrangements for the noisiest units; flexible couplings and flexible pipe connections; acoustic isolation of seatings; damping treatments on radiating structure; and low-noise selection of pumps, fans and compressors. Diesel-electric or hybrid propulsion decouples engine speed from shaft speed and is often markedly quieter at low speed. Air lubrication and hull coatings contribute, and voluntary class notations exist to formalise the whole target at the design stage.

6. Operational and maintenance measures, and the Chief Engineer's role

Operational measures. Speed reduction is the most powerful single lever available, precisely because cavitation noise rises so steeply with speed. Alongside it: routeing to avoid known sensitive habitats and participation in voluntary slowdown schemes where they operate; avoiding operation far off the design point and avoiding heavy propeller loading and excessive rudder movement; trim and draught optimisation to keep the propeller properly immersed in clean inflow; and machinery load management so that nothing unnecessary is running.

Maintenance measures — and this is where the Chief Engineer’s real influence lies. Everything here is within the department’s control:

  • Hull cleaning and propeller polishing on measured condition, not to the calendar. Fouling roughens the blade surface and disturbs the inflow, advancing cavitation inception — a fouled ship is both slower and louder.
  • Propeller blade edge repair. Even minor edge damage or erosion is acoustically significant out of all proportion to its hydrodynamic cost.
  • Shaft alignment and bearing condition, and correction of imbalance and misalignment, which generate the tonal components of the signature.
  • Resilient mount condition. Mounts that are degraded, oil-soaked, bottomed-out or bridged by a rigid pipe or cable run short-circuit the acoustic isolation designed into the ship — an entirely invisible failure with a large acoustic cost.
  • Flexible couplings and pipe connections maintained so that structure-borne paths are not inadvertently rigidly bridged during repairs.
  • Vibration monitoring used as an acoustic proxy — a rise in structure-borne vibration is generally a rise in radiated noise, and the ship already has the instrumentation.

The Chief Engineer’s role in ensuring compliance must be expressed through the management system, not as a list of good intentions — and it is shaped by the fact that the regime is voluntary. There is no certificate to hold and no survey to pass, so the discipline has to be self-imposed and auditable. Concretely, the Chief Engineer should:

  1. Document the measures where they can be audited — in the SEEMP, whose keeping on board is a duty under MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26, and in the URN Management Plan where the company keeps one under the revised guidelines;
  2. Bring it into the safety management system under the ISM Code as an environmental protection objective, with procedures for hull and propeller condition management, mount inspection and vibration monitoring, and with training and reporting to match;
  3. Advise the master and the company where speed and routeing are the effective controls, since those are commercial decisions that the engine room can inform but not take; and
  4. Make the efficiency argument. Almost every measure that quietens a ship — a clean hull, a polished propeller, optimised trim, reduced speed, well-matched machinery load — also reduces fuel consumption, cuts CO2 and improves the ship’s CII rating. That overlap is what turns a voluntary environmental measure into something a company will actually fund, and stating it is the strongest close available to this answer.
Study guide

Why this structure scores

The printed question is unlimbed at the top — it runs straight from “Q3.” into the first task and prints “(8)” at its end, with only the second limb labelled “b)”. That is a source anomaly, it is preserved, and the answer simply gives the first limb a descriptive heading without inventing an “(a)” the paper does not print.

Limb (a) sets three tasks — concept, sources, effects — and each has a section. The sources go in a table because the examinable point is not the list but the condition dependence: cavitation above inception, machinery below it. A table shows that in one glance; prose buries it.

Limb (b) also sets three tasks, and the measures task is deliberately split into design and operational/maintenance. That split is not cosmetic: it separates what a Chief Engineer can only influence from what a Chief Engineer actually controls, which is exactly what the third task — the role — is asking about.

The one physical fact that organises the whole question

Propeller cavitation dominates above inception speed, and cavitation noise rises very steeply with speed.

Almost every mark in this question can be derived from that sentence. It explains why the propeller is the top source and the design priority. It explains why slowing down is the single most powerful mitigation. It explains why hull fouling and blade damage matter acoustically — they advance inception. It explains why the sources reorder at low speed and at anchor, where machinery-borne noise takes over. And it explains the efficiency overlap, since the same conditions that cause cavitation also waste power.

Answer the circular the paper names - then say which one is current

The stem cites MEPC.1/Circ.833, which is the 2014 guidelines and was superseded by the revised circular MEPC.1/Circ.906, approved at MEPC 80 in 2023. Both dates are before this sitting.

The right technique — in the examination and in this answer — is not to correct the examiner and not to answer only on the superseded text. Name the circular the paper names, describe what it established, and then state plainly that the revised guidelines are the operative edition at the time of writing. The stem itself says “e.g.”, so it is not insisting on that circular exclusively. A candidate who does this cannot be marked wrong on either edition and picks up a currency mark that most will miss.

There is no regulation here, and saying so is worth marks

This is the most commonly mishandled feature of the question. Candidates write about URN as though it were regulated — “compliance with the noise limits”, “the noise certificate” — and there is no limit and no certificate. At April 2025 the regime is wholly recommendatory. MARPOL Annex VI, which is the natural place to look, contains nothing on it.

That changes what “the role of the Chief Engineer in ensuring compliance” can even mean. There is no statutory compliance to ensure. What there is, is company policy, management-system governance and documentation — which is why the answer routes the role through the SEEMP, the URN Management Plan and the ISM environmental objective, all of which are auditable, rather than through a survey that does not exist.

The four mechanisms of harm - do not write one blur

“It disturbs whales” is worth very little. Four named mechanisms are worth a great deal more, and they are genuinely distinct:

  • Masking — the chronic, population-scale effect, and the one that matters most for shipping. Loss of communication range is loss of acoustic habitat.
  • Behavioural disturbance — avoidance, displacement, interrupted feeding and breeding.
  • Physiological stress — chronic, sub-lethal, with reproductive consequences.
  • Auditory injury — threshold shift, but only close to a powerful source, and therefore not the main shipping issue.

Add breadth beyond mammals: fish, whose spawning aggregations are noise-sensitive, and invertebrates, which largely detect particle motion rather than pressure — a distinction that shows real reading.

Common mistakes

  • Treating URN as a regulated emission with limits and a certificate. It is recommendatory at this sitting.
  • Looking for it in MARPOL. Nothing in Annex VI or any other Annex addresses underwater noise.
  • Naming machinery as the dominant source. It dominates only below cavitation inception — at service speed the propeller dominates.
  • Quoting decibel figures or source levels from memory. They are ship-, speed- and condition-specific, and a wrong number is worse than none.
  • Writing the effects as one undifferentiated statement instead of four mechanisms.
  • Giving only newbuilding design measures. The examiner asks for operational, design and maintenance measures — and the Chief Engineer's marks are in the last two.
  • Omitting hull and propeller condition. Fouling and blade damage are acoustic problems, not just efficiency ones.
  • Forgetting resilient mount condition, which is the classic invisible failure — an isolation system defeated by an oil-soaked mount or a rigid pipe bridging it.
  • Saying only that Circ.833 applies, when the revised guidelines govern at this sitting; or conversely, ignoring Circ.833 when the paper names it.
  • Missing the closing argument — that quieting and fuel efficiency very largely coincide.

Examiner traps

  • “Which MARPOL Annex covers underwater noise?” None. It is a guidelines subject and there is no mandatory instrument.
  • “How can noise be pollution if nothing is discharged?” UNCLOS article 1(1)(4) defines pollution to include the introduction of energy into the marine environment.
  • “What is the loudest thing on your ship, acoustically?” The propeller, once cavitating — above cavitation inception speed. Below it, the machinery.
  • “Your ship is at anchor with one generator running. What is the source?” Machinery-borne, structure-borne into the hull and re-radiated. No cavitation, so the ranking inverts.
  • “You reduce speed by 10%. What happens to the noise?” It falls disproportionately, because cavitation noise depends very strongly on speed. Give the mechanism, not a number.
  • “Does a foul hull make a ship noisier?” Yes — roughness and disturbed wake advance cavitation inception and make cavitation more unsteady.
  • “What is tip vortex cavitation?” Cavitation in the vortex shed from the blade tip; usually the first to appear as loading increases, and often the loudest single mechanism.
  • “Where would you record your URN measures?” In the SEEMP, and in the URN Management Plan under the revised guidelines; and as an environmental objective in the SMS.
  • “Why would an owner spend money on this when it is voluntary?” Because nearly every measure also saves fuel and improves CII, and because port incentive schemes and charterer expectations reward it.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What does URN stand for and how is it expressed? Underwater radiated noise; source level in dB re 1 micropascal at 1 m, as a spectrum.
  • Name the three source groups in order of importance at service speed. Propeller cavitation, machinery-borne noise, hydrodynamic flow noise.
  • Which IMO circular does the syllabus name, and which edition is current? MEPC.1/Circ.833 (2014); the revised guidelines MEPC.1/Circ.906, approved at MEPC 80 in 2023.
  • Is compliance mandatory? No - the regime is recommendatory.
  • Name four ways noise harms marine life. Masking, behavioural disturbance, physiological stress, auditory injury.
  • Give three maintenance measures within your control. Hull cleaning and propeller polishing on condition; propeller blade edge repair; resilient mount and alignment condition.
  • What single operational measure helps most? Speed reduction.
  • Where do the measures get documented? SEEMP, URN Management Plan, and the SMS as an environmental objective.

Memory framework

For the sources, three words in descending order at service speed: PROPELLER, MACHINERY, FLOW — and one caveat: the order inverts at low speed.

For the effects, four words: MASK, MOVE, STRESS, INJURE. Masking is the chronic one that matters; injury is the acute one that mostly does not.

For the measures, work down the ship: PROPELLER → HULL → MACHINERY for design; SPEED → ROUTE → CONDITION for operations and maintenance.

And carry one closing sentence, because it is the highest-value thing in the answer: a quiet ship is usually an efficient ship.

Regulation and source map

  • MEPC.1/Circ.833 — 2014 Guidelines for the reduction of underwater noise from commercial shipping to address adverse impacts on marine life. The circular the printed stem names.
  • MEPC.1/Circ.906 — Revised guidelines on the reduction of underwater radiated noise from shipping, approved at MEPC 80 (2023); supersedes Circ.833 and is the operative edition at this sitting. Accompanied by an Action Plan and a three-year Experience Building Phase, which is running and has not reported.
  • MARPOL Annex VI — contains no provision on underwater radiated noise. Regulation 26 requires the SEEMP, which is where voluntary measures are documented; regulation 22 is the EEDI, establishing propulsive efficiency as a design-stage subject.
  • UNCLOS article 1(1)(4) — pollution includes the introduction of energy. Article 194 — the duty to prevent, reduce and control pollution from vessels.
  • ISO 17208-1 and 17208-2 — measurement of underwater sound from ships.
  • ISM Code — element 1.2.2 (safety and environmental protection objectives) and element 7 (shipboard operations) are the hooks for making a voluntary measure auditable.
  • Voluntary class notations and port incentive schemes — named as categories; no society or scheme is identified, because none is held.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

This question rests on the thinnest corpus evidence of any on the paper, and that is stated rather than concealed. Neither MEPC.1/Circ.833 nor MEPC.1/Circ.906 is held by the True Source corpus in any form — a full-text search for both circular numbers and for “underwater radiated noise” returns nothing. Their identity, dates, supersession and structure are therefore carried at authoritative-secondary level and are flagged for currency check before publication.

What IS primary is the negative, and it is the most important single statement in the answer. MARPOL Annex VI is held complete, verified against the official IMO resolutions and frozen, with its amendment chain closed through MEPC.392(82) — and it contains no provision on underwater radiated noise. The absence of a mandatory requirement is therefore established from a complete instrument rather than inferred from silence in a partial one.

No paragraph of either circular is cited by number, because that precision cannot be sourced. No recommendation is attributed to a specific paragraph.

No decibel value, source level, frequency band or reduction target appears anywhere in this answer, and no quantified noise reduction is attributed to any mitigation measure. Underwater sound levels are wholly ship-, speed- and condition-specific, and mitigation gains are installation-specific and vendor-sourced. The answer gives mechanism and direction only, which is a deliberate application of the production protocol’s rule against false precision.

No species-specific hearing range or behavioural threshold is quoted. The biological literature is outside the corpus and the thresholds are contested; the answer describes the four mechanisms rather than assigning numbers to them.

Currency. The position stated is that at April 2025: the revised guidelines are the operative edition, the Experience Building Phase is running and has not reported, and there is no mandatory measure. Nothing is asserted about what may follow. MEPC 83 sat inside this sitting month and adopted its resolutions on 11 April 2025, but none of its outputs touches this subject, so this question’s correctness does not depend on the examination day. Instruments postdating April 2025 are absent.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a What underwater radiated noise is, and how it is described
  2. __________a The primary sources on a merchant ship

Flashcards 10

Sound a ship puts into the sea as an unintended by-product of propulsion, machinery and motion through the water. It is pollution because UNCLOS article 1(1)(4) defines pollution of the marine environment to include the introduction of substances OR ENERGY resulting in deleterious effects; sound is energy. Article 194 obliges States to control it from vessels.

It explains why the subject sits outside MARPOL's discharge model and had to be approached through guidelines.

Propeller cavitation - dominant above cavitation inception speed, i.e. at normal service speed, with tip vortex cavitation usually first and loudest. Machinery-borne noise - main engine, gearbox, generators, pumps, transmitted as structure-borne vibration and re-radiated; dominant below inception, at anchor and in DP, and it carries the tonal content. Hydrodynamic flow noise - boundary layer, appendages, openings; third, but it makes the wake the propeller works in.

The condition dependence is the examinable point; a flat list of three misses it.

No. The regime is wholly recommendatory - guidelines addressed to designers, builders and operators, with no limit, no survey and no certificate. MARPOL Annex VI contains no provision on underwater noise at all.

Candidates routinely write about compliance with noise limits that do not exist, and this changes what the Chief Engineer's role can even mean.

The stem names MEPC.1/Circ.833, the 2014 Guidelines for the reduction of underwater noise from commercial shipping. It was superseded by the Revised guidelines MEPC.1/Circ.906, approved at MEPC 80 in 2023, which is the operative edition at this sitting and which reorganises the guidance around a URN Management Plan, measurement and target-setting.

Naming the circular the paper names and then stating the current edition cannot be marked wrong either way, and picks up a currency mark most candidates miss.

Masking - shipping noise occupies the same low frequencies animals use, drowning out communication, navigation and prey detection, and shrinking acoustic habitat. Behavioural disturbance - avoidance, displacement, interrupted feeding and breeding. Physiological stress - chronic, with reproductive consequences. Auditory injury - temporary or permanent threshold shift, but only close to a powerful source.

Four named mechanisms score; 'it disturbs whales' does not. Masking is the chronic effect that matters for shipping.

Because cavitation is the dominant source at service speed and cavitation noise rises very steeply and non-linearly with speed. Reducing speed can take the ship below, or nearer to, cavitation inception, so the loudest mechanism weakens disproportionately. Give the mechanism rather than a figure - the relationship is ship-specific.

It is the one measure that needs no capital expenditure, and the reasoning shows the physics has been understood.

Hull cleaning and propeller polishing planned on measured condition rather than to a calendar; propeller blade edge repair, since minor erosion is acoustically significant; shaft alignment and bearing condition, and correction of imbalance that generates tonal noise; resilient mount condition, since degraded, oil-soaked or bridged mounts defeat the designed isolation; and vibration monitoring used as an acoustic proxy.

This is where the Chief Engineer's marks are - design measures belong to the newbuilding, maintenance belongs to the department.

To raise the cavitation inception speed above the ship's normal service speed, so the dominant noise mechanism does not occur in ordinary operation. Achieved through increased blade area ratio and skew, optimised pitch distribution and blade section, and control of tip loading - supported by hull-form work that gives the propeller a uniform wake to work in.

It converts a vague 'better propeller design' into a stated engineering target.

Through the management system, because there is no survey. Document the measures in the SEEMP - which MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 requires to be kept on board - and in the URN Management Plan where the company keeps one; embed them in the SMS under ISM as an environmental protection objective with procedures, training and reporting; advise the master and company where speed and routeing are the effective controls; and justify it on fuel, since almost every quieting measure also improves CII.

It answers the printed question honestly given that no statutory compliance exists to ensure.

Yes, and significantly. Fouling roughens the blade and hull surfaces and disturbs the inflow to the propeller, which advances cavitation inception and makes the cavitation that does occur more unsteady. A fouled ship is both slower and louder, which is why hull and propeller condition is an acoustic measure and not only an efficiency one.

It links the maintenance limb to the physics limb and is the clearest example of the efficiency overlap.

Quick revision
15-second recall
URN is sound a ship puts into the sea as an unintended by-product. No discharge, so MARPOL does not reach it — but UNCLOS article 1(1)(4) defines pollution to include the introduction of energy. Expressed as a source level in dB re 1 microPa at 1 m, mostly low frequency, broadband plus tones; ISO 17208 measures it. Three sources: propeller cavitation dominant above inception speed (tip vortex first and loudest), machinery-borne below it and at anchor, hydrodynamic flow third. Cavitation noise rises very steeply with speed. Four harms: masking (the chronic one), behavioural disturbance, physiological stress, auditory injury. Framework: the stem names MEPC.1/Circ.833 (2014); it was superseded by the revised guidelines MEPC.1/Circ.906, MEPC 80, 2023, with an Action Plan and a three-year Experience Building Phase still running. The whole regime is recommendatory — no limit, no certificate. Design: propeller blade area, skew, tip loading to raise inception speed; wake-field and hull form; resilient mounting. Operations and maintenance: slow down, route away from sensitive habitat, clean hull and polished propeller on condition, blade edge repair, alignment, mount condition, vibration monitoring. Chief Engineer: document in the SEEMP (reg 26) and URN Management Plan, embed in the SMS, advise on speed and routeing — and argue it on fuel, because a quiet ship is usually an efficient ship.

Critical numbers
  • Source level is expressed in decibels referred to one micropascal at one metre
  • MEPC.1/Circ.833 dates from 2014
  • The revised guidelines MEPC.1/Circ.906 were approved at MEPC 80 in 2023
  • The Experience Building Phase runs for about three years and had not reported at this sitting
  • Three source groups; four mechanisms of harm to marine life
  • Measurement standards ISO 17208-1 and ISO 17208-2
  • No decibel value or reduction target is quoted anywhere - they are ship, speed and condition specific
Critical regulation
There is no mandatory instrument. The subject is governed by recommendatory IMO guidelines — MEPC.1/Circ.833 of 2014, superseded by the Revised guidelines MEPC.1/Circ.906 approved at MEPC 80 in 2023 — sitting beneath UNCLOS article 194 and the article 1(1)(4) definition of pollution as including the introduction of energy. MARPOL Annex VI contains nothing on underwater radiated noise, and the SEEMP required by its regulation 26 is simply the auditable document in which voluntary measures are recorded.
Major trap
Writing about underwater radiated noise as though it were a regulated emission — referring to noise limits, compliance and a certificate. At this sitting the regime is entirely recommendatory: there is no limit, no survey and no certificate, and MARPOL Annex VI contains no provision on it at all. The paired technical trap is naming machinery as the dominant source: machinery-borne noise dominates only below cavitation inception speed, and at normal service speed propeller cavitation swamps everything else.

Also on the platform: QP2504 Q6 - the CII regime, whose fuel-saving operational measures are very largely the same measures that reduce underwater radiated noise

Understand this first

The single most useful thing to hold about OPRC is what it deliberately is not. It is not a prevention convention — that is MARPOL. It is not a liability or compensation convention — that is CLC, FUND and Bunkers. OPRC assumes the spill has already happened and asks a different question: is anybody ready, and can they work together? Its three nouns are in its title — Preparedness, Response, Co-operation — and every article serves one of them. The second useful thing is structural, and it is where most answers go wrong: OPRC binds States, not ships. It tells a Party to require its ships to carry an emergency plan; the duty that actually reaches the vessel, with thresholds, contents and an approval requirement, is MARPOL Annex I regulation 37. So the third task in this question is answered out of MARPOL even though the question is about OPRC — and saying so is the best single sentence available here.

Cover54 core points beneath them

Memorise the 6 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 54 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. What OPRC is, and the casualty that produced it
  • the International Convention on Oil Pollution Preparedness, Response and Co-operation, 1990
  • adopted 30 November 1990, entered into force 13 May 1995
  • developed after the Exxon Valdez grounding of 1989 exposed the absence of any international framework for co-ordinated response
  • the premise is that no single State or operator has the resources to answer a major spill alone
  • it is a framework convention - it obliges Parties to build capability, rather than setting a discharge standard
  • extended to hazardous and noxious substances by the OPRC-HNS Protocol, 2000
2. Purpose and objectives - and what OPRC deliberately is not
  • the three objectives are the three nouns of the title - preparedness, response, co-operation
  • PREPAREDNESS - plans, designated authorities, pre-positioned equipment, exercises and training, in place before the incident
  • RESPONSE - a system that can act promptly and effectively, with a known chain of command and contact points
  • CO-OPERATION - mutual assistance between States, and the removal of the legal and administrative friction that delays it
  • the supporting objectives - reporting so that a spill is known about at once, information exchange, research and development, and technical assistance to developing countries
  • IT IS NOT A PREVENTION CONVENTION - MARPOL sets the discharge and construction standards
  • IT IS NOT A LIABILITY OR COMPENSATION CONVENTION - CLC, FUND and Bunkers do that work
  • saying what it is not is worth as much as saying what it is
3. The architecture - what the Convention obliges Parties to build
  • article 3 - oil pollution emergency plans, for ships, offshore units, sea ports and oil handling facilities
  • article 4 - oil pollution reporting procedures, requiring masters and others to report without delay to the nearest coastal State
  • article 5 - action on receiving a report: assess, and notify affected States and the Organization
  • article 6 - national and regional systems for preparedness and response, the core building obligation
  • article 6 requires a national contingency plan, a designated competent national authority and an operational contact point
  • and a minimum level of pre-positioned response equipment, a programme of exercises and training, and detailed plans and communications capability
  • article 7 - co-operation in pollution response, assistance on request, subject to capability
  • articles 8 and 9 - research and development, and technical co-operation with developing countries
  • article 10 - promotion of bilateral and multilateral agreements, which is where regional arrangements come from
  • article 12 - the institutional role, placing information services, education, training and technical services with IMO
4. The responsibilities of the flag State
  • separate the flag State's duties from the same State's coastal State duties - conflating them is the commonest error here
  • REQUIRE THE PLAN - article 3 obliges the Party to require ships flying its flag to have on board an oil pollution emergency plan as required by and in accordance with provisions adopted by the Organization
  • APPROVE THE PLAN - the approval requirement comes through MARPOL Annex I regulation 37.1, the plan being approved by the Administration
  • REQUIRE REPORTING - article 4 obliges the Party to require masters and persons in charge of ships flying its flag to report without delay
  • ENSURE THE PLAN IS USABLE - regulation 37.2 requires it to be in the working language of the master and officers, and based on Organization guidelines
  • SURVEY, INSPECT AND ENFORCE - verify the plan exists, is approved and is current, through the statutory survey regime and through action on its own ships
  • TRAIN AND EXERCISE - ensure crews are drilled on the plan, which for the flag State is discharged through the ISM Code and the SMS
  • COOPERATE AND COMMUNICATE - respond to requests for information, and support the coastal State receiving a report from one of its ships
  • IMPLEMENT DOMESTICALLY - give the Convention force in national law; in India at this sitting through the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 and rules made under it
  • NOTE WHAT IS NOT A FLAG DUTY - the national contingency plan, the pre-positioned equipment and the response organisation under article 6 are owed by the State in its territorial capacity, not as flag State
5. The documents required to be carried on board
  • the operative document is the SHIPBOARD OIL POLLUTION EMERGENCY PLAN, and its detail comes from MARPOL Annex I regulation 37
  • regulation 37.1 - every oil tanker of 150 GT and above, and every ship other than an oil tanker of 400 GT and above
  • the plan must be APPROVED BY THE ADMINISTRATION
  • regulation 37.2 - prepared on guidelines developed by the Organization, and written in the working language of the master and officers
  • the plan shall consist AT LEAST of four things - so the list is a floor, not a ceiling
  • 37.2.1 - the procedure for the master or other person in charge to report an oil pollution incident, as required by article 8 and Protocol I of MARPOL
  • 37.2.2 - the list of authorities or persons to be contacted
  • 37.2.3 - a detailed description of the action to be taken immediately by persons on board to reduce or control the discharge
  • 37.2.4 - the procedures and point of contact on the ship for co-ordinating shipboard action with national and local authorities
  • regulation 37.3 - where Annex II regulation 17 also applies, the plan may be COMBINED, and the combined plan is titled 'Shipboard marine pollution emergency plan'
  • regulation 37.4 - all oil tankers of 5,000 tons deadweight or more shall have prompt access to computerized shore-based damage stability and residual structural strength calculation programs
  • supporting documents that are NOT OPRC documents but are found with the plan - the Oil Record Book under Annex I regulation 17, the IOPP Certificate, and the coastal State contact list
6. How OPRC and MARPOL interlock
  • OPRC binds STATES; MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 binds the SHIP
  • OPRC article 3 says the Party shall require the plan 'as required by and in accordance with provisions adopted by the Organization'
  • those provisions are MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 - which supplies the thresholds, the approval, the language rule and the contents
  • so a single sheet of paper on the bridge satisfies an obligation created in one convention and specified in another
  • the enforcement also comes from MARPOL's machinery - the SOPEP is verified at survey and checked at port State control
  • MARPOL prevents, OPRC prepares and responds, CLC and FUND compensate - three instruments, one incident, three different questions
  • the OPRC-HNS Protocol 2000 repeats the same architecture for hazardous and noxious substances
  • UNCLOS article 199 sits above both, obliging States to develop contingency plans against pollution
Model written answer

1. What OPRC is, and the casualty that produced it

The International Convention on Oil Pollution Preparedness, Response and Co-operation, 1990 was adopted on 30 November 1990 and entered into force on 13 May 1995. It was developed in the aftermath of the Exxon Valdez grounding of 1989, which demonstrated that even a well-resourced State could be overwhelmed by a major spill, and that there was no international framework for asking anyone else for help.

It is a framework convention. It sets no discharge standard and prescribes no construction requirement. Instead it obliges its Parties to build and maintain a capability — plans, authorities, equipment, exercises — and to make that capability available to each other. Its architecture was extended to chemicals by the OPRC-HNS Protocol, 2000.

2. Purpose and objectives - and what OPRC deliberately is not

The objectives are the three nouns in the title:

  1. Preparedness — that plans, designated authorities, pre-positioned equipment, trained people and rehearsed procedures exist before the incident, not after it;
  2. Response — that a State can act promptly and effectively against an oil pollution incident, through a known chain of command and known contact points; and
  3. Co-operation — that States assist one another, and that the legal and administrative friction which delays cross-border assistance is removed in advance.

Three supporting objectives serve those: reporting, so that an incident becomes known at once; information exchange and research and development, so that response technique improves; and technical co-operation, so that developing countries can build the same capability.

It is worth as many marks to say what OPRC is not:

  • It is not a prevention convention. Preventing pollution — discharge limits, oil filtering equipment, segregated ballast, double hulls — is MARPOL’s work. OPRC begins where prevention has already failed.
  • It is not a liability or compensation convention. Who pays, and how much, is the CLC, FUND and Bunkers regime. OPRC allocates no liability and creates no fund.
  • It creates no direct duty on a shipowner. It binds States, which is why the duty that reaches the ship arrives through MARPOL.

3. The architecture - what the Convention obliges Parties to build

The substantive articles can be held as a sequence — plan, report, react, build, share:

  • Article 3 — oil pollution emergency plans. Each Party requires ships flying its flag to carry an oil pollution emergency plan in accordance with provisions adopted by the Organization, and imposes parallel requirements on offshore units, sea ports and oil handling facilities under its jurisdiction.
  • Article 4 — oil pollution reporting procedures. Masters and persons in charge report without delay any event involving a discharge or probable discharge of oil, to the nearest coastal State.
  • Article 5 — action on receiving a report. The Party assesses the incident and notifies all States whose interests may be affected, together with the Organization.
  • Article 6 — national and regional systems. The core building obligation. Each Party establishes a national system for responding promptly and effectively, including a national contingency plan, a designated competent national authority and an operational contact point; and, within its capabilities, a minimum level of pre-positioned response equipment, a programme of exercises and training, and detailed plans and communications capability.
  • Article 7 — international co-operation in response. Parties agree to co-operate and provide advisory services, technical support and equipment on request, subject to their capabilities and resources.
  • Articles 8 to 10 — research and development, technical co-operation with developing countries, and the promotion of bilateral and multilateral agreements, from which the regional arrangements grow.
  • Article 12 — the institutional role, placing information services, education and training, and technical services with IMO.

4. The responsibilities of the flag State

A Party wears two hats under OPRC and the question asks about only one. Separate the flag State’s duties from the same State’s coastal State duties — conflating them is the commonest structural error in this answer. As flag State, its responsibilities are:

  1. Require the plan. Under article 3, require ships entitled to fly its flag to have on board an oil pollution emergency plan, as required by and in accordance with provisions adopted by the Organization.
  2. Approve the plan. Through MARPOL Annex I regulation 37.1, the plan must be approved by the Administration — so this is not a document the ship simply writes for itself.
  3. Require reporting. Under article 4, require masters and persons having charge of its ships to report without delay to the nearest coastal State.
  4. Ensure the plan is usable. Under regulation 37.2, ensure it is based on the Organization’s guidelines and written in the working language of the master and officers — an emergency plan in a language the crew does not read is worthless.
  5. Survey, inspect and enforce. Verify at statutory survey that the plan exists, is approved and is current; and take enforcement action against its own ships for breach.
  6. Train and exercise. Ensure crews are drilled on the plan — discharged in practice through the ISM Code and the ship’s safety management system.
  7. Co-operate and communicate. Respond to requests for information about its ships, support the coastal State that receives a report, and maintain the contact arrangements that make that possible.
  8. Implement domestically. Give the Convention force in national law — in India, at this sitting, through the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 and the rules made under it.

What is not a flag State duty: the national contingency plan, the pre-positioned equipment stockpile and the national response organisation under article 6 are owed by the State in its territorial and coastal capacity, not because it registers ships. Saying so expressly shows the distinction has been understood rather than missed.

5. The documents required to be carried on board

The operative document is the Shipboard Oil Pollution Emergency Plan (SOPEP). OPRC article 3 requires the Party to require it; the detail — thresholds, approval, language and contents — is supplied by MARPOL Annex I regulation 37.

Regulation 37.1: every oil tanker of 150 gross tonnage and above, and every ship other than an oil tanker of 400 gross tonnage and above, shall carry on board a shipboard oil pollution emergency plan approved by the Administration. Note that the two thresholds are different, and that the tanker threshold is the lower.

Regulation 37.2: the plan shall be prepared based on guidelines developed by the Organization and written in the working language of the master and officers, and shall consist “at least” of four things — so the list is a floor, not a ceiling:

RegMandatory content
37.2.1The procedure to be followed by the master or other persons having charge of the ship to report an oil pollution incident, as required in article 8 and Protocol I of MARPOL, based on the Organization’s guidelines
37.2.2The list of authorities or persons to be contacted in the event of an oil pollution incident
37.2.3A detailed description of the action to be taken immediately by persons on board to reduce or control the discharge of oil following the incident
37.2.4The procedures and point of contact on the ship for co-ordinating shipboard action with national and local authorities in combating the pollution

Regulation 37.3 — the combination rule. Where regulation 17 of MARPOL Annex II also applies to the ship, the SOPEP may be combined with the shipboard marine pollution emergency plan for noxious liquid substances; and in that case the title of the combined plan shall be “Shipboard marine pollution emergency plan” — the SMPEP. This is why a chemical tanker carries an SMPEP and not a SOPEP, and it is a favourite oral question.

Regulation 37.4 adds a requirement that is easy to overlook: all oil tankers of 5,000 tons deadweight or more shall have prompt access to computerized, shore-based damage stability and residual structural strength calculation programs — the emergency response service the master calls after a grounding or collision.

Found alongside the plan, but properly labelled as belonging to other instruments rather than to OPRC: the Oil Record Book Part I (and Part II for tankers) under Annex I regulation 17; the IOPP Certificate and its Supplement; and the coastal State contact list which is kept current as part of the plan itself.

6. How OPRC and MARPOL interlock

The closing point is the one that shows the two instruments have been read together. OPRC binds States; MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 binds the ship. Article 3 requires the Party to require the plan “as required by and in accordance with provisions adopted by the Organization” — and those provisions are regulation 37, which supplies the thresholds, the approval requirement, the language rule and the four contents. A single document in the wheelhouse therefore discharges an obligation created in one convention and specified in another, and it is enforced through MARPOL’s machinery — verified at statutory survey, and checked by a port State control officer.

Set beside its neighbours the division of labour is clean: MARPOL prevents, OPRC prepares and responds, and CLC, FUND and Bunkers compensate. Three instruments, one incident, three entirely different questions. Above all of them UNCLOS article 199 obliges States to develop and promote contingency plans for responding to pollution incidents, and OPRC is how that obligation was given operational content.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The paper prints 16 marks in one block with no sub-division, and it hides the seam between its first two tasks behind a comma splice — “...Convention, Discuss the responsibilities...”. That is a printed anomaly, it is reproduced rather than corrected, and the practical consequence is that a candidate must supply the structure the paper does not: three tasks, and the word “Briefly” governs only the first.

So the purpose and objectives get two short sections and no more. The flag State duties and the documents are where the marks are, and each gets a substantial one. The four mandatory SOPEP contents go in a table, because that is a four-item list an examiner will scan for and either find instantly or not find at all.

The closing section on how OPRC and MARPOL interlock is not decoration. It is the single highest-value paragraph in the answer, because it demonstrates that the candidate knows why the third task is answered out of a different instrument from the one the question names.

The structural fact that most answers miss

OPRC binds States. It does not bind ships.

Article 3 does not say “every ship shall carry a plan”. It says each Party shall require ships flying its flag to have one, in accordance with provisions adopted by the Organization. Those provisions are MARPOL Annex I regulation 37, which is where the 150 GT and 400 GT thresholds, the approval by the Administration, the working language rule and the four contents all live.

This matters for two reasons. It explains why the answer to “list the documents required to be carried on board as per its provisions” is drawn from MARPOL. And it explains enforcement: a port State control officer who wants to check the SOPEP does so under MARPOL’s machinery, because OPRC gives no one power over a ship.

Three conventions, one incident, three questions

The clearest way to hold the whole subject:

InstrumentQuestion it answersWhen it operates
MARPOLHow do we stop it happening?Before — construction, equipment, discharge standards
OPRCIs anyone ready, and can they help each other?Before and during — plans, authorities, equipment, response
CLC / FUND / BunkersWho pays?After — liability and compensation

A candidate who writes a paragraph about double hulls, or about limitation of liability, has answered a different question and will be marked accordingly. Stating the boundaries expressly is itself worth marks in a “discuss the purpose and objectives” task.

Flag State or coastal State? Keep the two hats apart

This is the discriminating feature of the second task. The same State does both, but the question asks about one:

  • As flag State — require the plan, approve it, require reporting, make the plan usable in the crew’s language, survey and enforce, train and exercise, co-operate, and implement domestically.
  • As coastal State — the article 6 national contingency plan, the competent national authority and operational contact point, the pre-positioned equipment, the exercise programme, and the article 5 duty to assess a report and notify affected States and IMO.

A candidate who lists the national contingency plan as a flag State responsibility has misread the Convention. Saying explicitly “these are owed in the territorial capacity, not as flag State” converts a possible error into a demonstrated distinction.

The SOPEP details that get dropped

  • Two different thresholds. 150 GT for oil tankers; 400 GT for ships other than oil tankers. Candidates routinely give one figure for both.
  • Approved by the Administration. Not written by the master, not issued by class in its own right.
  • Working language of the master and officers. An explicit requirement of regulation 37.2, and a common oral question.
  • “At least”. The four contents are a minimum. Company plans are routinely longer and that is compliant, not excessive.
  • The combination rule. Combined with the Annex II regulation 17 plan, the document is retitled “Shipboard marine pollution emergency plan”. That is why chemical tankers carry an SMPEP.
  • Regulation 37.4. Oil tankers of 5,000 tons deadweight or more must have prompt access to computerized shore-based damage stability and residual structural strength calculation programs. Almost nobody mentions it and it is a whole limb of the regulation.
  • The Oil Record Book is not an OPRC document. If it is mentioned, label it as Annex I regulation 17.

Common mistakes

  • Describing OPRC as a pollution prevention convention. It is preparedness, response and co-operation.
  • Bringing liability, limitation or compensation into it. That is CLC, FUND and Bunkers.
  • Saying OPRC directly requires ships to carry a SOPEP. It requires Parties to require it; MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 imposes it on the ship.
  • Giving one tonnage threshold for both tankers and other ships.
  • Listing the national contingency plan and equipment stockpile as flag State duties.
  • Omitting the approval requirement, or the working-language requirement.
  • Treating the four contents as exhaustive when regulation 37.2 says at least.
  • Calling a combined plan a SOPEP after combination — it is retitled SMPEP.
  • Listing the Oil Record Book, IOPP Certificate and Garbage Management Plan as OPRC documents without labelling their real source.
  • Missing regulation 37.4 entirely.
  • Writing at length on the purpose when the paper says “Briefly”, and then running short on the two substantial tasks.

Examiner traps

  • “When was OPRC adopted and when did it enter into force?” Adopted 30 November 1990; in force 13 May 1995.
  • “Which casualty led to it?” The Exxon Valdez grounding of 1989.
  • “Does OPRC prevent pollution?” No — it assumes the spill has happened. Prevention is MARPOL.
  • “Your ship is 380 GT and not a tanker. Does it need a SOPEP?” No — regulation 37.1 catches other ships at 400 GT and above. A 380 GT oil tanker would, because the tanker threshold is 150 GT.
  • “Who approves the SOPEP?” The Administration.
  • “What language must it be in?” The working language of the master and officers.
  • “Name the four things the plan must contain.” Reporting procedure; contact list; immediate action description; and shipboard co-ordination procedures and point of contact.
  • “Your chemical tanker carries a combined plan. What is it called?” A Shipboard marine pollution emergency plan — regulation 37.3 fixes the title.
  • “What extra does a 30,000 dwt tanker need under regulation 37?” Prompt access to computerized shore-based damage stability and residual structural strength calculation programs — the 5,000 dwt threshold at 37.4.
  • “Whom does the master report to after a spill?” Under OPRC article 4, without delay to the nearest coastal State; the procedure itself is in the SOPEP under regulation 37.2.1, referring to MARPOL article 8 and Protocol I.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What do the letters OPRC stand for? Oil Pollution Preparedness, Response and Co-operation.
  • Name three things article 6 requires a national system to have. A national contingency plan; a competent national authority and operational contact point; a minimum level of pre-positioned response equipment, with exercises and training.
  • Which article requires the emergency plan? Article 3.
  • Which article requires reporting, and to whom? Article 4 - without delay, to the nearest coastal State.
  • Which Protocol extends OPRC to chemicals? The OPRC-HNS Protocol, 2000.
  • Which MARPOL regulation actually requires the SOPEP? Annex I regulation 37.
  • What is India's national system called in outline? A national oil spill disaster contingency plan with a designated response authority - the shape article 6 requires.
  • Which UNCLOS article obliges contingency planning? Article 199.

Memory framework

Start from the title, because the title is the objectives: PREPAREDNESS, RESPONSE, CO-OPERATION. Then add the negative in one line: not prevention, not compensation.

For the architecture, five verbs in article order: PLAN (3), REPORT (4), REACT (5), BUILD (6), SHARE (7 to 10).

For the flag State, four verbs: REQUIRE, APPROVE, VERIFY, ENFORCE — and one caution: the contingency plan is the coastal hat, not the flag hat.

For the SOPEP, two numbers and four contents: 150 and 400; and REPORT, CONTACT, ACT, CO-ORDINATE — which is also the order in which the plan is used in a real incident. Then one extra number: 5,000 dwt for shore-based damage stability access.

Regulation and source map

  • OPRC 1990 — adopted 30 November 1990, in force 13 May 1995. Unamended.
  • Article 3 — oil pollution emergency plans for ships, offshore units, sea ports and oil handling facilities. Article 4 — reporting without delay to the nearest coastal State. Article 5 — assess and notify affected States and the Organization.
  • Article 6 — national and regional systems: national contingency plan, competent national authority and operational contact point, minimum pre-positioned equipment, exercises and training. Article 7 — co-operation on request. Articles 8 to 10 — R&D, technical co-operation, bilateral and multilateral agreements. Article 12 — IMO's institutional role.
  • OPRC-HNS Protocol, 2000 — the same architecture for hazardous and noxious substances.
  • MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 — the shipboard duty. 37.1 thresholds and approval; 37.2 guidelines, working language and the four contents; 37.3 combination with the Annex II plan and the SMPEP title; 37.4 shore-based damage stability access for tankers of 5,000 dwt and above.
  • MARPOL article 8 and Protocol I — the reporting obligation regulation 37.2.1 cross-refers to. Annex I regulation 17 — the Oil Record Book. Annex II regulation 17 — the NLS emergency plan.
  • UNCLOS article 199 — contingency plans against pollution; article 194 — the general duty.
  • India at this sitting: the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 and rules made under it.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

The evidence for this answer splits unusually cleanly, and the split is declared rather than hidden.

The third task is primary. MARPOL Annex I as revised is held in the True Source corpus with a full extractable text layer — resolution MEPC.117(52), adopted 15 October 2004, in force 1 January 2007 — and regulation 37 was read directly from it in full. The thresholds, the approval requirement, the language rule, the four contents, the combination rule and the 5,000 dwt provision are all taken from that text.

The OPRC Convention itself is not held. That is not an oversight of this session: it is an acquisition gap already recorded in the corpus’s own question-mapping records and in its completion report, where OPRC is named as part of a legal and casualty package that has not been built. The Convention’s dates and its article-by-article content are therefore carried at authoritative-secondary level. No article of OPRC is quoted verbatim anywhere in this answer, and no article is attributed a sub-paragraph, because that precision cannot be sourced from a held text.

No Party count is given, and no response equipment specification, stockpile quantity or tier-response threshold is quoted. Tiered response classification is industry practice rather than a Convention requirement.

The Indian national system is described in outline only — the shape an article 6 system takes, and the statute in force at the sitting — because no Indian contingency-plan instrument and no DG Shipping circular is held by the corpus.

Currency. OPRC is unamended and regulation 37 is unamended at this sitting. The one live temporal edge is MEPC.384(81), which amends the MARPOL Protocol I that regulation 37.2.1 cross-refers to: it is adopted but not in force at April 2025, entering into force 1 January 2026, so the pre-amendment Protocol I position governs and nothing about container loss reporting belongs here. MEPC 83 sat inside this sitting month, on 11 April 2025, but none of its outputs touches this subject, so this question’s correctness does not depend on the examination day.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________What OPRC is, and the casualty that produced it
  2. __________Purpose and objectives - and what OPRC deliberately is not
  3. __________The architecture - what the Convention obliges Parties to build
  4. __________The responsibilities of the flag State
  5. __________The documents required to be carried on board
  6. __________How OPRC and MARPOL interlock
Flashcards 11

The International Convention on Oil Pollution Preparedness, Response and Co-operation, 1990. Adopted 30 November 1990; entered into force 13 May 1995, developed after the Exxon Valdez grounding of 1989. Its three objectives are the three nouns of its title: preparedness - plans, authorities, equipment and training in place beforehand; response - the ability to act promptly and effectively; and co-operation - mutual assistance between States with the friction removed in advance.

The title carries the objectives, which makes this the cheapest structured mark on the question.

It is not a prevention convention - discharge standards, equipment and construction are MARPOL's work, and OPRC begins where prevention has failed. It is not a liability or compensation convention - that is CLC, FUND and Bunkers. And it creates no direct duty on a shipowner, because it binds States.

Stating the boundaries is worth as many marks as stating the content, and it prevents a whole paragraph of wrong material.

Not directly. OPRC article 3 requires each Party to REQUIRE ships flying its flag to have an oil pollution emergency plan 'as required by and in accordance with provisions adopted by the Organization'. Those provisions are MARPOL Annex I regulation 37, which supplies the thresholds, the approval requirement, the language rule and the contents. OPRC binds States; regulation 37 binds the ship.

It is the structural fact the whole question turns on, and it explains why the document limb is answered out of MARPOL.

MARPOL Annex I regulation 37.1: every oil tanker of 150 gross tonnage and above, and every ship other than an oil tanker of 400 gross tonnage and above. The plan must be approved by the Administration. The two thresholds are different and the tanker threshold is the lower.

Candidates routinely give a single figure for both, which loses the mark outright.

Under regulation 37.2, and the plan shall consist AT LEAST of: the procedure for the master or other person in charge to report an oil pollution incident, as required by MARPOL article 8 and Protocol I; the list of authorities or persons to be contacted; a detailed description of the action to be taken immediately by persons on board to reduce or control the discharge; and the procedures and point of contact on the ship for co-ordinating shipboard action with national and local authorities.

Report, contact, act, co-ordinate - and that is also the order the plan is used in during a real incident.

Regulation 37.2: prepared based on guidelines developed by the Organization, and written in the working language of the master and officers. An emergency plan the crew cannot read is worthless, which is why the language requirement is express.

A standard oral question, and the language rule is frequently omitted.

A Shipboard marine pollution emergency plan (SMPEP). Regulation 37.3 permits the SOPEP to be combined with the shipboard marine pollution emergency plan for noxious liquid substances required by MARPOL Annex II regulation 17, and provides that the title of the combined plan shall be 'Shipboard marine pollution emergency plan'.

The retitling is expressly in the regulation and explains why chemical tankers do not carry a document called a SOPEP.

All oil tankers of 5,000 tons deadweight or more shall have prompt access to computerized, shore-based damage stability and residual structural strength calculation programs - the emergency response service the master calls after a grounding or collision.

It is a whole paragraph of the regulation that almost every candidate omits.

Flag State: require the plan (art 3), require reporting without delay (art 4), approve the plan and enforce the working-language and content requirements through MARPOL reg 37, survey and enforce, train and exercise through the ISM system, co-operate, and implement domestically. Coastal State: the article 6 national system - national contingency plan, competent national authority and operational contact point, pre-positioned equipment, exercises and training - and the article 5 duty to assess a report and notify affected States and IMO.

Listing the national contingency plan as a flag State duty is the commonest structural error in this question.

A national system for responding promptly and effectively to oil pollution incidents, including as a minimum a national contingency plan, a designated competent national authority and an operational contact point; and, within its capabilities, a minimum level of pre-positioned oil spill response equipment, a programme of exercises and training, and detailed plans and communication capabilities for responding.

Article 6 is the core building obligation and is where the Convention becomes a national institution rather than a treaty.

MARPOL asks how we stop it happening - construction, equipment and discharge standards, before the event. OPRC asks whether anyone is ready and whether they can help each other - plans, authorities, equipment and response, before and during. CLC, FUND and Bunkers ask who pays - liability and compensation, afterwards. Above all of them UNCLOS article 199 obliges States to develop contingency plans against pollution.

Three instruments, one incident, three different questions - it keeps prevention and compensation material out of an OPRC answer.

Quick revision
15-second recall
OPRC 1990 — adopted 30 November 1990, in force 13 May 1995, after the Exxon Valdez. Objectives are its three title words: preparedness, response, co-operation. It is not prevention (MARPOL) and not liability (CLC/FUND/Bunkers). Architecture: art 3 emergency plans (ships, offshore units, ports, oil handling facilities); art 4 report without delay to the nearest coastal State; art 5 assess and notify affected States and IMO; art 6 national system — contingency plan, competent national authority and operational contact point, pre-positioned equipment, exercises and training; art 7 co-operate on request; arts 8–10 R&D, technical co-operation, bilateral agreements; art 12 IMO. Flag State: require the plan, approve it, require reporting, ensure the working language, survey and enforce, train, co-operate, implement domestically — the national contingency plan is the coastal hat, not the flag hat. Documents: the SOPEP, under MARPOL Annex I reg 37 — oil tankers 150 GT and above, other ships 400 GT and above, approved by the Administration, on IMO guidelines, in the working language of master and officers, containing at least the reporting procedure, contact list, immediate actions and shipboard co-ordination point. Combined with the Annex II plan it is retitled SMPEP (37.3); tankers of 5,000 dwt and above need prompt access to shore-based damage stability programs (37.4). OPRC binds States; regulation 37 binds the ship.

Critical numbers
  • OPRC adopted 30 November 1990; entered into force 13 May 1995
  • SOPEP threshold: oil tankers of 150 gross tonnage and above
  • SOPEP threshold: ships other than oil tankers of 400 gross tonnage and above
  • MARPOL Annex I regulation 37.2 lists FOUR mandatory contents, and says 'at least'
  • Regulation 37.4 - oil tankers of 5,000 tons deadweight or more need prompt access to shore-based damage stability and residual structural strength programs
  • OPRC-HNS Protocol adopted 2000
  • The relevant MARPOL cross-reference for reporting is article 8 and Protocol I
Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 is the provision that actually reaches the ship. Regulation 37.1 requires every oil tanker of 150 gross tonnage and above and every other ship of 400 gross tonnage and above to carry a shipboard oil pollution emergency plan approved by the Administration; 37.2 requires it to be based on the Organization's guidelines, written in the working language of the master and officers, and to consist at least of the reporting procedure, the contact list, the description of immediate action and the shipboard co-ordination procedures and point of contact.
Major trap
Believing that OPRC itself requires the ship to carry a SOPEP. OPRC binds States, not ships: article 3 obliges each Party to require ships flying its flag to have a plan “in accordance with provisions adopted by the Organization”, and those provisions are MARPOL Annex I regulation 37, which supplies the two different thresholds, the approval requirement, the working-language rule and the four contents. The paired trap is answering the question as though OPRC were about preventing pollution or about who pays for it — prevention is MARPOL and compensation is the CLC, FUND and Bunkers regime.

Also on the platform: QP2504 Q2 - port State control, the inspection regime under which a SOPEP's presence, approval and currency is actually verified on board

The answer describes the SHAPE an article 6 national system takes and names the domestic statute in force at the sitting, without quoting a provision it cannot source. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
Understand this first

Admiralty is different from ordinary litigation in one respect that explains everything else: you can sue the ship. In an ordinary claim you sue a person and hope they have assets; in admiralty you proceed in rem against the vessel itself, arrest it, and hold a multi-million-dollar asset as security until you are paid or secured. That is enormous leverage against a defendant who may be a one-ship company registered somewhere you will never reach. The 2017 Act exists because India was exercising this power under colonial statutes of 1840, 1861, 1890 and 1891 and the Letters Patent of 1865 — law that predated the ships it was being applied to. The Act repeals all of that and puts the jurisdiction on a modern statutory footing: it says which court (the High Courts), how far (territorial waters), for what (twenty-three heads of maritime claim in section 4), with what teeth (arrest in rem, and a maritime lien that follows the ship through a change of flag), and in what order the money is shared (section 10). Limb (b) then asks about the one place where a court admits it cannot do this alone: an assessor — a maritime professional the Central Government has put on a list — sits with the judge and explains what the technical evidence actually means.

1. What admiralty law is, and what the 2017 Act was enacted to cure

  • so the Act modernises, consolidates and Indianises a jurisdiction that had been running on nineteenth-century English statutes

  • first proviso excludes an inland vessel under the Inland Vessels Act, 1917, and an unlaunched vessel under construction unless notified

  • 'vessel' is defined broadly at section 2(1)(l) - including barges, lighters, hovercraft, offshore industry mobile units and sunk, stranded or abandoned vessels
  • but a vessel broken up so that it cannot be put into use for navigation, as certified by a surveyor, is not a vessel
  • SECTION 3 - jurisdiction over all maritime claims vests in the RESPECTIVE HIGH COURTS
  • exercisable over the waters UP TO AND INCLUDING the territorial waters of their respective jurisdictions
  • proviso - the Central Government may by notification EXTEND that limit to the limit in section 2 of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976
  • section 2(2) - undefined words take their meaning from the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958
  • appeals lie under section 14 from a single Judge to a DIVISION BENCH of the same High Court; section 15 lets the Supreme Court transfer proceedings between High Courts
