  • PROPRIETARY AND FINANCIAL - possession and ownership, co-owner disputes over employment or earnings, mortgage or charge, sale contract disputes, insurance premium including mutual insurance calls, commission and brokerage
  • OPERATIONAL DAMAGE - loss or damage caused by the operation of a vessel; loss of life or personal injury in direct connection with it; loss or damage to goods
  • CONTRACTUAL AND SERVICES - carriage of goods or passengers; use or hire; salvage including special compensation; towage; pilotage; supplies, bunkers, equipment and services; construction, repair, conversion and equipping; disbursements; port, canal, dock and light dues
  • CREW - wages, repatriation costs and social insurance contributions, expressly notwithstanding sections 150 and 151 of the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958
  • AVERAGE AND ENVIRONMENT - particular and general average, taking the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 meanings; environmental damage and the cost of preventive and restoration measures; wreck raising, removal and rendering harmless
  • AND MARITIME LIEN ITSELF at 4(1)(w)
  • SECTION 5 - ARREST IN REM, the enforcement mechanism, to provide security against a maritime claim
  • available where the owner or demise charterer liable when the claim arose still holds that position at arrest, or the claim is on a mortgage, or relates to ownership or possession, or is secured by a maritime lien
  • section 5(2) - a SISTER SHIP may be arrested in lieu, but the proviso bars that for an ownership or possession claim under 4(1)(a)
  • SECTION 9 - FIVE MARITIME LIENS in order: crew wages; loss of life or personal injury; salvage; port, canal, waterway and pilotage dues; and tort damage other than to cargo and containers carried on board
  • section 9(2) - the lien CONTINUES notwithstanding change of ownership, registration or flag, and is extinguished after ONE YEAR - TWO YEARS for wages - unless arrest has led to a forced sale
  • section 9(4) - no lien for oil or HNS damage compensable under other law, or for radioactive properties
  • SECTION 10 - priority in three tiers: maritime liens, then registered mortgages and charges, then all other claims; equal claims rank equally and salvage claims rank in INVERSE order of time
  • section 8 - on judicial sale the vessel vests in the purchaser FREE from all encumbrances and liens; section 11 protects against wrongful arrest by requiring an undertaking or security
4. Who assessors are, and how they are appointed
  • SECTION 13 is the whole statutory basis and it is short - two sub-sections
  • section 13(1) - notwithstanding anything in any other law, THE CENTRAL GOVERNMENT SHALL APPOINT BY NOTIFICATION A LIST OF ASSESSORS
  • note three things in that phrase - the duty is on the Central Government, the instrument is a notification, and it is a LIST
  • so assessors are not chosen ad hoc by the parties or by the judge; the court draws from a panel appointed in advance
  • the list is appointed with such qualifications and experience IN ADMIRALTY AND MARITIME MATTERS as may be prescribed
  • the notification also fixes the nature of duties, the fees payable, and other ancillary or incidental matters
  • section 16(2)(a) gives the Central Government power to make rules on exactly those subjects
  • SECTION 13(2) - the appointment of assessors SHALL NOT BE CONSTRUED AS A BAR to the examination of expert witnesses by any of the parties
  • that sub-section is the key to the whole limb - it separates the assessor from the expert witness
  • an assessor advises the COURT; an expert witness is called by a PARTY and gives evidence that can be cross-examined
  • the assessor is not a witness, is not a judge, and does not decide - the judgment remains the court's
5. Qualifications, role, and how assessors assist the court
  • the Act does not itself list qualifications - it requires them to be PRESCRIBED, and the rules are the place they live
  • in substance the qualification is professional maritime competence plus experience: master mariners and chief engineers, naval architects, marine surveyors, salvage and casualty specialists, marine insurance and admiralty practitioners
  • with sea-going or technical experience of the subject matter, and the independence to advise a court rather than a client
  • THE ROLE - to supply the technical understanding a judge does not have, so that the law can be applied to facts the court can actually follow
  • HOW THEY ASSIST, concretely - explain the technical vocabulary and the systems in issue, so that survey reports, class records and engine data become intelligible
  • explain what good practice required in the circumstances, which is what negligence and seaworthiness questions turn on
  • help the court test conflicting expert evidence - identify where two experts actually differ, and which assumptions drive the difference
  • advise on the reasonableness of repair estimates, salvage effort, off-hire periods and the quantum of technical damage
  • assist on collision and casualty questions - manoeuvring, machinery failure, watchkeeping practice and the sequence of events
  • advise on maintenance, class survey and machinery condition where seaworthiness or a wages or repair claim is disputed

  • the court remains the tribunal of fact and law, and section 13(2) preserves the parties' right to call their own experts
  • the practical effect - faster, better-informed adjudication of technical maritime disputes, and less risk of a court being misled by confident but wrong expert evidence
  • for a Marine Engineer Officer this is a realistic later career - the assessor role is precisely what a Class I engineer's experience qualifies them to do
Model written answer

(a) Scope and application of Admiralty Law in India, and the key provisions of the 2017 Act — 8 marks

1. What admiralty law is, and what the 2017 Act was enacted to cure

Admiralty law is the body of law governing maritime claims and, distinctively, the right to proceed in rem — against the vessel itself rather than only against a person. That is what gives it its force: a ship is a valuable and mobile asset, and arresting it produces real security against a defendant who may be a single-ship company incorporated where no judgment could be enforced.

Until 2017 India exercised that jurisdiction under colonial legislation. Section 17(1) of the Act repeals, in their application to India, the Admiralty Court Act, 1840, the Admiralty Court Act, 1861, the Colonial Courts of Admiralty Act, 1890 and the Colonial Courts of Admiralty (India) Act, 1891, together with the Letters Patent, 1865 so far as it applied to the admiralty jurisdiction of the Bombay, Calcutta and Madras High Courts.

The Act’s own long title states its purpose: “to consolidate the laws relating to admiralty jurisdiction, legal proceedings in connection with vessels, their arrest, detention, sale and other matters connected therewith or incidental thereto”. It is Act No. 22 of 2017, dated 9 August 2017, and by section 12 the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908 applies to proceedings under it so far as not inconsistent with the Act or its rules.

2. Scope, application and the jurisdiction of the High Courts

Section 1(2) sets the reach, and it is deliberately wide: the Act applies to every vessel, irrespective of the place of residence or domicile of the owner. A foreign-owned, foreign-flagged ship lying in an Indian port is squarely within it. Two provisos carve out:

  • an inland vessel as defined in the Inland Vessels Act, 1917, and a vessel under construction that has not been launched, unless notified by the Central Government; and
  • warships, naval auxiliaries and other vessels owned or operated by the Central or a State Government used for non-commercial purposes, together with notified foreign non-commercial vessels.

“Vessel” is defined broadly at section 2(1)(l) — including barges, lighters, hovercraft, offshore industry mobile units and vessels that have sunk or are stranded or abandoned — but the Explanation removes a vessel broken up to such an extent that it cannot be put into use for navigation, as certified by a surveyor.

Section 3 is the jurisdiction provision. Subject to sections 4 and 5, jurisdiction in respect of all maritime claims vests in the respective High Courts and is exercisable over the waters up to and including the territorial waters of their respective jurisdictions. The proviso allows the Central Government, by notification, to extend that limit up to the limit defined in section 2 of the Territorial Waters, Continental Shelf, Exclusive Economic Zone and Other Maritime Zones Act, 1976. So the jurisdiction is High Court jurisdiction, and it is territorial with a statutory power of extension.

Two procedural provisions complete the picture: by section 14 an appeal lies from any judgment, decree or final or interim order of a single Judge of the High Court to a Division Bench of that Court; and by section 15 the Supreme Court may transfer an admiralty proceeding from one High Court to another. Section 2(2) imports the meanings of the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 for words not defined in this Act.

3. Maritime claims, arrest in rem, liens and priority

Section 4(1) recognises twenty-three heads of maritime claim, (a) to (w). They are best grouped:

GroupHeadsExamples
Proprietary and financial(a) (b) (c) (r) (s) (t)Possession and ownership; co-owner disputes over employment or earnings; mortgage or charge; sale contract disputes; insurance premium including mutual insurance calls; commission, brokerage and agency fees
Operational damage(d) (e) (f)Loss or damage caused by the operation of a vessel; loss of life or personal injury, on land or water, in direct connection with that operation; loss of or damage to goods
Contract and services(g) (h) (i) (j) (k) (l) (m) (n) (p)Carriage of goods or passengers; use or hire; salvage including special compensation; towage; pilotage; supplies, provisions, bunker fuel, equipment and services to the vessel; construction, repair, conversion and equipping; port, harbour, canal, dock and light dues; disbursements
Crew(o)Wages and sums due as wages, cost of repatriation and social insurance contributions — expressly notwithstanding sections 150 and 151 of the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958
Average and environment(q) (u) (v) (w)Particular and general average (Marine Insurance Act, 1963 meanings); environmental damage and the cost of prevention and restoration; wreck raising, removal and rendering harmless; and maritime lien itself

Section 5 supplies the teeth. The High Court may order arrest of a vessel within its jurisdiction to provide security against a maritime claim where the person or demise charterer liable when the claim arose still holds that position when the arrest is effected; or the claim rests on a mortgage or charge; or relates to ownership or possession; or is secured by a maritime lien under section 9. Under section 5(2) a sister ship may be arrested in lieu — but the proviso bars that for an ownership or possession claim under section 4(1)(a).

Section 9 identifies five maritime liens, and gives them their order among themselves: (a) crew wages and related sums including repatriation and social insurance; (b) loss of life or personal injury in direct connection with the operation of the vessel; (c) salvage reward including special compensation; (d) port, canal, waterway and pilotage and other statutory dues; and (e) tort claims from loss or damage caused by the operation of the vessel, other than to cargo and containers carried on board. A maritime lien continues notwithstanding any change of ownership, registration or flag — which is what makes it formidable — and is extinguished after one year, or two years for wages, unless arrest or seizure has led to a forced sale. Section 9(4) excludes liens for oil or hazardous and noxious substance damage compensable under other law, and for radioactive properties.

Section 10 then ranks everything in three tiers: first, claims where there is a maritime lien; second, registered mortgages and charges of the same nature; third, all other claims. Claims within a tier rank equally, and salvage claims rank in inverse order of time. Finally, section 8 provides that on a judicial sale the vessel vests in the purchaser free from all encumbrances, liens, attachments, registered mortgages and charges; and section 11 protects the shipowner by empowering the court to require the arresting claimant to give an unconditional undertaking or security against a wrongful or unjustified arrest or excessive security demanded.

(b) Assessors in Admiralty proceedings — qualifications, role, and technical assistance to the court — 8 marks

4. Who assessors are, and how they are appointed

The whole statutory basis is section 13, and it is only two sub-sections long.

Section 13(1): “Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being in force, the Central Government shall appoint by notification, a list of assessors with such qualifications and experience in admiralty and maritime matters, the nature of duties to be performed by them, the fees to be paid to them and other ancillary or incidental matters for the purposes of this Act, in the manner as may be prescribed.”

Three features of that sentence carry the marks:

  1. the duty is on the Central Government, not on the court or the parties;
  2. the instrument is a notification; and
  3. what is appointed is a list — a standing panel, settled in advance, from which the court draws. Assessors are not selected ad hoc by the litigants.

The same notification fixes their qualifications and experience in admiralty and maritime matters, the nature of their duties, their fees, and ancillary matters — all “as may be prescribed”, and section 16(2)(a) gives the Central Government the corresponding rule-making power.

Section 13(2) is short and decisive: “The appointment of assessors shall not be construed as a bar to the examination of expert witnesses by any of the parties in any admiralty proceeding.” That single sub-section fixes the assessor’s constitutional position:

AssessorExpert witness
Appointed byThe Central Government, to a list, by notificationA party, for that case
AdvisesThe courtThe party who calls them, by evidence to the court
Gives evidence?NoYes — and is cross-examined
Decides?No — the judgment remains the court’sNo

5. Qualifications, role, and how assessors assist the court

Qualifications. The Act itself does not enumerate them; it requires them to be prescribed, and they live in the rules. In substance what is required is professional maritime competence backed by experience — the people appointed are master mariners and chief engineers, naval architects, marine surveyors, salvage and casualty specialists, and marine insurance and admiralty practitioners, with practical experience of the subject matter in issue and the independence to advise a court rather than a client. For a Marine Engineer Officer, Class I, this is a realistic later professional role: the qualification the Act contemplates is precisely a senior engineer’s experience of machinery, survey and shipboard practice.

Role. An assessor exists because a judge is an expert in law and not in ships. Their function is to supply the technical understanding that allows the court to apply the law to facts it can actually follow — and to do so from a position of neutrality, which no party’s expert occupies.

How they assist in interpreting technical maritime matters — concretely:

  1. Translating the evidence. Survey reports, class records, engine performance data, planned maintenance printouts, VDR data and casualty reports are unintelligible to a lay reader. The assessor makes them intelligible and explains the vocabulary.
  2. Establishing what good practice required. Questions of negligence, seaworthiness and due diligence turn on what a competent owner, master or engineer should have done in the circumstances. That is a technical standard, and the assessor explains it.
  3. Testing competing expert evidence. Where two party-appointed experts disagree, the assessor helps the court identify where they actually differ and which assumptions drive the difference — the single most valuable thing an assessor does, and the best protection against a court being persuaded by confident but wrong evidence.
  4. Assessing reasonableness and quantum. Whether a repair specification and its cost were reasonable, whether a salvage effort was proportionate, whether an off-hire period was justified, whether machinery damage was fair wear or a casualty.
  5. Casualty reconstruction. In collision, grounding, fire and machinery-failure cases — manoeuvring characteristics, stopping distances, watchkeeping practice, the sequence of alarms and the plausibility of a claimed failure mode.
  6. Condition and maintenance questions. Where seaworthiness, a repair claim or a crew wages claim depends on the actual state of the ship, its class status and its maintenance history.

The limits matter as much as the role. The assessor does not give evidence and is not cross-examined; the assessor does not decide — the court remains the tribunal of both fact and law; and by section 13(2) the parties retain their full right to call their own experts. The result the Act is aiming at is faster and better-informed adjudication of technical maritime disputes by courts that would otherwise be wholly dependent on the parties for their understanding of the ship.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The printed split is 8 + 8, but the two limbs are not equally heavy in reading. Limb (a) names three separate statutory objects — key provisions, High Court jurisdiction, and the types of maritime claim recognised — and each has to be answered. Limb (b) has three demands too, but the Act gives it only two short sub-sections, so the marks there come from quality of explanation rather than from quantity of citation.

The twenty-three heads of section 4 go in a grouped table. Reciting (a) to (w) in prose is unreadable and wastes the page; five groups with the letters beside them shows the same knowledge in a quarter of the space and proves the candidate has read the section rather than memorised a list.

The assessor/expert-witness distinction goes in a second small table because it is a four-way comparison and because it is the highest-value single idea in limb (b).

What the 2017 Act actually changed

A candidate who says only “the Act governs admiralty jurisdiction in India” has not answered “scope and application”. The examinable point is what it replaced.

Until 2017 Indian admiralty jurisdiction rested on four colonial statutes and a set of Letters Patent — the Admiralty Court Acts of 1840 and 1861, the Colonial Courts of Admiralty Act 1890, the Colonial Courts of Admiralty (India) Act 1891, and the Letters Patent, 1865 for Bombay, Calcutta and Madras. Section 17(1) repeals all of them. The consequences are worth stating: the jurisdiction is now statutory and modern; it is exercisable by the High Courts generally rather than resting on the historic charters of three presidency towns; and the list of maritime claims is now enacted rather than inferred from Victorian case law.

Jurisdiction: which court, how far

Two facts, and both are commonly got wrong.

Which court: the High Courts — section 3 vests it in “the respective High Courts”. Not the district courts, not a specialist tribunal, and not the Supreme Court at first instance (though section 15 lets the Supreme Court transfer between High Courts).

How far: up to and including the territorial waters of the respective jurisdiction — not the EEZ as of right. The proviso to section 3 allows the Central Government by notification to extend the limit to the limit in section 2 of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976. Saying “territorial waters, extendable by notification” is precise; saying “Indian waters” is not.

Maritime claim, maritime lien, mortgage - three different things

This is the distinction that separates a good answer from a competent one, and section 4(1)(w) is the trap: “maritime lien” is itself listed as one of the twenty-three heads of maritime claim. So all maritime liens are maritime claims; almost no maritime claims are maritime liens.

How manyWhereStrength
Maritime claimTwenty-three headsSection 4(1)(a) to (w)Founds jurisdiction; may support arrest under section 5
Maritime lienFive onlySection 9(1)(a) to (e)Follows the ship through change of owner, registration or flag; first in priority; extinguished after one year (two for wages)
Registered mortgage—Section 10(1)(b)Second in priority — behind every maritime lien

Two consequences follow that examiners like. A financier’s registered mortgage ranks behind the crew’s unpaid wages, which is a deliberate policy choice. And under section 8 a judicial sale wipes the slate clean — the purchaser takes free of all encumbrances and liens — which is why a court-ordered sale, and not a private one, is what makes an arrested ship saleable at all.

Arrest is powerful, and it is not free

Candidates describe arrest as though it were costless leverage. Section 11 says otherwise: the High Court may, as a condition of arrest or of maintaining one, require the claimant to give an unconditional undertaking to pay damages or provide security for loss suffered by the defendant where the arrest was wrongful or unjustified or where excessive security was demanded. That is a real risk to an over-eager claimant.

The other limit is the sister-ship proviso. Section 5(2) allows another vessel to be arrested in lieu — but not where the claim is an ownership or possession claim under section 4(1)(a). The logic is obvious once seen: you cannot claim to own this ship and arrest a different one instead.

And section 11(3) has a practical provision worth knowing: if the owner or demise charterer abandons the vessel after arrest, the court shall auction it within forty-five days, extendable by thirty days for reasons recorded in writing.

Limb (b): the assessor is not an expert witness

Everything in limb (b) hangs off section 13(2). The appointment of assessors is not a bar to the examination of expert witnesses by any of the parties — so the two coexist, and they are different creatures.

An expert witness is retained by a party, gives evidence, and is cross-examined. An assessor is appointed by the Central Government to a list, advises the court, gives no evidence and is not cross-examined, and does not decide. The assessor is the court’s technical conscience; the expert is an advocate’s technical case.

Note also the mechanism: a list, appointed in advance by notification. That is a deliberate design choice — it means neutrality and availability are settled before any particular dispute arises, rather than being fought over inside it.

Common mistakes

  • Saying admiralty jurisdiction lies in any civil court. Section 3 vests it in the High Courts.
  • Saying the jurisdiction extends to the EEZ as of right. It runs to territorial waters, extendable only by Central Government notification.
  • Confusing maritime claim with maritime lien. Twenty-three claims, five liens — and maritime lien is itself head (w).
  • Putting the registered mortgage first in priority. Section 10 puts maritime liens first, mortgages second.
  • Saying a maritime lien survives a judicial sale. Section 8 vests the vessel free of all liens and encumbrances.
  • Saying a maritime lien is lost on a change of flag or owner. Section 9(2) says it continues.
  • Omitting the limitation periods — one year, two years for wages.
  • Saying any sister ship can always be arrested. Not for an ownership or possession claim — the proviso to section 5(2).
  • Treating arrest as risk-free. Section 11 exposes the claimant to an undertaking or security for wrongful arrest.
  • Calling assessors expert witnesses, or saying they give evidence, or saying they decide the case. Section 13(2) and the nature of the office say otherwise.
  • Saying the court or the parties appoint assessors. The Central Government appoints a list by notification.
  • Importing the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025. It commenced 15 March 2026, almost a year after this sitting. The 1958 Act governs, and it is the Act to which sections 2(2) and 4(1)(o) refer.

Examiner traps

  • “Give the full name and number of the Act.” The Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 — Act No. 22 of 2017, dated 9 August 2017.
  • “How many maritime claims does section 4 recognise?” Twenty-three, (a) to (w).
  • “How many maritime liens are there?” Five — section 9(1)(a) to (e), in that order of priority.
  • “Crew wages or the bank’s mortgage — who is paid first?” Crew wages. Maritime liens rank ahead of registered mortgages under section 10, and wages head the lien list.
  • “The ship is sold by the owner to a new owner under a new flag. Is your lien gone?” No — section 9(2), it continues notwithstanding change of ownership, registration or flag. But it dies after one year unless arrest has led to a forced sale.
  • “The court sells the ship. Is your lien gone?” Yes — section 8, the purchaser takes free of all liens and encumbrances.
  • “Can you arrest a warship for unpaid bunkers?” No — the second proviso to section 1(2) excludes warships, naval auxiliaries and non-commercial Government vessels.
  • “Your claim is that you own the ship. Can you arrest her sister instead?” No — the proviso to section 5(2) bars sister-ship arrest for a section 4(1)(a) claim.
  • “Who appoints assessors?” The Central Government, by notification, as a list — section 13(1).
  • “Can the parties still call their own experts?” Yes — section 13(2) says the appointment of assessors is no bar to it.
  • “Does an assessor decide the case?” No. They advise the court; the court decides.
  • “Where does an appeal go?” Section 14 — from a single Judge to a Division Bench of the same High Court.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which court exercises admiralty jurisdiction in India, and how far out? The High Courts, over waters up to and including territorial waters, extendable by Central Government notification.
  • What did the 2017 Act repeal? The Admiralty Court Acts 1840 and 1861, the Colonial Courts of Admiralty Act 1890, the Colonial Courts of Admiralty (India) Act 1891, and the Letters Patent 1865 in its admiralty application.
  • Name five maritime claims. Any five of section 4(1) - e.g. bunkers supplied, crew wages, salvage, towage, environmental damage.
  • Name the five maritime liens in order. Wages; loss of life or personal injury; salvage; port, canal and pilotage dues; tort damage other than to cargo carried.
  • How long does a maritime lien last? One year, or two years for wages, unless arrest has led to a forced sale.
  • State the section 10 priority. Maritime liens; then registered mortgages and charges; then all other claims.
  • Which section covers assessors, and what does it require? Section 13 - the Central Government appoints a list by notification, with prescribed qualifications, duties and fees.
  • Are assessors witnesses? No. They advise the court and are not cross-examined; section 13(2) preserves the parties' right to call experts.

Memory framework

For limb (a), hold the Act as five questions in order, which is also the order of its sections: WHICH COURT (3) → FOR WHAT (4) → WITH WHAT TEETH (5) → WHOSE CLAIM FOLLOWS THE SHIP (9) → WHO IS PAID FIRST (10).

For the five maritime liens, work from the person outwards: CREW → CASUALTY → SALVOR → STATE → STRANGER. Wages, then injury and death, then salvage, then port and pilotage dues, then tort damage to a third party.

For the numbers: 23 claims, 5 liens, 3 priority tiers, 1 year (2 for wages), 45 days (+30) for an abandoned ship’s auction.

For limb (b), one section and one sentence: section 13 — the Central Government appoints a LIST; and 13(2) — that is no bar to expert witnesses. Then the role in four words: TRANSLATE, STANDARD, TEST, QUANTIFY.

Regulation and source map

  • Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 — Act No. 22 of 2017, 9 August 2017. Long title: to consolidate the laws relating to admiralty jurisdiction, proceedings in connection with vessels, their arrest, detention and sale.
  • s.1(2) — applies to every vessel irrespective of the owner’s residence or domicile; provisos exclude inland vessels, unlaunched newbuildings, warships, naval auxiliaries and non-commercial Government vessels. s.2(1)(l) — wide definition of vessel; broken-up vessels excluded. s.2(2) — undefined words take Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 meanings.
  • s.3 — jurisdiction vests in the High Courts, over waters up to and including territorial waters; extendable by Central Government notification to the Maritime Zones Act, 1976 limit.
  • s.4(1)(a) to (w) — twenty-three heads of maritime claim; Explanation imports Marine Insurance Act, 1963 ss.64(1) and 66(2) for particular and general average. s.4(2) to (4) — accounts, sale, title to proceeds, and the arrested vessel as security.
  • s.5 — arrest in rem; s.5(2) and proviso — sister-ship arrest, barred for s.4(1)(a) claims. ss.6 and 7 — in personam jurisdiction and its restrictions.
  • s.8 — judicial sale vests the vessel free of all encumbrances and liens.
  • s.9 — five maritime liens and their inter se order; continuation through change of ownership, registration or flag; one year, two years for wages; exclusions for oil/HNS and radioactive claims.
  • s.10 — priority: liens, then registered mortgages and charges, then all other claims; equal ranking within a tier; salvage in inverse order of time.
  • s.11 — protection against wrongful or unjustified arrest and excessive security; abandoned vessel auctioned within 45 days, extendable by 30.
  • s.12 — Code of Civil Procedure, 1908 applies. s.14 — appeal to a Division Bench. s.15 — Supreme Court transfer.
  • s.13(1) — the Central Government appoints by notification a list of assessors with prescribed qualifications and experience, duties and fees. s.13(2) — no bar to expert witnesses. s.16(2)(a) — the rule-making power over those matters.
  • s.17(1) — repeal of the Admiralty Court Acts 1840 and 1861, the Colonial Courts of Admiralty Acts 1890 and 1891, and the Letters Patent 1865 in its admiralty application.
  • Surrounding statutes at this sitting: Merchant Shipping Act, 1958; Marine Insurance Act, 1963; Territorial Waters, Continental Shelf, Exclusive Economic Zone and Other Maritime Zones Act, 1976; Inland Vessels Act, 1917; Code of Civil Procedure, 1908.

Uncertainty, currency and the statute boundary

The statutory content of this answer is primary throughout. The Admiralty Act 2017 is held in the True Source corpus as an official India Code text with construction status complete-official, and every section cited above was read directly from it. There is no evidence gap in the law of this question.

The one genuine gap is the subordinate legislation. Section 13(1) requires assessors’ qualifications, duties and fees to be “prescribed”, and section 16(2)(a) gives the rule-making power — but the rules are not held, and no appointing notification is held. The answer therefore states what the Act provides, and describes the qualifications an assessor would hold as a matter of substance, expressly flagged as not a quotation from any prescribed rule. No fee scale is given.

No decided case is cited. Indian admiralty case law is outside the corpus, and attributing a proposition to a judgment that cannot be checked would be exactly the false precision the production protocol forbids. The stem asks for the Act’s key provisions, and the held text supplies them.

THE STATUTE BOUNDARY — the paper’s only HIGH temporal flag. The Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 received assent on 18 August 2025 and commenced on 15 March 2026, repealing the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 by section 324(1). This sitting is eleven months before that assent. The 1958 Act governs, and it matters here specifically because the Admiralty Act cross-refers to it twice — at section 2(2) for undefined terms and at section 4(1)(o), which disapplies sections 150 and 151 of the 1958 Act for crew wage claims. Both references are reproduced as the Act prints them.

That boundary was verified, not assumed. All eight surface classes of this question object — model answer, study guide, recall_15s, major_trap, answer-route core points, retrieval cards, regulations and search_aliases — were swept for the 2025 Act, the Coastal Shipping Act, 2025 and section 324. No hit. The donor used for this question is a February 2025 paper, earlier than this sitting and therefore incapable of importing later law; the sweep was run anyway, because a correct donor does not license an unchecked answer.

No in-month boundary. MEPC 83 sat inside April 2025, on 11 April, but nothing in it touches Indian admiralty jurisdiction. This question’s correctness does not depend on the examination day.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 5 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a What admiralty law is, and what the 2017 Act was enacted to cure
  2. __________a Scope, application and the jurisdiction of the High Courts
  3. __________a Maritime claims, arrest in rem, liens and priority
  4. __________b Who assessors are, and how they are appointed
  5. __________b Qualifications, role, and how assessors assist the court
Flashcards 12

The Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 - Act No. 22 of 2017, dated 9 August 2017. Section 17(1) repealed, in their application to India, the Admiralty Court Act 1840, the Admiralty Court Act 1861, the Colonial Courts of Admiralty Act 1890, the Colonial Courts of Admiralty (India) Act 1891, and the Letters Patent 1865 so far as it applied to the admiralty jurisdiction of the Bombay, Calcutta and Madras High Courts.

The repeal is what 'scope and application' is really asking about - the Act replaced colonial law with a modern statutory jurisdiction.

Section 3: jurisdiction over all maritime claims vests in the respective High Courts, exercisable over the waters up to and including the territorial waters of their respective jurisdictions. The proviso allows the Central Government by notification to extend it to the limit defined in section 2 of the Territorial Waters, Continental Shelf, Exclusive Economic Zone and Other Maritime Zones Act, 1976.

High Courts, not district courts; territorial waters, not the EEZ as of right. Both halves are commonly got wrong.

Section 4(1) recognises twenty-three heads of maritime claim, (a) to (w) - and maritime lien is itself head (w). Section 9(1) creates only five maritime liens: crew wages, loss of life or personal injury, salvage, port and pilotage dues, and tort damage other than to cargo carried. It matters because a lien follows the ship through a change of ownership, registration or flag, and ranks ahead of a registered mortgage under section 10.

All liens are claims; almost no claims are liens - and the difference decides who gets paid.

Section 9(1): (a) wages and other sums due to master, officers and crew including repatriation costs and social insurance contributions; (b) loss of life or personal injury in direct connection with the operation of the vessel; (c) salvage reward including special compensation; (d) port, canal and other waterway dues and pilotage dues and other statutory dues; (e) tort claims for loss or damage caused by the operation of the vessel, other than loss or damage to cargo and containers carried on board.

Crew, casualty, salvor, State, stranger - and the order is the priority.

Section 9(2): it continues notwithstanding any change of ownership, registration or flag, and is extinguished after one year - two years for a wages claim under 9(1)(a) - unless before then the vessel has been arrested or seized and that has led to a forced sale by the High Court. Section 9(3): the period runs continuously, excluding time under arrest or seizure.

The survival through a change of flag is what makes the lien formidable; the one-year cut-off is what makes speed essential.

First, claims on the vessel where there is a maritime lien; second, registered mortgages and charges of the same nature; third, all other claims. Claims within a category rank equally, and claims for various salvages rank in inverse order of time as they accrued.

It puts unpaid crew ahead of the financing bank, which is a deliberate policy choice and a favourite oral question.

No. Section 8: on sale of a vessel by the High Court in exercise of its admiralty jurisdiction, the vessel vests in the purchaser free from all encumbrances, liens, attachments, registered mortgages and charges of the same nature. That is precisely why a court-ordered sale, rather than a private one, is what makes an arrested ship saleable.

The lien follows the ship through a private sale and is destroyed by a judicial one - the contrast is the examinable point.

Section 5(2)'s proviso bars sister-ship arrest where the claim is a dispute regarding possession or ownership under section 4(1)(a) - you cannot claim to own this ship and arrest a different one instead. And under section 11 the court may require the claimant to give an unconditional undertaking or security for loss caused by a wrongful or unjustified arrest, or by excessive security being demanded.

Candidates present arrest as costless leverage; the Act deliberately makes it neither unlimited nor free.

Section 13(1): notwithstanding anything in any other law, the CENTRAL GOVERNMENT shall appoint BY NOTIFICATION a LIST of assessors, with such qualifications and experience in admiralty and maritime matters, nature of duties, fees and ancillary matters as may be prescribed. Section 16(2)(a) gives the corresponding rule-making power.

Not the court, not the parties, and not case by case - a standing panel settled in advance by notification.

An assessor is appointed by the Central Government to a list, advises the court, gives no evidence, is not cross-examined and does not decide the case. An expert witness is retained by a party, gives evidence for that party and is cross-examined on it. Section 13(2) provides that the appointment of assessors is not a bar to the examination of expert witnesses by any of the parties, so the two coexist.

Section 13(2) is the whole constitutional basis of the office and is the highest-value idea in limb (b).

By translating technical evidence - survey reports, class records, engine data, VDR output - into terms the court can follow; by establishing what good practice required, which is what negligence and seaworthiness turn on; by helping the court identify where two party experts genuinely differ and which assumptions drive the difference; by advising on the reasonableness of repair, salvage and off-hire claims; and by reconstructing casualties in collision, grounding, fire and machinery-failure cases. The assessor advises; the court decides.

This is the third demand the April stem adds over earlier sittings, and it is where a Class I engineer can genuinely outperform.

The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. The Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 received assent on 18 August 2025 and commenced on 15 March 2026, repealing the 1958 Act by section 324(1) - well after this sitting. It matters directly because the Admiralty Act cross-refers to the 1958 Act at section 2(2), for words not defined in the Admiralty Act, and at section 4(1)(o), which allows crew wage claims notwithstanding sections 150 and 151 of that Act.

The paper's only HIGH temporal flag, and the cross-references are where a later-sitting answer would carry the wrong statute in.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 — Act 22 of 2017. It consolidates Indian admiralty law and by s.17 repeals the Admiralty Court Acts 1840 and 1861, the Colonial Courts of Admiralty Acts 1890 and 1891 and the Letters Patent 1865. s.1(2) applies to every vessel whatever the owner's residence, excluding inland vessels, unlaunched newbuildings, warships and non-commercial Government vessels. s.3 vests jurisdiction in the High Courts, over waters up to and including territorial waters, extendable by Central Government notification. s.4(1) lists 23 heads of maritime claim, (a) to (w) — ownership, mortgage, operational damage, injury, cargo, carriage, hire, salvage, towage, pilotage, supplies and bunkers, repairs, port dues, crew wages, disbursements, average, sale, insurance premium, brokerage, environmental damage, wreck removal and maritime lien. s.5 gives arrest in rem, with sister-ship arrest under 5(2) but not for ownership claims. s.9 gives five maritime liens — wages, death or injury, salvage, port and pilotage dues, tort — which survive change of owner, registration or flag and die after one year (two for wages). s.10 ranks liens, then registered mortgages, then all other claims; s.8 makes a judicial sale clean; s.11 protects against wrongful arrest. Assessors: s.13(1) — the Central Government appoints by notification a LIST with prescribed qualifications, duties and fees; s.13(2) — that is no bar to expert witnesses. They advise the court, give no evidence, are not cross-examined and do not decide.

Critical numbers
  • Act No. 22 of 2017, dated 9 August 2017
  • Section 4(1) recognises 23 heads of maritime claim, (a) to (w)
  • Section 9(1) creates 5 maritime liens, (a) to (e)
  • Section 10 sets 3 tiers of priority
  • A maritime lien is extinguished after 1 year - 2 years for crew wages - unless arrest has led to a forced sale
  • Section 11(3) - an abandoned arrested vessel is auctioned within 45 days, extendable by 30 days
  • Jurisdiction runs up to and including territorial waters, extendable by notification
  • Repealed enactments: 1840, 1861, 1890, 1891 and the Letters Patent 1865
Critical regulation
Section 3 of the Admiralty Act 2017 vests jurisdiction over all maritime claims in the respective High Courts, exercisable over the waters up to and including their territorial waters, with a proviso allowing the Central Government to extend that limit. Section 4(1) recognises twenty-three heads of maritime claim, section 5 authorises arrest in rem to provide security for them, section 9 creates five maritime liens that survive a change of ownership, registration or flag, and section 10 ranks liens ahead of registered mortgages and of all other claims. For assessors, section 13(1) requires the Central Government to appoint by notification a list of assessors with prescribed qualifications and experience in admiralty and maritime matters, and section 13(2) provides that their appointment is no bar to the examination of expert witnesses by the parties.
Major trap
Treating the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 as the governing merchant shipping statute. It received assent on 18 August 2025 and commenced on 15 March 2026, repealing the 1958 Act by section 324(1) — almost a year after this sitting. The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 governs, and it matters directly because the Admiralty Act cross-refers to it at section 2(2) and disapplies its sections 150 and 151 at section 4(1)(o). The paired substantive trap is confusing a maritime claim with a maritime lien: there are twenty-three heads of claim in section 4 but only five liens in section 9, and it is the lien — not the claim — that follows the ship through a change of flag and ranks ahead of a registered mortgage.

Also on the platform: QP2504 Q8 - P&I cover, which is what stands behind an owner when a maritime claim of the kind section 4 lists is made and security has to be found to lift an arrest

Sources
  • PRIMARY: Admiralty Act 2017, long title - 'An Act to consolidate the laws relating to admiralty jurisdiction, legal proceedings in connection with vessels, their arrest, detention, sale and other matters connected therewith or incidental thereto'; Act No. 22 of 2017, dated 9 August 2017
  • PRIMARY: section 1(2) - the Act applies to EVERY VESSEL, irrespective of the place of residence or domicile of the owner; first proviso excludes an inland vessel as defined in the Inland Vessels Act, 1917 and a vessel under construction that has not been launched unless notified; second proviso excludes warships, naval auxiliaries and other vessels owned or operated by the Central or a State Government used for non-commercial purposes, and notified foreign non-commercial vessels
  • PRIMARY: section 2(1)(f) - 'maritime claim' means a claim referred to in section 4; section 2(1)(g) - 'maritime lien' means a maritime claim against the owner, demise charterer, manager or operator of the vessel referred to in clauses (a) to (e) of sub-section (1) of section 9
  • PRIMARY: section 2(1)(l) and its Explanation - 'vessel' includes any ship, boat, sailing vessel or other description of vessel used or constructed for use in navigation by water, whether propelled or not, and includes a barge, lighter, hovercraft, offshore industry mobile unit and a vessel that has sunk, is stranded or abandoned; a vessel broken up so that it cannot be put into use for navigation, as certified by a surveyor, is not a vessel for that clause
  • PRIMARY: section 2(2) - words used but not defined here and defined in the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 take their meaning from that Act
  • PRIMARY: section 3 - subject to sections 4 and 5, jurisdiction in respect of all maritime claims vests in the respective HIGH COURTS and is exercisable over the waters up to and including the territorial waters of their respective jurisdictions; proviso - the Central Government may by notification extend it up to the limit defined in section 2 of the Territorial Waters, Continental Shelf, Exclusive Economic Zone and Other Maritime Zones Act, 1976
  • PRIMARY: section 4(1) - twenty-three heads of maritime claim, (a) to (w), read in full: possession or ownership; disputes between co-owners as to employment or earnings; mortgage or charge; loss or damage caused by the operation of a vessel; loss of life or personal injury in direct connection with the operation of a vessel; loss or damage to goods; carriage agreements; use or hire agreements; salvage including special compensation; towage; pilotage; goods, materials, provisions, bunker fuel, equipment including containers, or services supplied to the vessel; construction, reconstruction, repair, converting or equipping; port, harbour, canal, dock, light and similar dues; crew wages, repatriation costs and social insurance contributions notwithstanding sections 150 and 151 of the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958; disbursements; particular or general average; sale contract disputes; insurance premium including mutual insurance calls; commission, brokerage or agency fees; damage or threat of damage to the environment, coastline or related interests with preventive and restoration measures; wreck raising, removal, recovery, destruction or rendering harmless; and maritime lien
  • PRIMARY: section 4 Explanation - 'particular average' and 'general average' take the meanings assigned by section 64(1) and section 66(2) respectively of the Marine Insurance Act, 1963
  • PRIMARY: sections 4(2) to 4(4) - the High Court may settle outstanding accounts and order sale; may determine title to the proceeds; and an arrested vessel or its sale proceeds are held as security pending final outcome
  • PRIMARY: section 5(1)(a) to (e) - arrest in rem where the owner or demise charterer liable for the claim is the owner or demise charterer when the arrest is effected, or the claim is on a mortgage or charge, or relates to ownership or possession, or is secured by a maritime lien under section 9
  • PRIMARY: section 5(2) and its proviso - sister-ship arrest in lieu of the vessel against which the claim is made, but NOT for a claim under section 4(1)(a)
  • PRIMARY: section 8 - on judicial sale the vessel vests in the purchaser FREE FROM all encumbrances, liens, attachments, registered mortgages and charges of the same nature
  • PRIMARY: section 9(1)(a) to (e) - the five maritime liens in their inter se order: crew wages and related sums including repatriation and social insurance; loss of life or personal injury in direct connection with the operation of the vessel; salvage reward including special compensation; port, canal, waterway and pilotage dues and other statutory dues; and tort claims arising from loss or damage caused by the operation of the vessel other than to cargo and containers carried on board
  • PRIMARY: section 9(2) and proviso - the lien continues notwithstanding change of ownership, registration or flag, and is extinguished after ONE YEAR unless the vessel has been arrested or seized and that has led to a forced sale; for a wages claim under 9(1)(a) the period is TWO YEARS
  • PRIMARY: section 9(3) - the lien commences on discharge from the vessel for wages claims and when the claim arises for the others, and runs continuously, the period of arrest or seizure being excluded
  • PRIMARY: section 9(4) - no maritime lien attaches for damage in connection with the carriage of oil or other hazardous or noxious substances for which compensation is payable under any law in force, or for radioactive properties of nuclear fuel or radioactive products or waste
  • PRIMARY: section 10(1) - order of priority: claims where there is a maritime lien; registered mortgages and charges of the same nature; all other claims. Section 10(2) - equal claims in one category rank equally, and salvage claims rank in INVERSE order of time
  • PRIMARY: section 11(1) - the High Court may require the arresting claimant to give an unconditional undertaking or security for loss caused by a wrongful or unjustified arrest or by excessive security demanded; section 11(3) - if the owner or demise charterer abandons the vessel after arrest the court shall cause it to be auctioned within FORTY-FIVE DAYS, extendable by THIRTY DAYS for reasons recorded in writing
  • PRIMARY: section 12 - the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908 applies so far as not inconsistent with the Act or its rules
  • PRIMARY: section 13(1) - 'Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being in force, the Central Government shall appoint by notification, a list of assessors with such qualifications and experience in admiralty and maritime matters, the nature of duties to be performed by them, the fees to be paid to them and other ancillary or incidental matters for the purposes of this Act, in the manner as may be prescribed.'
  • PRIMARY: section 13(2) - 'The appointment of assessors shall not be construed as a bar to the examination of expert witnesses by any of the parties in any admiralty proceeding.'
  • PRIMARY: section 14 - an appeal lies from any judgment, decree or final or interim order of a single Judge of the High Court to a DIVISION BENCH of that High Court
  • PRIMARY: section 15 - the Supreme Court may transfer an admiralty proceeding from one High Court to another, the parties having been heard

Understand this first

CII does something none of the older MARPOL rules do: it grades how the ship is operated, every year, and the pass mark moves. The ship's own fuel and distance figures, already reported to the flag State since 2019, are turned into a number; that number is compared with a target derived from the 2019 industry average and tightened by a percentage that grows each year; and the comparison produces a letter from A to E. Everything else follows. The reason AER and EEOI are worth arguing about is that the whole scheme rests on how you measure transport work, and IMO deliberately chose the measure it could audit — capacity times distance — over the measure that is actually true — cargo times distance. And the reason the improvement measures are not an arbitrary list is that the metric is a fraction: you can attack the numerator, the denominator, or the quality of the numbers, and nothing else. At this sitting there is one more thing to hold: the scheme is no longer theoretical. Ships carry ratings, the second reporting year closed three weeks ago, and the ratchet has moved from 5 % to 9 %.

Knowledge map
CII Rating, AER and EEOI
  1. 1Where CII sits in law, and where the scheme has reached
    • regulation 28 of MARPOL Annex VI, introduced by the 2021 revised Annex VI, resolution MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022
    • the OPERATIONAL half of the short-term GHG measure; EEXI at regulation 23 is the technical half
    • EEXI is assessed once on design; CII is assessed every year on operation
  2. 2The mechanism - attained, required, Z and the reference line
    • the rating is a COMPARISON, not an absolute measurement
    • attained annual operational CII = total mass of CO2 emitted (M) divided by total transport work undertaken (W) in the calendar year
    • M is the sum of CO2 from all fuel oil consumed, from DCS fuel figures multiplied by the fuel-to-CO2 conversion factor for each fuel type
  3. 3The rating, and what actually attaches to it
    • reg 28.6 - ratings A to E: major superior, minor superior, moderate, minor inferior, inferior
    • reg 28.6 - the middle point of rating level C IS the required annual operational CII
    • the rating is determined by the Administration, or any organization duly authorized by it, from the attained CII verified against the required CII
  4. 4AER - what it measures and why the regulation uses it
    • G1, MEPC.352(78), draws the distinction the question is really about
    • a DEMAND-BASED CII uses the actual mass or volume of cargo carried
    • a SUPPLY-BASED CII uses the ship's capacity as a PROXY for cargo
  5. 5EEOI, and the critical comparison
    • EEOI - the Energy Efficiency Operational Indicator - is defined in MEPC.1/Circ.684
    • it measures CO2 per tonne of cargo ACTUALLY CARRIED per mile - the demand-based metric
    • it is a truer measure of transport work: a ship that sails full has done more work than one in ballast, and EEOI says so where AER does not
  6. 6Operational measures to improve the rating
    • because the metric is fuel over capacity-distance, only two things are possible - burn less, or travel further for it
    • operational measures dominate because they are available now and cost little
    • SPEED AND VOYAGE MANAGEMENT - consumption rises far faster than speed, so optimising speed to the required arrival time is the largest single lever
  7. 7Technical, fuel and calculation measures, and where they are administered
    • TECHNICAL RETROFIT - low-friction and foul-release hull coatings; propeller retrofit or upgrade; energy saving devices such as ducts, fins and rudder bulbs
    • engine derating with engine or shaft power limitation; waste heat recovery; air lubrication; wind assistance
    • each must be justified for the specific ship and trade - none is universally worthwhile, and no percentage saving is quoted
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  1. Where CII sits in law, and where the scheme has reached
  2. The mechanism - attained, required, Z and the reference line
  3. The rating, and what actually attaches to it
  4. AER - what it measures and why the regulation uses it
  5. EEOI, and the critical comparison
  6. Operational measures to improve the rating
  7. Technical, fuel and calculation measures, and where they are administered

Memory cue. The mechanism is three words in order — ATTAINED, REQUIRED, RATED (steps 1, 2, 3). The argument is two metrics — AER the enforceable one (4), EEOI the true one (5). The measures follow the fraction — numerator and denominator, operational first (6), then technical, fuel and data (7).

Remember7 route headings
Cover75 core points beneath them

Memorise the 7 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 75 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Where CII sits in law, and where the scheme has reached

  • regulation 28 prints NO detention, NO fine, NO certificate withdrawal and no sanction at all - the pressure is commercial and administrative

  • G1, MEPC.352(78), draws the distinction the question is really about

  • a SUPPLY-BASED CII uses the ship's capacity as a PROXY for cargo
  • AER - the Annual Efficiency Ratio - is the supply-based CII which uses DWT as the capacity; cgDIST is the same idea using GT

  • denominator - the ship's capacity multiplied by total distance travelled
  • DWT is the capacity for bulk carriers, tankers, container ships, gas carriers, LNG carriers, general cargo ships, refrigerated cargo carriers and combination carriers
  • GT is the capacity for cruise passenger ships, ro-ro cargo ships including vehicle carriers, and ro-ro passenger ships

  • EEOI - the Energy Efficiency Operational Indicator - is defined in MEPC.1/Circ.684
  • it measures CO2 per tonne of cargo ACTUALLY CARRIED per mile - the demand-based metric

  • its status must be stated exactly - G1 section 3.2 and section 5 list it, with EEPI, cbDIST and clDIST, among indicators encouraged for TRIAL PURPOSES only
  • it is voluntary; regulation 28 is satisfied by the supply-based indicator
  • THE CASE AGAINST AER - it rewards distance, not cargo; ballast legs and idle days at anchor both worsen it
  • it is blind to utilisation - a full ship and a half-empty ship score the same on the same fuel and distance

  • it can be gamed at the margin by slow steaming with more ships, which lowers each ship's AER while the cargo moves no more efficiently overall

  • a car carrier, a container ship and a crude tanker cannot be ranked on tonne-miles

  • operational measures dominate because they are available now and cost little

  • just-in-time arrival converts waiting at anchor - which burns fuel and adds no distance - into a slower, cheaper passage
  • HULL AND PROPELLER CONDITION - in-water cleaning and propeller polishing planned against measured performance, not a calendar
  • TRIM AND DRAUGHT optimisation against the ship's own trim data, and optimum ballast - carry no more than is needed

  • MACHINERY AND AUXILIARY LOAD - shut down surplus generators, use the shaft generator, run pumps and fans on variable frequency drives, manage cargo heating and inert gas, control HVAC and lighting
