Model written answer

1. Where CII sits in law, and where the scheme has reached

The Carbon Intensity Indicator is regulation 28 of MARPOL Annex VI, introduced by the 2021 revised Annex VI (resolution MEPC.328(76)), which entered into force on 1 November 2022. It is the operational half of the short-term GHG measure; EEXI, regulation 23, is the technical half. EEXI is assessed once against the ship’s design; CII is assessed every year against how the ship is actually run.

It applies to ships of 5,000 gross tonnage and above in the cargo and passenger categories listed in regulation 28.1. Under that regulation the ship calculates its attained annual operational CII after the end of calendar year 2023 and after the end of each following calendar year, over 1 January to 31 December, using the fuel oil consumption data collected under regulation 27 — the IMO DCS, running since calendar year 2019. Under regulation 28.2 it reports that value to its Administration within three months of the year end.

The position at this examination is precise, and it is not the position of the scheme’s first year. Calendar years 2023 and 2024 are both complete measured years. Ratings for 2023 have been assigned. The attained CII for 2024 fell due for reporting by 31 March 2025 — three weeks before this paper. And the SEEMP guidance has moved on: the current edition is the 2024 SEEMP Guidelines, resolution MEPC.395(82), adopted 4 October 2024, which superseded the 2022 guidelines.

2. The mechanism - attained, required, Z and the reference line

The rating is a comparison, not an absolute measurement. Three quantities are needed.

  • Attained annual operational CII — what the ship achieved. It is the ratio of the total mass of CO2 emitted, M, to the total transport work undertaken, W, in the calendar year. M is the sum of CO2 from all fuel oil consumed, obtained by multiplying each fuel’s DCS consumption figure by its fuel-mass-to-CO2-mass conversion factor.
  • Required annual operational CII — the target. Regulation 28.4: Required CII = (1 − Z/100) × CIIR.
  • CIIR — the reference value for the ship type, taken from the 2019 reference line under the G2 guidelines, resolution MEPC.353(78).

Z is the annual reduction factor, and it is what makes the standard tighten each year. Under the G3 guidelines, resolution MEPC.338(76), table 1, relative to the 2019 line:

YearReduction factor Z
20235 %
20247 %
2025 — this sitting9 %
202611 %
2027 to 2030Printed blank in G3 — see below

The blank rows were filled in by resolution MEPC.400(83), adopted on 11 April 2025 — the same month as this examination. It replaced table 1 in full, but left the 2023 to 2026 rows exactly as they stood and supplied only the previously unspecified figures for 2027 onwards. The factor governing the 2025 reporting year is therefore 9 % regardless of when in April this paper was sat, and nothing in this answer depends on the later figures.

The consequence for a ship is blunt: the same operating pattern earns a worse rating every year, because the target moves and the ship does not.

3. The rating, and what actually attaches to it

Under regulation 28.6 the attained CII is verified against the required CII to give a rating of A, B, C, D or E — major superior, minor superior, moderate, minor inferior, inferior — determined by the Administration or any organization duly authorized by it. Critically, the middle point of rating level C is the required annual operational CII itself: C is the target, not a pass mark below it.

The G4 guidelines, resolution MEPC.354(78), place the boundaries around that midpoint. Their design intent is a fleet distribution: across a ship-type segment, the middle 30 % of ships fall in C, the upper 20 % and a further 15 % in D and E, and the lower 20 % and a further 15 % in B and A. No numeric boundary value is quoted here — the Annex fixes only the midpoint of C, and the boundaries are ship-type-specific tables in the guidelines.

The teeth are in regulations 28.7 to 28.9. A ship rated D for three consecutive years, or rated E in any single year, must develop a plan of corrective actions; the SEEMP is reviewed to incorporate it and submitted for verification no later than one month after reporting the attained CII; and the ship must duly undertake those actions. Regulation 28.10 works the other way, encouraging Administrations, port authorities and other stakeholders to provide incentives to ships rated A or B — hortatory language that creates no entitlement.

At this sitting, no ship can yet have been rated D for three consecutive years. The earliest possible run is 2023, 2024 and 2025, and 2025 is only three months old. So in April 2025 the only live trigger for regulation 28.7 is a single E rating — a precise point that distinguishes an answer written for this paper from one written for a later one.

Note also what the regulation does not do. There is no detention, no fine and no certificate withdrawn for a poor rating, and regulation 28 prints no withdrawal, rejection or sanction of any kind. The pressure is commercial — charterers, financiers and cargo owners read the rating — and administrative, through the SEEMP. Regulation 28.11 requires a review to be completed by 1 January 2026, covering among other things the reinforcement of corrective actions and the revision of the Z and CIIR values; that review had not reported at this sitting.

4. AER - what it measures and why the regulation uses it

The G1 guidelines, resolution MEPC.352(78), draw the distinction the question is really about. A demand-based CII uses the actual mass or volume of cargo carried. A supply-based CII uses the ship’s capacity as a proxy for cargo. AER — the Annual Efficiency Ratio — is the supply-based CII which uses DWT as the capacity; the variant using GT is cgDIST.

So AER is, in grams of CO2 per deadweight-tonne-mile:

  • numerator — total CO2 from all fuel consumed in the calendar year, from DCS data;
  • denominator — the ship’s capacity × total distance travelled, also from DCS data;
  • capacity is DWT for bulk carriers, tankers, container ships, gas carriers, LNG carriers, general cargo ships, refrigerated cargo carriers and combination carriers, and GT for cruise passenger ships, ro-ro cargo ships including vehicle carriers, and ro-ro passenger ships.

Its virtue is entirely practical: every input is already reported, already auditable and already comparable. Nothing new has to be measured, and two sister ships can be compared on identical terms.

5. EEOI, and the critical comparison

EEOI — the Energy Efficiency Operational Indicator — is defined in MEPC.1/Circ.684 and measures CO2 per tonne of cargo actually carried per mile. It is the demand-based metric and it is a truer measure of transport work: a ship that sails full has done more work than one that sails in ballast, and EEOI says so where AER does not.

Its status must be stated exactly. EEOI is not the regulatory metric. G1 applies the section 4 indicators to all ships caught by regulation 28, and lists EEOI at section 5 — with EEPI, cbDIST and clDIST — among indicators encouraged to be additionally used for trial purposes. It is voluntary.

The critical case against AER is real and should be made:

  • it rewards distance, not cargo. A long ballast leg improves it; three weeks at anchor burning fuel and travelling no distance ruins it;
  • it is blind to utilisation. Two identical ships, one full and one half empty, score identically on the same fuel over the same distance — so the metric cannot reward the commercially efficient operator;
  • it penalises short-sea and multi-port trades, where port time and manoeuvring are a large share of consumption and sea distance is small;
  • it can be gamed at the margin by slow steaming with more ships, which lowers each ship’s AER while the cargo moves no more efficiently overall.

The case for choosing it anyway is equally real. EEOI needs cargo mass data that the DCS does not collect, that is commercially confidential, that an Administration cannot verify as it verifies fuel and distance, and that is not comparable across trades — a car carrier, a container ship and a crude tanker cannot be ranked on tonne-miles. IMO therefore chose the metric that could be audited and enforced over the one that is conceptually correct.

That trade-off — enforceability bought at the cost of validity — is the answer to “critically discuss”, and regulation 28.11’s inclusion of the CIIR and Z values within its own review is IMO’s own acknowledgement that the metric is provisional rather than settled.

6. Operational measures to improve the rating

Because the metric is fuel over capacity-distance, only two things can be done: burn less, or travel further for it. Operational measures dominate because they are available now and cost little.

  • Speed and voyage management. Consumption rises far faster than speed, so optimising speed to the required arrival time is the single largest lever. Just-in-time arrival converts waiting at anchor — which burns fuel and adds no distance — into a slower, cheaper passage.
  • Hull and propeller condition. Planned in-water cleaning and propeller polishing against measured performance, not to a calendar.
  • Trim and draught optimisation against the ship’s own trim data, and optimum ballast — carry no more than is needed.
  • Weather routeing to avoid added resistance, and autopilot tuning to cut rudder-induced drag.
  • Machinery and auxiliary load. Shut down surplus generators, use the shaft generator where fitted, run pumps and fans on variable frequency drives, manage cargo heating and inert gas use, and control accommodation HVAC and lighting.
  • Reduce port and idle consumption — shore power where available, minimise boiler use, avoid unnecessary main engine running.

7. Technical, fuel and calculation measures, and where they are administered

Technical retrofit. Low-friction and foul-release hull coatings; propeller retrofit or upgrade and energy saving devices such as ducts, fins and rudder bulbs; engine derating with engine or shaft power limitation; waste heat recovery; air lubrication; and wind assistance. Each must be justified for the specific ship and trade — none is universally worthwhile, and no percentage saving is claimed here for any of them.

Fuel. Because the numerator is CO2 and not fuel mass, switching to a fuel with a lower carbon factor — LNG, methanol or a biofuel blend — reduces attained CII directly for the same energy delivered.

Data and calculation — two legitimate levers that are not fuel savings at all. First, DCS data quality: the attained CII is only as good as the reported fuel and distance, so flowmeter calibration, consistent bunker figures and correct noon-report distances bear directly on the rating. The DCS regime is itself moving — resolution MEPC.385(81) replaces appendix IX and, by its operative paragraph 4, invites Parties to consider early application of the revised data submission from 1 January 2025; the amendments were deemed accepted on 1 February 2025 but do not enter into force until 1 August 2025, so at this sitting the early application is an invitation, not a duty.

Second, the G5 interim guidelines, resolution MEPC.355(78), allow defined voyage adjustments — which deduct fuel and the associated distance for qualifying periods — and correction factors, which adjust the numerator or denominator for cases such as ice conditions and ship-to-ship operations. G5 states the boundary itself: their use should in no way undermine the goal of reducing the carbon intensity of international shipping.

All of it is administered through the SEEMP. Under regulation 26 an affected ship’s SEEMP must carry the calculation methodology, the required CII for the next three years, an implementation plan for achieving it and a procedure for self-evaluation and improvement, and it is subject to verification and company audit. The plan is now developed against the 2024 SEEMP Guidelines, MEPC.395(82). That is where an examiner expects the improvement measures to live — inside a verified plan, not in a loose list.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The question is one unlimbed block of 16 marks setting three tasks — explain CII rating, critically discuss AER and EEOI, and list improvement measures — and the middle one is where the marks actually are. “Critically discuss” is not “describe”: a candidate who defines AER and EEOI and stops has answered half of the highest-value task.

Sections 1 to 3 build the mechanism, and they must come first because the AER discussion is meaningless without knowing that the rating is a comparison against a moving target. Sections 4 and 5 are the critical core. Sections 6 and 7 are the improvement measures, split operational from technical so the answer reads as a plan rather than a shopping list — and the operational half comes first because it is what a ship’s engineer actually controls.

The reduction factors go in a table. Four numbers in prose are lost; four numbers in a table with the current year marked are unmissable.

Where the scheme has reached at THIS sitting - the currency marks

This is the single most valuable thing on the question, because a great deal of published material was written in the scheme’s first year and says something different. At April 2025:

  • 2023 and 2024 are both complete measured years. The scheme is in its third year, not its first.
  • Ratings exist. Ships hold a CII rating for calendar year 2023. Saying “no ship has yet been rated” is a first-year statement and is wrong here.
  • The 2024 attained CII fell due for reporting by 31 March 2025 — three weeks before this paper, under the three-month rule in regulation 28.2.
  • Z is 9 % for 2025. Not 5 %, which was 2023, and not 7 %, which was 2024.
  • No ship can yet have three consecutive D ratings. The earliest possible run is 2023–2025 and 2025 is incomplete, so the only live trigger for regulation 28.7 is a single E.
  • The SEEMP guidelines are the 2024 edition, MEPC.395(82) of 4 October 2024, which superseded the 2022 guidelines MEPC.346(78).

Any one of those, stated correctly, marks the answer as anchored to its own sitting.

The instrument adopted during the examination month - and why it changes nothing

MEPC.400(83) was adopted on 11 April 2025 and replaced the G3 reduction-factor table. This paper is an April 2025 paper and no examination day is printed on it, so whether the candidate sat before or after 11 April cannot be known.

It does not matter, and that can be shown rather than assumed. The original G3 table printed 5, 7, 9 and 11 per cent for 2023 to 2026 and left 2027 to 2030 blank, footnoted “to be further strengthened and developed taking into account the review of the short-term measure”. MEPC.400(83) supplied those four blanks — 13.625 %, 16.250 %, 18.875 % and 21.500 % — and left 2023 to 2026 untouched.

So Z = 9 % for 2025 on every day of April 2025. The lesson to carry is general: when a boundary falls inside your sitting month, do not guess the date — find out whether the change reaches the thing you are being asked about. Here it does not.

The one thing that must NOT appear in this answer

The IMO Net-Zero Framework was approved at MEPC 83 — on 11 April 2025, inside this examination month. It is not part of the answer, for three independent reasons.

  • It was approved, not adopted. It was circulated with a view to adoption at an extraordinary session, and that session adjourned without adopting anything.
  • On an early-April sitting day it had not even been approved, and the day is unknown — so the answer cannot safely assert even the approval.
  • It is irrelevant to the question. The stem asks about the CII rating, AER and EEOI, all of which live in regulation 28, which is in force and untouched by it.

The correct formula, if it must be mentioned at all in an oral, is “approved and not adopted” — never “pending entry into force”.

The critical argument, stated cleanly

The examiner’s word is critically. The argument to make is that IMO chose an enforceable metric over a valid one.

EEOI is the better measure of what shipping is for — moving cargo. AER measures how far a hull travelled per tonne of CO2, regardless of whether it carried anything. But EEOI requires cargo mass, which is commercially confidential, is not collected by the DCS, cannot be verified by an Administration, and cannot be compared across ship types. AER’s inputs are already reported, already audited and already comparable. So regulation 28 uses AER and G1 relegates EEOI to trial purposes.

The honest closing point is that regulation 28.11 puts the CIIR and Z values inside the scope of its own review — IMO conceding the metric is provisional. A candidate who says that has genuinely criticised the scheme rather than listed its flaws.

AER and EEOI - one line each

AER — supply-based; capacity is a proxy for cargo; capacity is DWT; every input comes from the DCS; this is the regulatory metric under regulation 28. cgDIST is the same idea with GT, used for cruise passenger, ro-ro cargo and ro-ro passenger ships. EEOI — demand-based; uses actual cargo carried; defined in MEPC.1/Circ.684; voluntary, for trial purposes.

The word to hold on to is proxy. Every strength and every weakness of AER follows from substituting capacity for cargo.

Common mistakes

  • Saying no ship has been rated yet. That was true in early 2024; at this sitting ratings for 2023 exist and 2024 has been reported.
  • Giving the wrong reduction factor. For 2025 it is 9 %, relative to the 2019 reference line.
  • Saying a single D rating triggers corrective action. It is D for three consecutive years, or E — and at this sitting a three-year run is not yet arithmetically possible.
  • Saying a D or E rating leads to detention, a fine or a withdrawn certificate. Regulation 28 prints no sanction at all; the consequence is a corrective action plan in the SEEMP.
  • Quoting rating boundary values. The Annex fixes only that the middle point of C is the required CII; the boundaries are ship-type-specific guideline tables and no value should be invented.
  • Treating EEOI as a regulatory requirement. It is voluntary and, in G1, for trial purposes.
  • Confusing CII with EEXI. EEXI is technical and assessed once on design; CII is operational and assessed every year.
  • Citing the 2022 SEEMP guidelines. MEPC.395(82) of October 2024 superseded them before this sitting.
  • Stating MEPC.385(81) as in force. It is accepted but not in force until 1 August 2025; the early DCS application is an invitation.
  • Bringing in the Net-Zero Framework or GFI. Approved during this month, never adopted, and irrelevant to regulation 28.
  • Listing improvement measures with invented percentage savings. Quote mechanism, not vendor figures.
  • Forgetting that data quality and the G5 adjustments are legitimate levers alongside burning less fuel.

Examiner traps

  • “Where does rating C sit?” Regulation 28.6 — the middle point of rating level C is the required annual operational CII. C is the target itself, not a pass mark.
  • “What happens to a ship rated D this year?” Nothing. It is D for three consecutive years, or a single E, that triggers regulation 28.7 — and at April 2025 no three-year run is yet possible.
  • “What is Z this year?” 9 % for 2025. And if asked about 2027: those factors were blank in G3 and were supplied by MEPC.400(83) in April 2025.
  • “A ship waits three weeks at anchor. What does that do to AER?” It worsens it — fuel burned, no distance travelled, so the numerator grows and the denominator does not. The clearest illustration that AER measures distance, not work.
  • “How would EEOI treat the same three weeks?” The same way in the numerator, but EEOI at least credits the cargo actually carried on the voyages either side.
  • “Can an owner improve CII without touching the ship?” Yes — speed and voyage management, better DCS data quality, and the G5 voyage adjustments and correction factors where the ship genuinely qualifies.
  • “Which guideline gives what?” G1 the indicators and calculation, G2 the reference lines, G3 the reduction factors, G4 the rating boundaries, G5 correction factors and voyage adjustments.
  • “Who assigns the rating?” The Administration, or any organization duly authorized by it, from the attained CII verified against the required CII.
  • “Is CII in the SEEMP?” Yes — regulation 26, and the plan is now developed against the 2024 SEEMP Guidelines, MEPC.395(82).

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which regulation is CII, and which Annex? Regulation 28 of MARPOL Annex VI, from MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022.
  • Which ships? Ships of 5,000 GT and above in the cargo and passenger categories listed in regulation 28.1.
  • Write the required CII formula. Required CII = (1 - Z/100) x CII reference.
  • What is the reference year for the reference line? 2019.
  • What is Z for the current year? 9 per cent for 2025.
  • What is AER in words? Grams of CO2 per deadweight tonne-mile, using capacity as a proxy for cargo.
  • Where is EEOI defined and what is its status? MEPC.1/Circ.684; voluntary, for trial purposes under G1 section 5.
  • What triggers a corrective action plan? D for three consecutive years, or a single E.
  • When must the review of the measure be completed? By 1 January 2026, under regulation 28.11.
  • Which SEEMP guidelines apply? The 2024 guidelines, MEPC.395(82).

Memory framework

Hold the mechanism as three words in order: ATTAINED, REQUIRED, RATED — what the ship did, what it was supposed to do, and the letter that follows. The letter grades are the five words of regulation 28.6: major superior, minor superior, moderate, minor inferior, inferior.

For the guidelines, count them in the order the calculation runs: G1 how to calculate it, G2 what to compare it to, G3 how much to tighten it, G4 how to grade it, G5 what may be adjusted.

For the reduction factors, the odd numbers climb by two — 5, 7, 9, 11 for 2023, 2024, 2025, 2026. This sitting is the 9.

For the improvement measures, work down the formula: the numerator is fuel and its carbon, the denominator is capacity and distance. Everything you can do is in one of those two places — or in the quality of the numbers themselves.

Regulation and source map

  • MARPOL Annex VI regulation 28 — Operational carbon intensity. Introduced by resolution MEPC.328(76), the 2021 revised Annex VI, in force 1 November 2022.
  • Reg 28.1, 28.2 — calculate after the end of 2023 and each following year, 1 January to 31 December; report within three months. Reg 27 — the DCS, from calendar year 2019.
  • Reg 28.4 — Required CII = (1 − Z/100) × CIIR. Reg 28.6 — ratings A to E; the middle point of C is the required CII; assigned by the Administration or an organization duly authorized by it.
  • Reg 28.7 to 28.9 — D for three consecutive years or E: corrective action plan, SEEMP revised and submitted within one month of reporting, actions undertaken. Reg 28.10 — incentives encouraged for A and B.
  • Reg 28.11 — review to be completed by 1 January 2026, including revision of Z and CIIR.
  • Reg 26 — the SEEMP, its content and its verification.
  • G1 — MEPC.352(78): indicators and calculation; AER and cgDIST as supply-based CII; EEOI at section 5 for trial purposes. G2 — MEPC.353(78): reference lines. G3 — MEPC.338(76): reduction factors, table 1. G4 — MEPC.354(78): rating boundaries. G5 — MEPC.355(78): correction factors and voyage adjustments.
  • MEPC.395(82), 4 October 2024 — the 2024 SEEMP Guidelines, superseding MEPC.346(78). MEPC.1/Circ.684 — EEOI guidelines.
  • MEPC.385(81) — adopted 22 March 2024, accepted 1 February 2025, in force 1 August 2025; operative paragraph 4 invites early application of the revised appendix IX DCS data from 1 January 2025.
  • MEPC.400(83) — adopted 11 April 2025; replaced G3 table 1, leaving 2023 to 2026 unchanged and supplying 2027 to 2030.
  • Context: MEPC.377(80), the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, adopted 7 July 2023.

Uncertainty, currency and a corpus correction request

No numeric rating boundary and no reference-line coefficient is quoted. This is not caution but a rule: the corpus’s own canonical record for regulation 28 states that the Annex fixes only that the middle point of rating level C equals the required annual operational CII, prints no band width and no boundary value, and that the boundaries are not derivable from the corpus and no value may be supplied for them from any source. The answer explains the mechanism and names the guideline that carries the figures.

No percentage saving is attributed to any improvement measure. Gains from hull cleaning, propeller polishing, energy saving devices, air lubrication, wind assistance or speed reduction are installation-specific and vendor-sourced. Mechanism and direction only.

No body text of MARPOL Annex VI is reproduced. The Annex is held frozen and citation-ready in the corpus, with a facts-and-pointers export policy; identity, structure, numerics, duty-bearers and temporal facts are used, and the wording above is the answer’s own.

A TRUE_SOURCE_CORRECTION_REQUEST is raised and has not been actioned locally. Three canonical corpus records — the Annex VI amendment register and the canonical records for regulations 26 and 28 — give the entry into force of MEPC.328(76) as 1 November 2023. It is 1 November 2022, stated in that resolution’s own operative paragraph 3 and corroborated in the recitals of two further held resolutions, MEPC.352(78) and MEPC.346(78). QP production never edits True Source, so the corpus was not modified and no frozen record was touched; this answer uses 1 November 2022 and the defect is reported for the Founder’s decision.

Currency. Every instrument cited is in the state it was in at this sitting: G1, G2, G4 and G5 from MEPC 78 and G3 from MEPC 76; the 2024 SEEMP guidelines; MEPC.385(81) accepted but not in force; MEPC.400(83) adopted during this month and shown to be immaterial to the 2025 factor; the regulation 28.11 review not reported; and the Net-Zero Framework absent, having been approved but never adopted. MEPC.392(82), whose entry into force is 1 March 2026, and every instrument of the 34th Assembly of December 2025, are deliberately absent.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________Where CII sits in law, and where the scheme has reached
  2. __________The mechanism - attained, required, Z and the reference line
  3. __________The rating, and what actually attaches to it
  4. __________AER - what it measures and why the regulation uses it
  5. __________EEOI, and the critical comparison
  6. __________Operational measures to improve the rating
  7. __________Technical, fuel and calculation measures, and where they are administered
Flashcards 13

Regulation 28 of MARPOL Annex VI, introduced by the 2021 revised Annex VI, resolution MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022. It applies to ships of 5,000 gross tonnage and above falling into the cargo and passenger categories listed in regulation 28.1. It is the operational half of the short-term measure; EEXI at regulation 23 is the technical half.

The regulation number and the tonnage threshold are the two cheapest marks in the question.

Required annual operational CII = (1 - Z/100) x CII reference, the reference being the 2019 reference line value for the ship type under G2. Under G3, MEPC.338(76) table 1, Z is 5 % for 2023, 7 % for 2024, 9 % for 2025 and 11 % for 2026. At an April 2025 sitting the operative factor is 9 %.

Getting the current year's Z right is what shows the answer is anchored to its own sitting rather than inherited.

Into its third year. Calendar 2023 and 2024 are both complete measured years; ratings for 2023 have been assigned; and the 2024 attained CII fell due for reporting by 31 March 2025 under the three-month rule - three weeks before the paper. Crucially, no ship can yet have been rated D for three consecutive years, because the earliest possible run is 2023 to 2025 and 2025 is incomplete. So the only live trigger for regulation 28.7 is a single E.

A great deal of published material was written in the scheme's first year and says no ship has been rated. That is wrong here.

The Annual Efficiency Ratio - the supply-based CII which uses deadweight tonnage as the capacity. The numerator is total CO2 from all fuel consumed in the calendar year; the denominator is the ship's capacity multiplied by the total distance travelled. The variant using gross tonnage instead of DWT is cgDIST, used for cruise passenger, ro-ro cargo and ro-ro passenger ships.

Supply-based means capacity is a PROXY for cargo, and every strength and weakness of AER follows from that one substitution.

AER is supply-based: it uses the ship's capacity as a proxy for cargo, takes every input from the IMO DCS, and is the metric by which regulation 28 is satisfied. EEOI is demand-based: it uses the cargo actually carried, is defined in MEPC.1/Circ.684, and under G1 section 5 is encouraged for trial purposes only. EEOI is voluntary.

This is the whole of the critical limb, and treating EEOI as mandatory is the commonest error in it.

Because EEOI requires cargo mass data which the DCS does not collect, which is commercially confidential, which an Administration cannot verify as it verifies fuel and distance, and which is not comparable across ship types. AER's inputs are already reported, already audited and already comparable. IMO chose the enforceable metric over the valid one - and regulation 28.11 puts the reference values and Z factors inside its own review, which concedes the choice is provisional.

'Critically discuss' is asking for this trade-off, not for a list of AER's flaws.

A single D does nothing, and two do nothing. It is D for three consecutive years, or E in any one year, that triggers regulation 28.7: the ship develops a plan of corrective actions, the SEEMP is reviewed to include it and submitted to the Administration no later than one month after reporting the attained CII, and the actions must be duly undertaken. There is no detention, fine or certificate withdrawal - regulation 28 prints no sanction at all.

The three-consecutive-years condition and the total absence of a penal sanction are both routinely got wrong.

Regulation 28.6 - the middle point of rating level C is the value equivalent to the required annual operational CII. C is the target itself, not a pass mark below it. The boundaries cannot be stated from the Annex: it fixes only that midpoint and prints no band width or boundary value. The boundaries are ship-type-specific tables in G4, MEPC.354(78), whose design intent is that the middle 30 % of a segment falls in C.

It explains why the whole fleet cannot simply settle at C as the standard tightens - and why quoting a boundary number is a mistake.

G1, MEPC.352(78) - the indicators and the calculation method. G2, MEPC.353(78) - the reference lines. G3, MEPC.338(76) - the reduction factors. G4, MEPC.354(78) - the rating boundaries. G5, MEPC.355(78) - correction factors and voyage adjustments. They run in the order the calculation runs: calculate, compare, tighten, grade, adjust.

Naming them and what each does is the clearest sign a candidate has opened the instruments.

It worsens it. Fuel is burned, so the numerator grows, while no distance is travelled, so the denominator does not. Time at anchor is pure penalty under AER - which is exactly why just-in-time arrival, converting waiting into a slower passage, is one of the strongest operational measures.

It is the sharpest single illustration that AER measures distance covered, not work done.

Speed and voyage management, especially just-in-time arrival; improving the quality of the DCS fuel and distance data on which the attained CII is calculated; and applying the G5 voyage adjustments and correction factors where the ship genuinely qualifies, for example ice conditions or ship-to-ship operations. G5 adds that their use must in no way undermine the goal of reducing carbon intensity.

It shows the metric is a fraction with three attackable parts, not just a fuel bill.

The 2024 Guidelines for the development of a SEEMP, resolution MEPC.395(82), adopted 4 October 2024, which superseded the 2022 guidelines MEPC.346(78). Under regulation 26 an affected ship's SEEMP must carry the methodology for calculating the attained CII and the reporting process, the required CII for the next three years, an implementation plan for achieving it, and a procedure for self-evaluation and improvement - and it is subject to verification and company audit.

Citing the 2022 guidelines is exactly the error a stem-identical January 2024 donor would carry forward.

MEPC.400(83) was adopted on 11 April 2025 and replaced the G3 reduction-factor table. It does not change the answer: it left 2023 to 2026 unchanged at 5, 7, 9 and 11 per cent and supplied only the previously blank 2027 to 2030 figures. Z remains 9 % for 2025 whichever day in April the paper was sat. Separately, the IMO Net-Zero Framework was approved at the same session but never adopted, and has no place in a regulation 28 answer.

It is the paper's in-month boundary, and the discipline is to check whether the change reaches the question rather than to guess the date.

Quick revision
15-second recall
CII is regulation 28 of MARPOL Annex VI, from the 2021 revised Annex VI (MEPC.328(76)), in force 1 November 2022, applying to ships of 5,000 GT and above. Calculate the attained CII after each calendar year from DCS data (reg 27, since 2019) and report within three months. Required CII = (1 − Z/100) × CII reference, the reference being the 2019 line under G2, and Z being 5, 7, 9, 11 per cent for 2023 to 2026 under G3 — 9 % for 2025. Rating A to E; the middle point of C is the required CII; G4 sets the boundaries. D for three consecutive years, or a single E, means a corrective action plan in the SEEMP submitted within one month of reporting; incentives are encouraged for A and B; there is no detention, fine or sanction. At this sitting ratings for 2023 exist, 2024 was reported by 31 March 2025, and no three-year D run is yet possible — so only a single E bites. AER is the supply-based CII using DWT as a proxy for cargo — the regulatory metric; cgDIST uses GT. EEOI (MEPC.1/Circ.684) uses actual cargo and is voluntary, for trial purposes under G1 section 5. The critical point: IMO chose the enforceable metric over the valid one. Improve by speed and voyage management, hull and propeller condition, trim and ballast, auxiliary load, retrofit and fuel switching — plus DCS data quality and the G5 adjustments, all administered through the SEEMP under the 2024 guidelines MEPC.395(82).

Critical numbers
  • Regulation 28 applies to ships of 5,000 gross tonnage and above
  • Reduction factor Z relative to the 2019 reference line: 5 % in 2023, 7 % in 2024, 9 % in 2025, 11 % in 2026
  • MEPC.400(83), adopted 11 April 2025, supplied 2027 to 2030 and left 2023 to 2026 unchanged
  • Attained CII reported within three months after the end of the calendar year - so 2024 was due by 31 March 2025
  • Revised SEEMP submitted no later than one month after reporting the attained CII
  • D for three consecutive years, or E in one year, triggers corrective action
  • Five rating grades; G4's design intent is middle 30 % at C, upper 20 % and further 15 % at D and E
  • IMO DCS data collected from calendar year 2019 under regulation 27
  • Review of the measure to be completed by 1 January 2026 under regulation 28.11
  • MEPC.385(81): deemed accepted 1 February 2025, in force 1 August 2025, early DCS application invited from 1 January 2025
Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex VI regulation 28. Regulation 28.4 gives the required annual operational CII as (1 − Z/100) × CII reference; regulation 28.6 gives the A to E rating and fixes the middle point of rating level C at the required annual operational CII, the rating being determined by the Administration or any organization duly authorized by it; and regulations 28.7 to 28.9 require a plan of corrective actions in the SEEMP, submitted for verification no later than one month after reporting, for a ship rated D for three consecutive years or rated E.
Major trap
Writing the answer in the scheme's first-year frame. At an April 2025 sitting, ratings for calendar year 2023 already exist and the 2024 attained CII fell due for reporting by 31 March 2025, so “no ship has yet been rated” is false; the reduction factor is 9 % for 2025, not 7 %; and the SEEMP guidance is the 2024 edition MEPC.395(82), not the 2022 one. The paired traps are treating EEOI as a regulatory requirement when G1 lists it for trial purposes only, and saying a single D rating triggers corrective action — it is D for three consecutive years or a single E, and at this sitting a three-year run is not yet arithmetically possible.

Also on the platform: QP2504 Q3 - underwater radiated noise, whose mitigation measures are very largely the same hull, propeller, speed and trim measures that improve a CII rating

Repeated — same wording Returns in the same words from January 2024 Q3.

Sources
  • PRIMARY: MEPC.328(76) operative paragraph 3 - the amendments to MARPOL Annex VI shall enter into force on 1 NOVEMBER 2022; corroborated in the recitals of MEPC.352(78) and MEPC.346(78), both of which note the 2021 revised Annex VI 'which will enter into force on 1 November 2022'
  • PRIMARY (identity, structure and numerics only - the Annex is frozen and citation-ready): MARPOL Annex VI regulation 28, Operational carbon intensity. The corpus canonical record MARPOLVI_REG28 confirms that 28.6 is the ONLY provision in the Annex creating the operational carbon intensity rating; that the labels are A, B, C, D and E, descriptors major superior, minor superior, moderate, minor inferior and inferior; that the rating is determined by the Administration or any organization duly authorized by it from the attained annual operational CII documented and verified against the required annual operational CII; and that the middle point of rating level C is the required annual operational CII
  • PRIMARY (structure and numerics): MARPOLVI_REG28 corrective action model - a ship rated D for THREE CONSECUTIVE YEARS or rated E develops a plan of corrective actions (28.7); the SEEMP shall be reviewed to include it and the revised SEEMP submitted for verification preferably with the report but IN NO CASE later than ONE MONTH after reporting the attained annual operational CII under 28.2 (28.8); and the ship shall duly undertake the planned actions (28.9). The record notes expressly that the three-consecutive-year period attaches to the D limb only, that a single D or two carries no consequence, and that regulation 28 prints NO withdrawal, rejection, re-submission or sanction
  • PRIMARY (numerics): MARPOLVI_REG28 temporal model - first measured calendar year 2023; recurring measurement window 1 January to 31 December; annual reporting deadline three months after the end of each calendar year; transfer cohort condition 1 January 2023; relative corrective-action deadline one month after reporting
  • PRIMARY (numerics): MARPOLVI_REG28 rating semantics - the Annex fixes ONLY that the middle point of rating level C equals the required annual operational CII, and states NO band width and no boundary value. The boundaries are not derivable from the Annex and no value may be supplied for them from any source
  • PRIMARY (identity and structure): MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 - the duty to keep a SEEMP on board is unconditional in time from the Annex's entry into force; the carbon-intensity content had to be in the SEEMP on or before 1 January 2023
  • PRIMARY: Resolution MEPC.352(78), G1, adopted 10 June 2022, section 2 - the demand-based CII uses actual cargo carried, the supply-based CII uses capacity as a proxy; AER is the supply-based CII which uses DWT as the capacity, and cgDIST is the supply-based CII which uses GT
  • PRIMARY: MEPC.352(78) section 3.1 and 3.2 - for all ships to which regulation 28 applies the section 4 indicators should be applied; the section 5 indicators are encouraged to be additionally used for TRIAL PURPOSES
  • PRIMARY: MEPC.352(78) section 4.1 and 4.2 - the attained annual operational CII is the ratio of the total mass of CO2 (M) emitted to the total transport work (W) undertaken in a given calendar year; M is the sum of CO2 from all fuel oil consumed, calculated as fuel consumption multiplied by the fuel-oil-mass-to-CO2-mass conversion factor for each fuel type, as reported under IMO DCS and in line with MEPC.308(73)
  • PRIMARY: MEPC.352(78) section 5 - EEOI as defined in MEPC.1/Circ.684; the formulas use the same M, capacity and total distance as the attained CII, with Dl the laden distance travelled, ALB the available lower berths of a cruise passenger ship and Lanemeter the lane length of a ro-ro ship
  • PRIMARY: Resolution MEPC.338(76), G3, adopted 17 June 2021, table 1 read verbatim - reduction factor Z relative to the 2019 reference line: 2023 5%, 2024 7%, 2025 9%, 2026 11%; 2027 to 2030 printed blank and footnoted 'to be further strengthened and developed taking into account the review of the short-term measure'; a further note records that Z of 1%, 2% and 3% were set for 2020 to 2022, similar to business as usual until entry into force of the measure
  • PRIMARY: Resolution MEPC.354(78), G4, adopted 10 June 2022 - the rating boundaries are expected to place the middle 30% of ships across the fleet segment at C, the upper 20% and further upper 15% at D and E, and the lower 20% and further lower 15% at B and A; the boundaries are determined from the required annual operational CII in conjunction with vectors, and the actual distribution in any year need not match the 2019 scenario
  • PRIMARY: Resolution MEPC.353(78), G2 - reference lines for use with operational carbon intensity indicators. Resolution MEPC.355(78), G5, adopted 10 June 2022 - 2022 Interim Guidelines on correction factors and voyage adjustments for CII calculations
  • PRIMARY: Resolution MEPC.395(82), 2024 Guidelines for the development of a Ship Energy Efficiency Management Plan (SEEMP), adopted 4 OCTOBER 2024. The corpus instrument log records MEPC.346(78) as SUPERSEDED by it and MEPC.395(82) as the CURRENT guidance
  • PRIMARY: Resolution MEPC.385(81), adopted 22 March 2024 - operative paragraph 2 determines the amendments deemed accepted on 1 FEBRUARY 2025; operative paragraph 3 records entry into force on 1 AUGUST 2025; operative paragraph 4 INVITES Parties to consider the EARLY APPLICATION of the amendments to appendix IX with regard to information to be submitted to the IMO Ship Fuel Oil Consumption Database from 1 January 2025. The corpus amendment register records its operations as including a full replacement of appendix IX
  • PRIMARY: Resolution MEPC.400(83), adopted 11 APRIL 2025 - Amendments to the 2021 Guidelines on the operational carbon intensity reduction factors relative to reference lines (G3). Its recitals record that regulation 28.5 requires Z factors to be established for each ship type and that regulation 28.11 requires the review of the CII regulations to be completed by 1 January 2026. Its annex replaces table 1 with 2023 5%, 2024 7%, 2025 9%, 2026 11%, 2027 13.625%, 2028 16.250%, 2029 18.875%, 2030 21.500%
  • PRIMARY: Resolution MEPC.377(80), 2023 IMO Strategy on Reduction of GHG Emissions from Ships, adopted 7 July 2023 - used as context only
  • INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED: QP2401-Q3 (January 2024), built and stem-identical - the answer structure was reused and every sitting-relative statement re-authored. QP2410-Q8 (October 2024), built and limb-level - used as the temporal cross-check that surfaced the MEPC.395(82) supersession and the MEPC.385(81) DCS position
Unresolved
  • The numeric boundary values in the G4 rating tables and the numeric coefficients of the G2 reference lines are ship-type specific and are NOT reproduced. The corpus record for regulation 28 states this in the strongest terms: the Annex fixes only that the middle point of rating level C equals the required CII, states no band width and no boundary value, and the boundaries are not derivable from the corpus, so no value may be supplied for them from any source. The answer explains the mechanism and names the guideline that carries the figures.
  • No percentage saving is attributed to any improvement measure. Gains from hull cleaning, propeller polishing, energy saving devices, air lubrication, wind assistance or speed reduction are installation-specific and vendor-sourced. Mechanism and direction only.
  • The outcome of the regulation 28.11 review had NOT been reported at this sitting - the review was required to be completed by 1 January 2026. Nothing is asserted about what it decided, beyond the fact, established from the resolution's own face, that MEPC.400(83) of 11 April 2025 supplied the previously blank 2027 to 2030 Z factors.
  • MARPOL Annex VI is held FROZEN and CITATION-READY ONLY. Its export policy is facts-and-pointers, so no body text of regulation 26, 27 or 28 is reproduced anywhere in this answer; identity, structure, numerics, duty-bearers and temporal facts are used, and the wording is the answer's own.

It is 2022-11-01 on the face of the resolution's own operative paragraph 3, corroborated in the recitals of MEPC.352(78) and MEPC.346(78). A TRUE_SOURCE_CORRECTION_REQUEST is recorded; the corpus was not modified and no frozen record was touched.

Re-verify before publication
  • At this sitting ratings exist for calendar year 2023, the 2024 attained CII fell due for reporting by 31 March 2025, and NO ship can yet have been rated D for three consecutive years — The load-bearing sitting-relative statement in the answer, derived from regulations 28.1 and 28.2 and from the fact that the earliest possible three-year run is 2023 to 2025. It is exactly the kind of statement a later editor would silently modernise. Confirm it is still stated in the sitting frame before publication. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK)
  • The reduction factor Z for 2025 is 9% relative to the 2019 reference line, and MEPC.400(83) of 11 April 2025 does not change it — Taken verbatim from G3 table 1 and verified row by row against the replacement table in MEPC.400(83). This is the in-month boundary of the paper and the proof of immateriality must not be lost in editing: the answer states the 2025 factor and rests nothing on the 2027 to 2030 figures. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK)
  • MEPC.385(81) is deemed accepted but NOT in force at this sitting, and the early application of the revised appendix IX DCS data from 1 January 2025 is an INVITATION and not a duty — Date-driven. It enters into force 1 August 2025, four months after this sitting. A later editor could easily state it as operative. The accepted-not-in-force classification and the invitation/duty distinction must both survive. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK)
  • The 2024 SEEMP Guidelines, MEPC.395(82), superseded MEPC.346(78) and are the current guidelines at this sitting — Date-driven and it is precisely the delta a stem-identical donor cannot show. Adopted 4 October 2024, six months before this sitting. Re-check before publication that the answer cites the 2024 guidelines and not the 2022 ones. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK)
  • No rating boundary value and no reference-line coefficient is quoted anywhere in the answer — Deliberate, and required by the corpus itself: the canonical record for regulation 28 states that the Annex fixes only the midpoint of rating level C, prints no band width and no boundary, and that the boundaries are NOT derivable from the corpus and no value may be supplied for them from any source. Recorded as an accepted limitation, not an omission to be filled. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • No percentage saving is attributed to any improvement measure — Gains from hull cleaning, propeller polishing, energy saving devices, air lubrication, wind assistance and speed reduction are installation-specific and vendor-sourced. The production protocol forbids that precision. Mechanism and direction only. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • TRUE_SOURCE_CORRECTION_REQUEST - three canonical corpus records give the entry into force of MEPC.328(76) as 2023-11-01; it is 2022-11-01 — Established from the resolution's own operative paragraph 3 and corroborated in the recitals of MEPC.352(78) and MEPC.346(78), all held official texts. The error appears in amendment-register.json, MARPOLVI_REG26 and MARPOLVI_REG28, inside an instrument that is FROZEN and QUALIFIED. QP production never edits True Source, so the corpus was not modified and this answer uses the correct date. The Founder must decide on the correction. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
Understand this first

The fastest way into this question is to notice that four different people can inspect the same tanker and they are not asking the same question. The flag State surveyor asks does it comply with the conventions? and can invalidate a certificate. The classification society asks does it meet the rules? and can withdraw class. The port State control officer asks is it substandard, here and now? and can detain it. The vetting inspector asks something none of them asks: would I put my cargo, my terminal and my company's name behind this ship? — and the sanction is not a certificate or a detention but no fixture. That is why vetting is simultaneously voluntary in law and unavoidable in practice: a tanker no major charterer will accept is commercially finished, whatever its certificates say. Everything else follows. The programme is an information system, not an approval system, because the acceptance decision belongs to each charterer. Its instruments split ship from shore. And the whole of limb (b) is about one thing: the industry rebuilt that inspection regime, and by this sitting the rebuild had landed.
