Model written answer

(a) The concept of vetting, its significance, and its contribution to improvement and safety culture — 8 marks

1. What vetting is, and what it is not

A vetting inspection is a commercial risk assessment of a tanker, carried out by or for a charterer, oil major or terminal before the ship is employed or accepted. It is voluntary in law — no IMO instrument requires it — and it is enforced by the market rather than by any Administration.

It is best defined by contrast, because four different people can inspect the same tanker and none of them is asking the same question:

InspectionThe question it asksThe sanction
Statutory survey (flag or RO)Does the ship comply with the conventions?An invalid or withdrawn certificate
Class surveyDoes the ship meet the society’s rules?Loss or suspension of class
Port State control (A.1185(33))Is the ship substandard, here and now?Detention
VettingWould I put my cargo, terminal and reputation behind this ship?No fixture

Vetting arose because a charterer’s liability and reputation do not stop at the ship’s rail. After the major tanker pollution casualties, the oil companies concluded that accepting a tanker on the strength of its certificates alone was not a defensible risk position. OCIMF — the Oil Companies International Marine Forum — operates the SIRE programme through which the industry does that assessment.

2. How the programme works - an information model, not an approval scheme

SIRE is an information programme. This is the most misunderstood point in the whole subject and the cheapest mark to secure. The sequence is:

  1. an accredited inspector, nominated by a submitting member, attends the ship and completes a report;
  2. the operator has a right to submit comments on that report;
  3. the report is uploaded to the SIRE database; and
  4. recipient members download it and apply their own acceptance criteria.

OCIMF neither approves nor rejects any ship. There is no such thing as an “OCIMF-approved tanker”. The same report can produce acceptance by one charterer and refusal by another, because the acceptance decision belongs to each member and its criteria are its own. The design purpose was to replace many duplicated company inspections of the same ship with one shared report — reducing burden while raising the standard of scrutiny.

3. The instruments - and the ship/shore split

The programme rests on a small set of instruments, and the organising idea is that the inspection examines the ship while the management framework examines the company:

  • an inspection questionnaire, completed by the accredited inspector on board — the ship-side instrument;
  • a vessel particulars questionnaire, maintained by the operator, describing the ship and feeding the inspection;
  • TMSA, the tanker management and self-assessment framework, which assesses the shore-side management system element by element against staged levels with key performance indicators, self-assessed and verified; and
  • CDI, which operates the parallel inspection scheme for chemical and gas carriers.

Beneath all of them sits the ISM Code, which supplies the statutory safety management system that the industry schemes assess against and into which their findings are fed.

4. Significance in the maritime oil trade

  1. Employment. Major charterers will not fix a tanker without an acceptable vetting position. Vetting therefore determines what the ship can trade and for whom.
  2. Terminal acceptance. Terminals apply their own vetting criteria before allowing a berth, so a poor position can close a trade route as well as a charter.
  3. Commercial value. Vetting history bears on charter rates, marketability and residual value.
  4. Risk transfer. The charterer is exposed to pollution liability, cargo loss, delay and reputational damage. Vetting is how that exposure is managed before it arises rather than litigated afterwards.
  5. Insurance and finance. Underwriters and lenders read vetting performance as a proxy for operational quality.
  6. Standard-setting. Because acceptance is commercial rather than statutory, the market standard runs ahead of the regulatory minimum and is enforced faster than any Administration could enforce it.

The last point is the real significance and is worth stating explicitly: vetting is where the oil trade sets a standard higher than the law requires, and makes it stick.

5. Continuous improvement and safety culture - and the honest criticism

The contribution is mechanical, not aspirational, and the mechanisms should be named:

  • Frequency and independence. Far more scrutiny than statutory survey provides, carried out by inspectors with no relationship to the owner, class or flag.
  • The closed loop. Observations are routed into the ship’s safety management system as non-conformities under ISM Code element 9, with root-cause analysis and corrective action. This is what converts a finding into a change in practice — and, critically, it is auditable.
  • Management maturity. TMSA addresses causes ashore rather than symptoms on deck, and its staged levels give a company a path to improve along rather than a pass mark to clear.
  • Industry learning. Aggregated observations reveal trends that no single operator could see from its own fleet.
  • Behaviour. A crew that expects competent, independent technical scrutiny maintains the ship for the scrutiny it expects — which is the honest description of how inspection shapes culture.

The criticism must be made, because limb (b) is the answer to it. Frequent inspection against a known and learnable question set can produce compliance behaviour rather than safety behaviour: what is predictable can be rehearsed, and what is rehearsed can be presented without being lived. Add the cumulative inspection burden — vetting, port State control, class, flag, terminal and internal audit, all falling on the same crew — and the risk is a ship that is very good at being inspected. That is not an argument against vetting; it is the specification for redesigning it.

(b) Emerging trends and advancements, including technology and data analytics — 8 marks

6. SIRE 2.0 - the position at this sitting

The dominant advancement in tanker vetting is SIRE 2.0, OCIMF’s rebuilt inspection regime. It was deliberately delivered as a phased roll-out rather than a single switch-over — internal testing, then a closed beta, then an open beta available to programme users.

Phased implementation began in September 2024, and at this examination SIRE 2.0 is the operative programme. That should be stated plainly, because a great deal of published material — textbooks, training notes and older model answers — still describes it as forthcoming or “in transition”. It is not.

That change of state matters to the limb itself. The advancement the industry spent years calling emerging has arrived, so this limb has two jobs: report the arrival accurately, and identify what is genuinely still emerging at this sitting — which is the first substantial body of inspection data accumulating under the new observation model, human-factors observation practice maturing among inspectors and among the operators receiving it, and risk-based scope extending toward cyber security and crew welfare.

7. What SIRE 2.0 actually changes

A bespoke, compiled questionnaire. Instead of one fixed list applied to every vessel, an inspection compiler builds a questionnaire for that ship and that inspection, drawn from the vessel particulars, a pre-inspection questionnaire and the ship’s own history, and ranked by risk.

Four question types, each with a distinct design intent:

TypeWhat it doesWhy
CoreAppears on every relevant inspectionThe significant risks are always examined
RotationalDrawn unpredictably from a wider poolDefeats rehearsal — the direct answer to the limb (a) criticism
CampaignDeployed across all inspections for a periodResponds to an incident or an industry-wide trend
ConditionalTriggered by the operator’s own responses or the ship’s historyTargets scrutiny where this ship’s risk actually lies

Three observation tools instead of a binary record. The old model recorded compliance or non-compliance. SIRE 2.0 observes across hardware, process and human factors — asking not only whether the equipment is right, but whether the procedure is workable and whether the people can actually do the task. The human-factors tool can record positive observations where performance exceeds expectation, which is a structural change: an inspection record that can say what is being done well, not only what is wrong.

Digital inspection. Tablet-based capture with photographic and location capability, so evidence is recorded rather than described — and so the output is structured data rather than narrative.

Inspector quality assurance. Accredited inspectors are themselves audited in the field, attacking the inconsistency between inspectors that was one of the older programme’s real weaknesses.

Read together, these are not incremental improvements. They are a deliberate answer to the criticism in limb (a): unpredictability defeats rehearsal, and human-factors observation looks past the presented condition to the practice underneath it.

8. Data analytics, remote inspection, and the limits

Analytics operates at three levels. At ship level, an operator trends its own observations by category and vessel and sees a weakness developing before it becomes a finding. At fleet level, it benchmarks across the fleet and integrates with the shore-side framework’s key performance indicators. At industry level, aggregated observations identify emerging risks across the tanker fleet that no single operator could detect. The campaign question is the visible feedback loop: analytics identifies a trend, and the next round of inspections goes and asks about it.

Remote and hybrid inspection — document review, video and remote witnessing in place of some physical attendance — was accelerated by the pandemic and has persisted. The statutory world moved the same way: the current Assembly survey guidelines expressly take account of remote surveys and advanced technology. Alongside it run continuing efforts at harmonisation, reducing duplication between vetting, class, flag and terminal inspection, and a broadening of scope toward cyber security and crew welfare.

The limits must be stated, and stating them is what makes this a discussion rather than a list:

  • Analytics inherits the quality of its inputs. Inconsistent observation produces confident nonsense at scale, which is precisely why inspector quality assurance was built in alongside it.
  • The data is commercially sensitive, which constrains how widely it can be shared and therefore how much industry-level learning is actually realised.
  • Risk-based targeting creates blind spots wherever the model treats something as low risk — and the model is built from past findings, so it is structurally slow to see a new failure mode.
  • A tablet does not open a crankcase. Remote techniques extend reach; they do not replace an experienced inspector standing in the machinery space. The instrument, in the end, is still the inspector’s judgement.
Study guide

Why this structure scores

The printed split is 8 + 8, and the two limbs are genuinely different in kind. Limb (a) is definitional and evaluative; limb (b) is about change. That second word governs everything: “emerging trends or advancements” is a question whose correct answer depends on when you sit it, and a candidate answering from material written two years earlier will describe a retired inspection programme as the current one.

Limb (a) opens with a four-row contrast table because the fastest way to define vetting is to say what the other three inspections ask and what each can do to you. An examiner reading “the sanction is no fixture” knows immediately that the candidate has understood the subject.

Limb (a) closes on the criticism, and that placement is deliberate rather than balanced-sounding: the criticism is the specification that limb (b)’s redesign answers. Ending (a) with “a predictable inspection can be rehearsed” and opening (b) with a questionnaire built to be unpredictable makes the two limbs one argument instead of two lists.

The change of state - and why it is the whole of limb (b)

SIRE 2.0 is the operative tanker vetting programme at April 2025. Phased implementation began in September 2024, seven months before this paper.

That single fact is what separates a current answer from an inherited one. A model answer written for a January 2024 sitting — and the same question was set then, word for word — correctly described SIRE 2.0 as being in transition with the previous questionnaire still operative. Reproduced here, that would be describing a superseded programme as the present one, and would miss the largest development the limb is asking about.

There is a second-order point worth making, because it is what an examiner rewards. Once the arrival is reported, the limb still asks about what is emerging — so the answer must look past SIRE 2.0 to what is genuinely unsettled at this sitting: the first real body of data accumulating under the new observation model, human-factors observation practice still maturing on both sides of the gangway, and risk-based scope extending toward cyber and crew welfare.

Vetting is voluntary in law and unavoidable in practice

No IMO instrument requires a vetting inspection. There is no convention, no code and no circular behind it. And yet a tanker that no major charterer will accept is commercially finished, whatever its certificates say.

Holding both halves of that at once is what the question is really testing. It explains why vetting can set a standard above the regulatory minimum — nothing constrains a charterer’s criteria to the convention floor — and why it moves faster than regulation, since changing an inspection questionnaire needs no diplomatic conference.

OCIMF approves nothing

The commonest single error in this question is to write about “OCIMF approval” or “passing the SIRE inspection”. There is no pass and no approval.

The inspector reports; the operator may comment; the report goes into the database; and each recipient member applies its own acceptance criteria. The same report can produce acceptance by one charterer and refusal by another, and both are correct outcomes of the same document. SIRE is an information programme, not an approval scheme — and the design reason is that the risk being assessed is each charterer’s own, so only that charterer can weigh it.

SHIP or SHORE - keep the instruments apart

A clean distinction that earns marks cheaply:

  • The inspection questionnaire examines the ship, on board, through an accredited inspector.
  • The vessel particulars questionnaire is maintained by the operator and describes the ship — it feeds the inspection rather than being part of it.
  • TMSA examines the company ashore — element by element, against staged levels, with key performance indicators.
  • CDI is the parallel scheme for chemical and gas carriers.

Ship, shore, and a different cargo sector. Three sentences, and the instruments are placed.

The four question types are the answer to the criticism

This is the most examinable content in limb (b), because each type has a design intent and the intents are not interchangeable:

  • Core — always asked, so the significant risks are never missed;
  • Rotational — drawn unpredictably from a wider pool, so the question set cannot be rehearsed;
  • Campaign — deployed fleet-wide in response to an incident or trend, so the programme can react within weeks;
  • Conditional — triggered by the operator’s own answers or the ship’s history, so scrutiny lands where this ship’s risk is.

Say why rotational questions exist and the limb (a) criticism is answered in a sentence. That link — criticism in (a), designed remedy in (b) — is what turns a two-part answer into a single argument.

Three observation tools, and the positive observation

The old model was binary: compliant or not. SIRE 2.0 observes across hardware, process and human factors.

The point most candidates miss is that the human-factors tool can record a positive observation — performance exceeding expectation. An inspection record that can say what is being done well is a different instrument from one that can only record failure: it changes what the crew expects from the visit, and it gives the operator something to propagate rather than only something to fix.

Common mistakes

  • Describing SIRE 2.0 as forthcoming, in beta or “in transition”. At this sitting it is the operative programme.
  • Writing about “OCIMF approval” or “passing SIRE”. OCIMF approves and rejects nothing.
  • Calling vetting a survey, or saying a certificate is issued. Nothing is certified and no statutory consequence follows.
  • Saying vetting is mandatory. It is voluntary in law and enforced commercially.
  • Confusing the ship-side inspection with TMSA, which assesses the company ashore.
  • Treating vetting as a duplicate of port State control. They ask different questions and carry different sanctions.
  • Answering limb (b) with only technology. It asks for trends or advancements, of which the regime change is the largest.
  • Listing SIRE 2.0 features without their design intent — especially rotational questions, whose whole purpose is to defeat rehearsal.
  • Presenting data analytics uncritically. It inherits input quality, the data is commercially sensitive, and risk-based targeting creates blind spots.
  • Saying remote inspection replaces attendance. It extends reach; it does not open a crankcase.
  • Omitting the criticism in limb (a). Without it, limb (b) has nothing to answer.
  • Quoting question counts, TMSA element numbers or inspection intervals from memory. Give the structure, not invented figures.

Examiner traps

  • “Which IMO instrument requires vetting?” None. It is voluntary in law and enforced by the market.
  • “Who approves the ship after a SIRE inspection?” Nobody. The report is uploaded, and each recipient member applies its own acceptance criteria.
  • “Can two charterers reach different conclusions on the same report?” Yes — and that is the programme working as designed.
  • “What is the difference between a vetting inspection and a port State control inspection?” The question asked and the sanction. PSC asks whether the ship is substandard and can detain; vetting asks whether the charterer should employ it and results in no fixture.
  • “What does TMSA inspect?” The shore-side management system, element by element against staged levels with KPIs — not the ship.
  • “Why does SIRE 2.0 use rotational questions?” Because an unpredictable question set cannot be rehearsed, which attacks compliance behaviour directly.
  • “Can an inspection record something good?” Yes — the human-factors observation tool can record positive observations where performance exceeds expectation.
  • “Where does a vetting observation go on board?” Into the SMS as a non-conformity under ISM element 9, with root-cause analysis and corrective action.
  • “Is more inspection always safer?” Not necessarily. Predictable inspection can produce compliance behaviour, and the burden falls on one crew — which is exactly what the redesign addresses.
  • “What are the limits of vetting analytics?” Input quality, commercial sensitivity, and blind spots wherever the risk model says “low”.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What does OCIMF stand for? The Oil Companies International Marine Forum.
  • Is vetting mandatory? No - voluntary in law, decisive in practice.
  • Who carries out the inspection? An accredited inspector nominated by a submitting member.
  • What happens to the report? The operator may comment; it is uploaded to the database; recipient members download it and apply their own criteria.
  • What is the difference between the ship inspection and TMSA? The inspection examines the ship; TMSA assesses the company's shore-side management system against staged levels.
  • What scheme covers chemical and gas carriers? CDI.
  • Name the four SIRE 2.0 question types. Core, rotational, campaign and conditional.
  • Name the three observation tools. Hardware, process and human factors.
  • Which of them can record something positive? The human-factors tool.
  • Is SIRE 2.0 still in beta? No - phased implementation began in September 2024 and it is the operative programme at this sitting.

Memory framework

For limb (a), hold four inspectors and four sanctions: flag asks does it comply → certificate; class asks does it meet the rules → class; port State asks is it substandard → detention; the charterer asks would I trust it with my cargo → no fixture.

For the programme, three words in order: INSPECT → PUBLISH → DECIDE — and the decision belongs to the member, not to OCIMF.

For the instruments, two places: SHIP (the inspection questionnaire, fed by the vessel particulars) and SHORE (TMSA). Plus CDI for chemicals and gas.

For SIRE 2.0, four question types and three observation tools: CORE, ROTATIONAL, CAMPAIGN, CONDITIONAL; HARDWARE, PROCESS, HUMAN. And one sentence to close the whole answer: the rebuild exists to defeat rehearsal.

Regulation and source map

  • No IMO instrument governs vetting. That absence is itself examinable content and should be stated.
  • ISM Code — element 9 (reports and analysis of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences) is the mechanism by which a vetting observation becomes a corrective action; elements 1.2.2, 5, 7 and 12 supply the objectives, the master’s authority, shipboard operations and company verification.
  • SOLAS chapter IX — makes the ISM Code mandatory.
  • Resolution A.1185(33), Procedures for Port State Control, 2023 — the statutory inspection regime against which vetting is defined by contrast.
  • OCIMF — operates the SIRE programme and SIRE 2.0, and publishes TMSA. Industry bodies, not IMO.
  • CDI — the parallel inspection scheme for chemical and gas carriers. Industry, not IMO.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

The corpus holds no OCIMF material of any kind, and that is stated rather than concealed. The True Source corpus’s industry file is expressly labelled citation/index only — official web sources listed; no PDFs held, and its OCIMF entry records only the organisation’s identity, that SIRE 2.0 is its tanker vetting inspection programme, and its website. There is no SIRE programme document, no inspection questionnaire of any edition, no TMSA element description and no CDI material.

Everything above about the SIRE programme, its instruments and SIRE 2.0’s design is therefore industry guidance. The one part of the answer that is primary is the contrast: port State control is drawn from resolution A.1185(33), held in the corpus, and the closed-loop mechanism is drawn from the ISM Code element 9 requirement.

No paragraph, element number, question count, scoring scale or inspection interval of any OCIMF document is cited, because that precision cannot be sourced. No named oil major, terminal operator or charterer appears, and no acceptance criterion is attributed to any of them — acceptance criteria are each member’s own and are commercially confidential. No statistic is given for inspection numbers, observation rates or fleet coverage.

One date is deliberately withheld. The answer states that phased implementation of SIRE 2.0 began in September 2024 and that it is the operative programme at this sitting, but asserts no date for the withdrawal of the previous questionnaire. The question turns on which programme was operative in April 2025, and a wrong retirement date would misstate exactly that fact. No held document records it.

Currency. The temporal risk on this question is a donor risk, not a sitting risk. No instrument boundary falls near April 2025 for vetting and nothing at MEPC 83 on 11 April touches it, so this question’s correctness does not depend on the examination day. The risk is entirely that a stem-identical January 2024 answer would describe a regime that has since been replaced — and the whole of limb (b) has been re-authored to the April 2025 position for that reason. Nothing after April 2025 is asserted about the programme’s development.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________(a) What vetting is, and what it is not
  2. __________(a) How the programme works - an information model, not an approval scheme
  3. __________(a) The instruments - and the ship/shore split
  4. __________(a) Significance in the maritime oil trade
  5. __________(a) Continuous improvement and safety culture - and the honest criticism
  6. __________(b) SIRE 2.0 - the position at this sitting
  7. __________(b) What SIRE 2.0 actually changes
  8. __________(b) Data analytics, remote inspection, and the limits
Flashcards 10

Statutory survey asks whether the ship complies with the conventions - sanction, an invalid certificate. Class survey asks whether it meets the society's rules - sanction, loss of class. Port State control under A.1185(33) asks whether it is substandard here and now - sanction, detention. Vetting asks whether the charterer would put its cargo, terminal and reputation behind the ship - sanction, no fixture.

Four inspections, four questions, four sanctions - the fastest and most complete way to define vetting.

Neither. SIRE is an information programme: an accredited inspector nominated by a submitting member reports, the operator may submit comments, the report is uploaded to the database, and each recipient member downloads it and applies its OWN acceptance criteria. The same report can produce acceptance by one charterer and refusal by another. There is no such thing as an OCIMF-approved tanker.

The commonest single error in this question, and the design reason matters - the risk being assessed is each charterer's own.

The inspection questionnaire, completed on board by an accredited inspector and fed by the operator's vessel particulars questionnaire, examines the SHIP. TMSA assesses the SHORE-SIDE management system, element by element against staged levels with key performance indicators, self-assessed and verified. CDI operates the parallel scheme for chemical and gas carriers.

Ship, shore, and a different cargo sector - three sentences that place all the instruments.

Through the closed loop into the safety management system. The observation is raised as a non-conformity under ISM Code element 9 - reports and analysis of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences - with root-cause analysis and corrective action, which the company then verifies. That is what makes the improvement auditable rather than aspirational.

'Continuous improvement' is asking for a mechanism, and ISM element 9 is the mechanism.

That inspection against a known and learnable question set can produce compliance behaviour rather than safety behaviour - what is predictable can be rehearsed, and what is rehearsed can be presented without being lived. Added to that is cumulative inspection burden: vetting, port State control, class, flag, terminal and internal audit all falling on the same crew. It is not an argument against vetting; it is the specification for redesigning it.

Without the criticism, limb (b) has nothing to answer - it is what makes the two limbs one argument.

It is the OPERATIVE tanker vetting inspection programme. Phased implementation began in September 2024, seven months before this examination. Material written for earlier sittings describes it as forthcoming or in transition; that was correct in January 2024 and is wrong here.

The single fact separating a current answer from an inherited one, and the largest development limb (b) asks about.

Core - asked on every relevant inspection, so significant risks are never missed. Rotational - drawn unpredictably from a wider pool, so the question set cannot be rehearsed. Campaign - deployed across all inspections in response to an incident or industry trend, so the programme can react quickly. Conditional - triggered by the operator's own responses or the ship's history, so scrutiny lands where this ship's risk actually is.

The intents are not interchangeable, and rotational questions are the direct answer to the compliance-behaviour criticism.

Hardware, process and human factors, replacing the old binary compliant-or-not record. The HUMAN FACTORS tool can record positive observations where performance exceeds expectation - so the inspection record can say what is being done well, not only what is wrong.

The positive observation is a structural change in what an inspection is for, and most candidates miss it.

Ship level - an operator trends its own observations by category and vessel and sees weakness before it becomes a finding. Fleet level - benchmarking integrated with the shore-side framework's KPIs. Industry level - aggregated observations identify emerging risks no single operator could see. The campaign question closes the loop: analytics identifies a trend and the next round of inspections goes and asks about it.

It shows analytics as a feedback system rather than as reporting, which is what 'integration of data analytics' is asking for.

Analytics inherits the quality of its inputs, so inconsistent observation produces confident nonsense at scale - which is why inspector quality assurance was built in alongside it. The data is commercially sensitive, constraining how much industry-level learning is realised. Risk-based targeting creates blind spots wherever the model says low risk, and the model is built from past findings so it is slow to see a new failure mode. And a tablet does not open a crankcase - remote techniques extend reach but do not replace the inspector's presence and judgement.

Stating the limits is what makes limb (b) a discussion rather than a list of features.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Vetting is a commercial risk assessment of a tanker by a charterer, oil major or terminal before employing it — voluntary in law, decisive in practice. Define by contrast: flag asks does it comply (certificate); class asks does it meet the rules (class); port State control under A.1185(33) asks is it substandard (detention); vetting asks would I put my cargo and reputation behind it (no fixture). SIRE is an INFORMATION programme: accredited inspector nominated by a submitting member reports, operator may comment, report goes to the database, and each recipient member applies its own acceptance criteria — OCIMF approves nothing. Instruments: the inspection questionnaire and vessel particulars questionnaire examine the ship; TMSA assesses the company ashore against staged levels with KPIs; CDI covers chemical and gas carriers. Significance: employment, terminal acceptance, charter rates and value, risk transfer, insurance and finance — and it sets a standard above the regulatory minimum. Improvement comes through independence, frequency, and the closed loop into the SMS as an ISM element 9 non-conformity with root cause and corrective action. The criticism: predictable inspection breeds compliance behaviour, and the burden falls on one crew. SIRE 2.0 answers that criticism and is the OPERATIVE programme at this sitting, phased implementation having begun September 2024 — bespoke compiled questionnaire; core, rotational, campaign and conditional questions, rotational ones drawn unpredictably to defeat rehearsal; three observation tools — hardware, process, human factors — the last able to record positive observations; tablet-based capture; inspectors audited in the field. Analytics runs at ship, fleet and industry level with the campaign question as the feedback loop — but it inherits input quality, the data is commercially sensitive, risk-based targeting creates blind spots, and a tablet does not open a crankcase.

Critical numbers
  • SIRE 2.0 phased implementation began in September 2024 and it is the operative programme at this April 2025 sitting
  • Four SIRE 2.0 question types: core, rotational, campaign, conditional
  • Three observation tools: hardware, process, human factors
  • Only the human-factors tool can record a positive observation
  • Four inspection regimes to distinguish: statutory survey, class survey, port State control, vetting
  • ISM Code element 9 is the non-conformity and corrective-action route
  • No IMO instrument requires vetting - the count of governing conventions is zero
Critical regulation
There is no IMO instrument. Vetting is voluntary in law and enforced commercially, which is itself the examinable point. The statutory material that surrounds it is the ISM Code — made mandatory by SOLAS chapter IX, with element 9 providing the reports-and-analysis route by which a vetting observation becomes a recorded non-conformity with root-cause analysis and corrective action — and resolution A.1185(33), the Procedures for Port State Control, which supplies the statutory inspection regime against which vetting must be distinguished.
Major trap
Describing SIRE 2.0 as forthcoming, in beta or “in transition”. The identical question was set in January 2024, when that was correct; at this April 2025 sitting phased implementation has been running since September 2024 and SIRE 2.0 is the operative programme. Reproducing the earlier answer describes a superseded inspection regime as the present one and misses the largest advancement limb (b) is asking about. The paired conceptual trap is writing about “OCIMF approval” or “passing the SIRE inspection”: SIRE is an information programme, OCIMF approves and rejects nothing, and each recipient member applies its own acceptance criteria to the same report.

Also on the platform: QP2504 Q2 - port State control, the statutory inspection regime against which vetting is best defined and from which it must be distinguished · QP2504 Q9 - maritime cyber risk management, one of the areas into which risk-based vetting scope has been extending

OCIMF neither approves nor rejects any ship.

Understand this first

A P&I club is not an insurance company and the whole answer turns on that. It is a mutual: a group of shipowners who have agreed to carry each other’s third-party liabilities, who own the club themselves, and who therefore pay calls rather than premiums — because a call is what a member of an association contributes, not what a customer pays a supplier. There is no external shareholder taking a profit, so a good year is retained for the members and a bad year is paid for by them. The second organising idea is the boundary of the cover: the club insures liability to other people, and it does not insure the member’s own loss. Hull and machinery damage belongs to the hull underwriter; lost earnings belong to a loss-of-hire policy; the club answers for the crewman injured, the cargo damaged, the pier struck, the oil spilled and the wreck to be removed. And the third idea, which the third limb is about, is why the club is present at the incident at all: because a maritime claim can arrest the ship, and the club’s letter of undertaking is what substitutes for the ship as security so the ship can sail.

Knowledge map
P&I Clubs - Funding, Cover and Claims Handling
  1. a)
    1. 1What a P&I club is - the mutual
      • a Protection and Indemnity club is a MUTUAL ASSOCIATION of shipowners, charterers and operators formed to cover THIRD-PARTY LIABILITIES arising from the operation of their ships
      • the members ARE the club - there are no external shareholders and no profit is taken out
      • so it is not a company selling a product to a customer; it is a group of owners sharing each other's liabilities
    2. 2How the club collects funds
      • funding is in TIERS, and the tiers are the answer to 'how do they collect fund from ship-owners'
      • ADVANCE CALL - levied on entry for the policy year, and paid in instalments
      • it is assessed on the member's ENTERED TONNAGE and rated for ship type, trade and area of operation, crew nationality and terms, and above all the member's CLAIMS RECORD
  2. b)
    1. 3The risks covered under 'protection'
      • PROTECTION covers liabilities connected with the OPERATION OF THE SHIP other than cargo - broadly, people and the ship's interaction with the world
      • CREW - personal injury, illness and death; medical and hospital expenses; repatriation; loss of personal effects; and crew wages in defined circumstances
      • OTHER PERSONS - injury to and death of stevedores, pilots, visitors, passengers and third parties
    2. 4The risks covered under 'indemnity' - and the boundary of the cover
      • INDEMNITY covers the member's liability to CARGO
      • loss of, damage to, shortage of and contamination of cargo carried, arising from breach of the contract of carriage
      • cargo's proportion of GENERAL AVERAGE where it cannot be recovered from cargo interests because of a breach of the contract
  3. c)
    1. 5How the club handles a claim
      • the club is not a passive payer of invoices - it takes CONDUCT of the claim
      • THE CORRESPONDENT is the first mechanism - a local representative the club appoints in ports worldwide
      • on notification the correspondent attends the ship, protects the member's position, and appoints surveyors, lawyers and experts locally
    2. 6The procedure for filing a claim
      • state it as a SEQUENCE, because the question asks for a procedure
      • 1. IMMEDIATE NOTIFICATION - notify the club, through the managers or the local correspondent, without delay. Prompt notice is a CONDITION of the cover
      • 2. DO NOT ADMIT LIABILITY - and do not sign any document accepting responsibility or quantum. Sign for receipt only
    3. 7What the ship - and the Chief Engineer - contributes
      • the club can only defend a claim on the evidence the ship preserved in the first hours
      • REPORT IMMEDIATELY through the master and the Company to the club and the correspondent
      • PRESERVE THE SCENE and the physical evidence - do not repair, discard or clean up the failed component before it has been surveyed

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  2. How the club collects funds

  2. The procedure for filing a claim

Memory cue. Three words carry the question. MUTUAL — members own it, so calls not premiums (1, 2). LIABILITY — other people's losses, never your own ship (3, 4). SECURITY — the letter of undertaking that stops the arrest, which is why the club is on the quay at all (5, 6, 7).

Remember7 route headings
Cover80 core points beneath them

Memorise the 7 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 80 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. What a P&I club is - the mutual
  • a Protection and Indemnity club is a MUTUAL ASSOCIATION of shipowners, charterers and operators formed to cover THIRD-PARTY LIABILITIES arising from the operation of their ships
  • the members ARE the club - there are no external shareholders and no profit is taken out
  • so it is not a company selling a product to a customer; it is a group of owners sharing each other's liabilities
  • the consequence: an underwriting surplus is retained for the members, and a deficit is met by them
  • hence CALLS, not premiums - a call is what a member of an association contributes
  • the cover is LIABILITY cover, and it is not a fixed sum insured - it is the liability the member incurs, subject to the rules
  • that distinguishes it from ordinary fixed-premium marine insurance in three ways - mutuality, breadth of cover, and the absence of a fixed sum insured
  • the club is governed by its members through a board of directors drawn from the membership, and managed day to day by professional managers
  • twelve clubs participate in the International Group, which is the industry body through which the largest claims are shared
2. How the club collects funds
  • funding is in TIERS, and the tiers are the answer to 'how do they collect fund from ship-owners'
  • ADVANCE CALL - levied on entry for the policy year, and paid in instalments
  • it is assessed on the member's ENTERED TONNAGE and rated for ship type, trade and area of operation, crew nationality and terms, and above all the member's CLAIMS RECORD
  • SUPPLEMENTARY CALL - levied if the policy year's claims and expenses exceed the advance calls plus investment income
  • this is the mutual mechanism working as designed, not a penalty - the members carry the year between them
  • RELEASE CALL - payable by a member leaving the club, buying out its exposure to future supplementary calls on the open policy years
  • policy years are kept open for several years because liability claims take years to mature, which is why the release call exists at all
  • DEDUCTIBLES - the member bears an agreed first amount of each claim, which controls small-claim frequency
  • ABOVE THE CLUB: the INTERNATIONAL GROUP POOLING AGREEMENT shares claims exceeding each club's own retention across the participating clubs
  • above the pool sits the GROUP'S COLLECTIVE REINSURANCE, and above that a further overspill layer
  • so a single very large claim is met by the whole group rather than by one club - which is what makes cover of that size possible at all
  • note the statute's own recognition of the mechanism - the Admiralty Act 2017 lists at section 4(1)(s) 'insurance premium (including mutual insurance calls)' as a maritime claim
3. The risks covered under 'protection'
  • PROTECTION covers liabilities connected with the OPERATION OF THE SHIP other than cargo - broadly, people and the ship's interaction with the world
  • CREW - personal injury, illness and death; medical and hospital expenses; repatriation; loss of personal effects; and crew wages in defined circumstances
  • OTHER PERSONS - injury to and death of stevedores, pilots, visitors, passengers and third parties
  • COLLISION - the proportion of collision liability NOT covered by the hull and machinery policy, which classically covers only three-fourths
  • FIXED AND FLOATING OBJECTS - damage to piers, jetties, berths, buoys, cables and navigational aids
  • POLLUTION - liability for oil and other pollution, and the cost of clean-up and preventive measures, beyond what the hull cover reaches
  • WRECK REMOVAL - the cost of raising, removing, destroying or marking the wreck where that is legally compelled
  • STOWAWAYS, REFUGEES AND PERSONS RESCUED AT SEA - maintenance and repatriation costs
  • QUARANTINE and disinfection expenses arising from infectious disease on board
  • FINES - for pollution, for cargo shortage or overlanding, for immigration and customs breaches, and for breaches of safety or navigation rules, so far as the rules permit
  • TOWAGE and salvage liabilities under approved contracts, and the member's liability under approved indemnities
  • LEGAL COSTS of investigating, defending or settling any of the above
4. The risks covered under 'indemnity' - and the boundary of the cover
  • INDEMNITY covers the member's liability to CARGO
  • loss of, damage to, shortage of and contamination of cargo carried, arising from breach of the contract of carriage
  • cargo's proportion of GENERAL AVERAGE where it cannot be recovered from cargo interests because of a breach of the contract
  • liabilities arising under approved bill of lading and charterparty terms, and under through and combined transport documents
  • the historical division is simple: PROTECTION is people and the outside world, INDEMNITY is the goods you carried
  • NOW STATE THE BOUNDARY, because the marks are as much in what is excluded as in what is covered
  • the club covers LIABILITY, not the member's own loss - so hull and machinery damage, and loss of hire, are NOT club risks
  • cargo the member owns is not a club risk, because there is no liability to a third party
  • cover is conditioned on the ship being CLASSED with an approved society and MAINTAINED IN CLASS, and on flag and statutory certification being maintained
  • and on the member conducting the claim as the rules require - prompt notice, no admission of liability, evidence preserved
  • PAY TO BE PAID - in principle the club indemnifies a liability the member has DISCHARGED, so the member pays first and is reimbursed
  • that is relaxed in practice, and displaced by statute where a compulsory-insurance regime gives the claimant a DIRECT ACTION against the insurer
  • the club issues the BLUE CARD evidencing that compulsory insurance - for CLC 1992, the Bunkers Convention, the Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention and the MLC financial security standards
5. How the club handles a claim
  • the club is not a passive payer of invoices - it takes CONDUCT of the claim
  • THE CORRESPONDENT is the first mechanism - a local representative the club appoints in ports worldwide
  • on notification the correspondent attends the ship, protects the member's position, and appoints surveyors, lawyers and experts locally
  • that is why a club is worth more than its balance sheet: it puts a competent, instructed person on the quay within hours, anywhere
  • THE CLAIMS EXECUTIVE in the club takes conduct of substantial claims - assessing liability and quantum, instructing lawyers, negotiating and settling
  • the club investigates whether liability exists at all before it pays anything, and defends where it does not
  • SECURITY is the club's most valuable single service - a maritime claim can arrest the ship under section 5 of the Admiralty Act 2017
  • the club issues a LETTER OF UNDERTAKING to the claimant, substituting the club's covenant for the ship as security
  • that lifts or prevents the arrest and lets the ship sail - and it is not a payment or an admission
  • the club also advises the member throughout - on the SMS response, on dealing with authorities, and on preserving privilege
  • and it applies the deductible, the pooling arrangement and the reinsurance layers behind the scenes, which the member never sees
  • SUBROGATION - having indemnified, the club steps into the member's rights of recovery against third parties, a general marine insurance principle
6. The procedure for filing a claim
  • state it as a SEQUENCE, because the question asks for a procedure
  • 1. IMMEDIATE NOTIFICATION - notify the club, through the managers or the local correspondent, without delay. Prompt notice is a CONDITION of the cover
  • 2. DO NOT ADMIT LIABILITY - and do not sign any document accepting responsibility or quantum. Sign for receipt only
  • 3. THE CORRESPONDENT ATTENDS - and appoints a surveyor to establish the facts while they still exist
  • 4. PRESERVE AND GATHER EVIDENCE - this is where the claim is won or lost, and it is largely the ship's work
  • 5. SUBMIT THE FORMAL CLAIM to the club with the supporting documentation, quantified, with the member's account of the incident
  • 6. THE CLUB ASSESSES cover, liability and quantum - is this a covered risk under the rules, is the member liable, and for how much
  • 7. SECURITY IS PUT UP where an arrest is threatened - the letter of undertaking
  • 8. NEGOTIATION, SETTLEMENT OR DEFENCE - the club conducts it, and may litigate or arbitrate where liability is denied
  • 9. PAYMENT AND REIMBURSEMENT - the deductible is borne by the member; pooling and reinsurance operate above the club's retention
  • 10. RECOVERY - subrogated recovery against third parties, and the claim's effect on the member's record and future calls
  • note that the last point closes the loop back to limb (a): the claims record rates the advance call
7. What the ship - and the Chief Engineer - contributes
  • the club can only defend a claim on the evidence the ship preserved in the first hours
  • REPORT IMMEDIATELY through the master and the Company to the club and the correspondent
  • PRESERVE THE SCENE and the physical evidence - do not repair, discard or clean up the failed component before it has been surveyed
  • SECURE THE RECORDS - engine room logbook, bell book, alarm and event printouts, planned maintenance records, running hours, oil analysis, bunker delivery notes and sounding records
  • PHOTOGRAPH everything, dated, before anything is disturbed
  • PREPARE A FACTUAL STATEMENT - what happened, in sequence, with times. Facts and not opinion, and no speculation about cause
  • ATTEND THE SURVEY and make the machinery and the records available to the club's surveyor
  • for a MACHINERY-RELATED claim - a fuel contamination, a crankcase explosion, a blackout leading to a grounding - the engine room evidence IS the case
  • raise it in the SMS as a non-conformity under ISM element 9 with root cause and corrective action, because the club will be asked what was done about it
  • and never give a statement to a third party's surveyor or lawyer without the club's instruction
Model written answer

(a) What P&I clubs are, and how they collect funds from shipowners — 5 marks

1. What a P&I club is - the mutual

A Protection and Indemnity club is a mutual association of shipowners, charterers and operators, formed to cover the third-party liabilities arising from the operation of their ships. The defining feature is that the members are the club: there are no external shareholders, and no profit is extracted.

Three consequences follow, and they are what distinguish a club from an ordinary marine insurer:

  • Mutuality. An underwriting surplus is retained for the members; a deficit is met by them. The members carry each other’s liabilities rather than buying a product from a supplier — which is why they contribute calls, not premiums.
  • Breadth of cover. The club responds to the wide and unpredictable range of liabilities that ship operation generates, rather than to a defined list of perils.
  • No fixed sum insured. The cover responds to the liability the member incurs, subject to the club’s rules, rather than to a stated policy limit.

A club is governed by its members through a board of directors drawn from the membership and run day to day by professional managers. Twelve clubs participate in the International Group, the industry body through which the largest claims are shared.

2. How the club collects funds

Funding operates in tiers:

  1. Advance call. Levied on entry for the policy year and paid in instalments. It is assessed on the member’s entered tonnage and rated for ship type, trade and area of operation, crew nationality and terms, and above all the member’s claims record.
  2. Supplementary call. Levied if the policy year’s claims and expenses exceed the advance calls plus investment income. It is not a penalty: it is the mutual mechanism working exactly as designed.
  3. Release call. Payable by a member leaving the club, buying out its exposure to future supplementary calls on the open policy years. Policy years stay open for several years because liability claims take years to mature — which is why a release call is needed at all.
  4. Deductibles. The member bears an agreed first amount of every claim, which controls the frequency of small claims.

Above the individual club sit two further layers. The International Group pooling agreement shares claims exceeding each club’s own retention across the participating clubs; above the pool sits the Group’s collective reinsurance, and above that a further overspill layer. The effect is that a single very large claim is met by the whole group rather than by one club, which is what makes cover of that magnitude possible at all.

Indian law recognises the mechanism by name: the Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 lists at section 4(1)(s) “insurance premium (including mutual insurance calls)” among the maritime claims for which a vessel may be arrested.

(b) The risks covered under “protection” and “indemnity” — 5 marks

3. The risks covered under 'protection'

Protection covers liabilities connected with the operation of the ship other than cargo — broadly, people, and the ship’s interaction with the world:

  • Crew — personal injury, illness and death; medical, hospital and funeral expenses; repatriation; loss of personal effects; and crew wages in defined circumstances;
  • Other persons — injury to and death of stevedores, pilots, visitors, passengers and third parties;
  • Collision — the proportion of collision liability not covered by the hull and machinery policy, which classically covers only three-fourths;
  • Fixed and floating objects — damage to piers, jetties, berths, buoys, cables and navigational aids;
  • Pollution — liability for oil and other pollution and the cost of clean-up and preventive measures, beyond what the hull cover reaches;
  • Wreck removal — the cost of raising, removing, destroying or marking a wreck where legally compelled;
  • Stowaways, refugees and persons rescued at sea — maintenance and repatriation;
  • Quarantine and disinfection expenses following infectious disease on board;
  • Fines — for pollution, cargo shortage or overlanding, immigration and customs breaches, and breaches of safety and navigation rules, so far as the rules permit; and
  • Legal costs of investigating, defending or settling any of the above.

4. The risks covered under 'indemnity' - and the boundary of the cover

Indemnity covers the member’s liability to cargo: loss of, damage to, shortage of and contamination of cargo carried, arising from breach of the contract of carriage; cargo’s proportion of general average where it cannot be recovered from cargo interests because of a breach; and liabilities arising under approved bill of lading, charterparty and through-transport terms.

So the historical division is simple and should be stated in one line: protection is people and the outside world; indemnity is the goods you carried.

The boundary of the cover carries as many marks as the cover itself:

  • The club covers liability, not the member’s own loss. Hull and machinery damage belongs to the hull underwriter; loss of hire to a separate policy; and cargo the member owns is not a club risk, because there is no third party to be liable to.
  • Cover is conditioned on the ship being classed with an approved society and maintained in class, and on flag and statutory certification being maintained.
  • And on the member conducting the claim as the rules require — prompt notice, no admission of liability, evidence preserved.

One further principle should be named. Under “pay to be paid” the club in principle indemnifies a liability the member has already discharged — the member pays first and is reimbursed. It is relaxed in practice, and displaced by statute wherever a compulsory-insurance regime gives the claimant a direct action against the insurer. For those regimes — CLC 1992, the Bunkers Convention 2001, the Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention and the MLC, 2006 financial security standards — it is the club that issues the blue card evidencing the insurance on which the ship’s certificate is issued.

(c) How P&I clubs handle claims, and the procedure for filing a claim — 6 marks

5. How the club handles a claim

A club is not a passive payer of invoices; it takes conduct of the claim.

The correspondent is the first mechanism. The club appoints local representatives in ports worldwide. On notification the correspondent attends the ship, protects the member’s position, and appoints surveyors, lawyers and experts locally. This is why a club is worth more than its balance sheet: it can put a competent, instructed person on the quay within hours, almost anywhere.

The club’s claims executive then takes conduct of any substantial claim — assessing cover, liability and quantum, instructing lawyers, negotiating, and defending where liability is denied. The club investigates whether liability exists at all before it pays anything.

Security is the club’s single most valuable service. A maritime claim can arrest the ship — under section 5 of the Admiralty Act 2017 a High Court may order arrest to provide security for a maritime claim. The club issues a letter of undertaking to the claimant, substituting the club’s covenant for the ship itself. That lifts or prevents the arrest and lets the ship sail, and it is neither a payment nor an admission of liability.

Behind the scenes the club applies the deductible, and the pooling and reinsurance layers above its own retention, none of which the member sees. And having indemnified, the club is subrogated to the member’s rights of recovery against third parties.

6. The procedure for filing a claim

  1. Notify immediately — the club, through its managers or the local correspondent, without delay. Prompt notice is a condition of the cover.
  2. Do not admit liability, and sign nothing accepting responsibility or quantum. Sign for receipt only.
  3. The correspondent attends and appoints a surveyor to establish the facts while they still exist.
  4. Preserve and gather the evidence — the stage at which the claim is actually won or lost, and largely the ship’s work.
  5. Submit the formal claim to the club with the supporting documentation, quantified, with the member’s account of the incident.
  6. The club assesses cover, liability and quantum — is this a covered risk under the rules, is the member liable, and for how much?
  7. Security is put up where arrest is threatened — the letter of undertaking.
  8. Negotiation, settlement or defence, conducted by the club, which may litigate or arbitrate where liability is denied.
  9. Payment and reimbursement — the member bears the deductible; pooling and reinsurance operate above the club’s retention.
  10. Recovery — subrogated recovery against third parties, and the claim’s effect on the member’s record.

The last step closes the loop back to limb (a): the claims record rates the advance call, so a claim badly handled is paid for twice.

7. What the ship - and the Chief Engineer - contributes

The club can only defend a claim on the evidence the ship preserved in the first hours, and for a machinery-related claim that evidence is the engine department’s:

  • Report immediately through the master and the Company to the club and its correspondent.
  • Preserve the scene. Do not repair, discard or clean up the failed component before it has been surveyed — a scrapped fuel pump or a discarded filter element can lose a defensible claim outright.
  • Secure the records — engine room logbook and bell book, alarm and event printouts, planned maintenance records, running hours, lube oil analysis, bunker delivery notes and retained samples, and sounding records.
  • Photograph everything, dated, before anything is disturbed.
  • Prepare a factual statement — what happened, in sequence, with times. Facts, not opinion, and no speculation about cause.
  • Attend the survey and make the machinery and records available to the club’s surveyor.
  • Raise it in the SMS as a non-conformity under ISM element 9, with root-cause analysis and corrective action — the club will be asked what was done about it.
  • Give no statement to a third party’s surveyor or lawyer without the club’s instruction.

In a fuel contamination claim, a crankcase explosion or a blackout leading to a grounding, the engine room evidence is the case — and it is perishable.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The printed split is 5 + 5 + 6, and that is the structural fact of this question. The same first two questions were set at 8 + 8 at an earlier sitting. A candidate working from that material will deliver two eight-mark answers, overrun by roughly a third, and then find they have no page left for the limb that is worth more than either of them.

So limbs (a) and (b) are deliberately tight. Limb (c), at 6 marks, is the largest single limb on the question and gets three sections — how the club handles the claim, the filing procedure as a numbered sequence, and what the ship contributes. It is also the limb a Marine Engineer Officer is most likely to be examined on orally, because it is the only one in which the engine room actually participates.

The protection/indemnity split goes in a clear two-section treatment because the split is the question. A single undifferentiated list of covered risks answers neither half of limb (b).

The one idea that unlocks limb (a): mutuality

A P&I club is not an insurance company. It is a mutual association whose members own it. There is no external shareholder, so there is no profit to be taken out and no premium in the ordinary sense.

That single fact explains everything else in the limb. It explains why contributions are calls rather than premiums. It explains why a supplementary call exists at all and why it is not a penalty — if the year costs more than was collected, the members who make up the club pay for it. It explains the release call, because a departing member must buy its way out of years that are still open. And it explains why the club can offer breadth of cover with no fixed sum insured, which no fixed-premium insurer selling a product could do.

Say the word mutual in the first line of the answer and the framing mark is banked.

Protection or indemnity? Get cargo the right way round

The commonest error in limb (b) is to put cargo damage under protection. It is indemnity.

ProtectionIndemnity
In one phrasePeople and the outside worldThe goods you carried
Typical headsCrew injury, illness, death and repatriation; third-party injury; the uninsured proportion of collision liability; damage to fixed and floating objects; pollution and clean-up; wreck removal; stowaways; quarantine; finesCargo loss, damage, shortage and contamination; cargo’s unrecoverable proportion of general average; liabilities under approved bills of lading and charterparties
Memory hookP for PeopleI for Items carried

P for People, I for Items carried. Crude, and it works under pressure.

What the club does NOT cover - worth as many marks as what it does

The club insures liability to other people. It does not insure the member’s own loss. Three exclusions follow directly and each is a standard oral question:

  • Hull and machinery damage — the hull underwriter’s risk, not the club’s. Your own main engine failure is not a P&I claim.
  • Loss of hire — the member’s own lost earnings, covered if at all by a separate loss-of-hire policy.
  • Cargo the member owns — there is no third party to be liable to, so there is no liability to indemnify.

Add the conditions: the ship must be classed with an approved society and maintained in class, its flag and statutory certification must be maintained, and the member must conduct the claim as the rules require. A ship that has fallen out of class has a cover problem as well as a class problem.

Pay to be paid, and why the blue card matters

The “pay to be paid” rule is that the club indemnifies a liability the member has already discharged — pay first, be reimbursed. Historically that meant a claimant could not sue the club directly, and an insolvent owner left the claimant with nothing.

The compulsory-insurance conventions broke that. CLC 1992, the Bunkers Convention 2001, the Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention and the MLC, 2006 financial security standards each give a claimant a direct action against the insurer, and each requires the ship to carry a certificate issued on the strength of a blue card from the club. So the club is not merely paying claims — it is the institution that makes the ship’s statutory certificates issuable. A ship whose club declines to issue a blue card cannot trade.

Why the club is on the quay: arrest and the letter of undertaking

This is the highest-value idea in limb (c) and it links directly to the Admiralty Act question on the same paper.

A maritime claim can arrest the ship — section 5 of the Admiralty Act 2017 empowers the High Court to order arrest to provide security. An arrested ship earns nothing and may be off-hire, so the commercial damage from a modest claim can dwarf the claim itself.

The club’s answer is the letter of undertaking: a written promise by the club to meet any judgment or agreed settlement up to a stated amount, substituting the club’s covenant for the ship as security. The arrest is lifted or never made, and the ship sails. It is not a payment and not an admission of liability — which is precisely why it can be given while liability is still being denied.

Limb (c) is the engineer's limb

The examiner has added a limb the earlier sitting did not have, and it is the one in which the ship participates. The point to grasp is that the club can only defend the claim on the evidence the ship preserved, and engine room evidence is perishable.

The single most damaging thing an engineer can do after a machinery casualty is tidy up. A failed fuel pump sent ashore for repair, a filter element binned, a bunker sample discarded, an alarm printout overwritten — each can convert a defensible claim into an indefensible one. Preserve, photograph, record, and only then repair.

The second is to speculate on paper. A factual statement says what happened and when. It does not say why, and it does not apologise. Cause is for the surveyor and the club’s experts, and an engineer’s written guess at cause will be read back in cross-examination.

Common mistakes

  • Calling a club an insurance company. It is a mutual; the members own it.
  • Saying members pay premiums. They pay calls — advance, supplementary and release.
  • Describing a supplementary call as a penalty for bad claims. It is the mutual mechanism operating as designed for the whole policy year.
  • Putting cargo under protection. Cargo is indemnity.
  • Saying the club covers the ship. It covers liability to third parties; hull and machinery damage and loss of hire are not club risks.
  • Omitting the class and certification conditions on which cover depends.
  • Forgetting the International Group pooling agreement and reinsurance, which is how very large claims are actually met.
  • Confusing the letter of undertaking with a payment or an admission of liability.
  • Saying the claimant sues the club directly as a general rule. That is the exception, under the compulsory-insurance conventions; the general rule is pay to be paid.
  • Quoting pool retentions, reinsurance limits or cover ceilings from memory. They are reset annually.
  • Answering limb (c) as a description rather than a sequence. The question asks for a procedure.
  • Delivering limbs (a) and (b) at eight-mark length. They are worth five each; limb (c) is worth six.

Examiner traps

  • “Is a P&I club an insurance company?” No — a mutual association owned by its members, funded by calls rather than premiums.
  • “Your main engine crankshaft fails. Is that a P&I claim?” No — that is your own loss, and it belongs to hull and machinery. The club covers liability to others.
  • “The same failure causes a grounding and an oil spill. Now?” Yes — pollution liability, clean-up and preventive measures, wreck removal if it comes to that, and any third-party damage are all club risks. The engine damage still is not.
  • “Cargo is water-damaged. Protection or indemnity?” Indemnity.
  • “A stevedore is injured by your crane. Which one?” Protection — injury to a third party.
  • “What is a supplementary call?” A further call levied when the policy year’s claims and expenses exceed the advance calls and investment income. Not a penalty.
  • “What is a release call for?” To buy a departing member out of its exposure to future supplementary calls on years still open.
  • “What is a letter of undertaking, and is it a payment?” Security given by the club to lift or prevent an arrest, substituting the club’s covenant for the ship. It is neither a payment nor an admission.
  • “What is a blue card?” The club’s evidence of insurance on which the State issues the ship’s compulsory-insurance certificate — CLC, Bunkers, Wreck Removal, MLC.
  • “What is the first thing you do after a machinery casualty that may generate a claim?” Notify the club through the master and Company at once, admit nothing, and preserve the evidence before repairing anything.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What do the letters P and I stand for? Protection and Indemnity.
  • Who owns a P&I club? Its members - it is a mutual.
  • Name the three kinds of call. Advance, supplementary and release.
  • On what is the advance call assessed? Entered tonnage, rated for ship type, trade, crew and the member's claims record.
  • How are very large claims met? Through the International Group pooling agreement, then the Group's collective reinsurance, then an overspill layer.
  • Give four protection risks. Crew injury and repatriation; third-party injury; the uninsured share of collision liability; damage to fixed and floating objects; pollution; wreck removal.
  • Give two indemnity risks. Cargo loss or damage; cargo's unrecoverable share of general average.
  • Name three things the club does NOT cover. Hull and machinery damage; loss of hire; cargo the member owns.
  • What is 'pay to be paid' and when does it not apply? The member must discharge the liability before being indemnified; it is displaced where a compulsory-insurance convention gives a direct action against the insurer.
  • Who attends the ship first after a casualty? The club's local correspondent.

Memory framework

For limb (a), one word and three calls: MUTUAL, then ADVANCE → SUPPLEMENTARY → RELEASE. Then two layers above the club: POOL → REINSURANCE.

For limb (b), the split is P for People, I for Items carried. Then the boundary in one sentence: liability to others, never your own ship.

For limb (c), the procedure runs on four verbs before anything else happens: NOTIFY → ADMIT NOTHING → PRESERVE → SUBMIT. And the club’s side runs on three: ASSESS → SECURE → SETTLE OR DEFEND.

One sentence links the whole question to the paper’s Admiralty question: the claim can arrest the ship; the letter of undertaking is what lets her sail.

Regulation and source map

  • No convention or code governs P&I clubs. They are private mutual associations and the International Group is an industry body. That absence is itself examinable.
  • Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 — section 4(1) lists the heads of maritime claim a club insures against, including (e) death and personal injury, (f) loss or damage to goods, (o) crew wages and repatriation, (u) environmental damage and preventive measures, (v) wreck removal, and (s) insurance premium, including mutual insurance calls. Section 5 — arrest in rem. Section 11 — protection against wrongful arrest.
  • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 — the governing Indian marine insurance statute at this sitting, and the source of the subrogation principle.
  • Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 — the governing merchant shipping statute at this sitting.
  • Compulsory insurance regimes for which the club issues the blue card: CLC 1992 (oil pollution from tankers), the Bunkers Convention 2001, the Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention 2007, and MLC, 2006 Standards A2.5.2 and A4.2.1 (repatriation, and contractual claims for death and long-term disability).
  • ISM Code element 9 — the non-conformity, root-cause and corrective-action route the club will ask about.
  • International Group of P&I Clubs — pooling agreement and collective reinsurance. Industry, not law.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

The corpus holds no P&I material of any kind, and that is stated rather than concealed. Its industry file is expressly labelled citation/index only — official web sources listed; no PDFs held, and records the subject at outline level: that P&I means Protection and Indemnity, that clubs are mutual and member-owned with calls not premiums, that twelve clubs pool large claims through the International Group pooling agreement and collective reinsurance, and that clubs differ from ordinary fixed-premium insurance by mutuality, breadth of cover and the absence of a fixed sum insured. No club rule book, no pooling agreement and no International Group document is held.

Everything above about club structure, cover, calls and claims procedure is therefore industry guidance. The statutory frame is primary: the Admiralty Act 2017 was read directly from the held official India Code text, and section 4(1)(s)’s express reference to “mutual insurance calls” is the statute’s own recognition of the mechanism described in limb (a).

No club rule number is cited anywhere. Club rules differ between clubs and between policy years and there is no common numbering, so a rule number would be invented precision.

No monetary figure appears anywhere in this answer — not the individual club retention, not the pool attachment point, not the limits of the collective reinsurance or the overspill layer, and not any pollution cover limit. All are reset annually and differ between clubs and layers. This is also the only real decay mechanism in the topic, and quoting none of them is what keeps the answer durable.

The figure of twelve clubs is taken from the corpus’s own note and is flagged for currency check: it changes with mergers and withdrawals. No individual club is named. No time bar for notifying a claim is quoted, because notification periods are set by each club’s rules; the answer states that prompt notice is a condition of cover without inventing a period. No section number is cited for subrogation under the Marine Insurance Act, 1963, because the corpus’s own note records that reference as requiring verification.

Currency. No instrument governs this subject, so there is no amendment window and no edition question. The Indian statutory frame is on the correct side of the statute boundary — the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 governs and the 2025 Act, which commenced 15 March 2026, is absent; the sweep for it across all eight surface classes returned no hit. The compulsory-insurance conventions cited are all long operative, and the MLC 2022 amendments (in force 23 December 2024) are operative while the 2025 amendments, approved by the ILC in June 2025, are post-sitting and absent. No in-month boundary: nothing at MEPC 83 on 11 April 2025 touches marine insurance, so this question’s correctness does not depend on the examination day.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a) What a P&I club is - the mutual
  2. __________a) How the club collects funds
  3. __________b) The risks covered under 'protection'
  4. __________b) The risks covered under 'indemnity' - and the boundary of the cover
  5. __________c) How the club handles a claim
  6. __________c) The procedure for filing a claim
  7. __________c) What the ship - and the Chief Engineer - contributes
Flashcards 11

The members own the club - there are no external shareholders - so a surplus is retained for them and a deficit met by them. It differs from fixed-premium insurance in three ways: mutuality, breadth of cover, and no fixed sum insured. Members pay calls, not premiums.

The word 'mutual' is the framing mark and everything else in limb (a) follows from it.

Advance call - levied on entry for the policy year, assessed on entered tonnage and rated for ship type, trade, crew and the member's claims record. Three calls answer 'how do they collect fund from ship-owners' completely, and the release call shows why policy years stay open.

In layers. The member bears the deductible; the club bears its own retention; claims above that retention are shared across the participating clubs under the International Group pooling agreement; above the pool sits the Group's collective reinsurance, and above that a further overspill layer. So a single very large claim is met by the whole group rather than by one club.

It is what makes cover of that magnitude possible at all, and it is routinely omitted.

Protection is people and the ship's interaction with the outside world: crew injury, illness, death and repatriation; injury to third parties; the proportion of collision liability the hull policy does not cover; damage to fixed and floating objects; pollution and clean-up; wreck removal; stowaways and persons rescued; quarantine; fines; and legal costs.
