The split IS limb (b); an undifferentiated list of covered risks answers neither half.

The crankshaft damage is NOT a P&I claim - it is the member's own loss and belongs to hull and machinery cover. The club insures liability to third parties. But if the failure causes a grounding, then the pollution liability, the clean-up and preventive measures, any wreck removal and any third-party damage ARE club risks. The engine damage still is not.

It is the sharpest test of the liability boundary and a standard oral question.

That the ship is classed with an approved classification society and maintained in class; that flag and statutory certification are maintained; and that the member conducts the claim as the rules require - prompt notice, no admission of liability, evidence preserved. A ship out of class has a cover problem as well as a class problem.

Cover is conditional, and candidates who list only the covered risks miss the conditions the club actually relies on.

The principle that the club indemnifies a liability the member has already discharged - the member pays first and is reimbursed. It is relaxed in practice and displaced by statute wherever a compulsory-insurance convention gives the claimant a direct action against the insurer: CLC 1992, the Bunkers Convention 2001, the Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention and the MLC, 2006 financial security standards. For those the club issues a blue card supporting the ship's certificate.

It explains why a claimant can sometimes sue the club directly, and why a club that refuses a blue card can stop a ship trading.

Security given by the club to a claimant - a written promise to meet any judgment or agreed settlement up to a stated amount - which substitutes the club's covenant for the ship itself and so lifts or prevents an arrest under section 5 of the Admiralty Act 2017. It is NEITHER a payment NOR an admission of liability, which is exactly why it can be given while liability is still denied.

The single best discriminator in limb (c), and it links the question to the Admiralty Act question on the same paper.

Notify the club through its managers or local correspondent without delay - prompt notice is a condition of cover. Admit no liability and sign nothing but a receipt. The correspondent attends and appoints a surveyor. Preserve and gather the evidence. Submit the formal claim with supporting documentation, quantified. The club assesses cover, liability and quantum. Security is given by letter of undertaking where arrest is threatened. The club negotiates, settles or defends. Payment follows, the member bearing the deductible. Finally, subrogated recovery against third parties - and the claim affects the member's record and future calls.

The question asks for a procedure, so the answer must be a sequence rather than a description.

Report immediately through the master and Company to the club and correspondent. Preserve the scene - do NOT repair, discard or clean up the failed component before it is surveyed. Secure the records: engine room logbook and bell book, alarm and event printouts, planned maintenance records, running hours, lube oil analysis, bunker delivery notes and retained samples, sounding records. Photograph everything, dated. Prepare a factual statement - times and sequence, facts not opinion, no speculation on cause. Attend the survey. Raise an ISM element 9 non-conformity. Give no statement to a third party's surveyor or lawyer without the club's instruction.

Engine room evidence is perishable and it IS the case in a machinery-related claim - tidying up is the single most damaging thing an engineer can do.

Because the same first two limbs were set at 8 + 8 at an earlier sitting, so material carried over will overrun them by roughly a third. The third limb - claims handling and the procedure for filing a claim - is worth 6 marks, more than either of the others, and has no counterpart at the earlier sitting. Deliver (a) and (b) tightly and give (c) the most room.

The mark split is the structural fact of the question and mismanaging it costs more marks than any single point of content.

Quick revision
15-second recall
A P&I club is a MUTUAL association of shipowners covering THIRD-PARTY LIABILITIES — the members own it, there are no shareholders, so they pay CALLS not premiums and there is no fixed sum insured. Funding in tiers: advance call on entered tonnage rated for ship type, trade, crew and claims record; supplementary call if the year's claims exceed advance calls plus investment income; release call to leave; plus deductibles. Above the club: the International Group pooling agreement (twelve clubs), then collective reinsurance, then overspill. PROTECTION = people and the outside world — crew injury, illness, death and repatriation; third-party injury; the uninsured share of collision liability; damage to fixed and floating objects; pollution and clean-up; wreck removal; stowaways; quarantine; fines; legal costs. INDEMNITY = cargo — loss, damage, shortage, contamination, and cargo's unrecoverable share of general average. NOT covered: hull and machinery damage, loss of hire, your own cargo. Conditions: in class with an approved society, certification maintained, claim conducted per the rules. Pay to be paid — displaced by direct action under CLC, Bunkers, Wreck Removal and MLC, for which the club issues the blue card. Claims: the correspondent attends and appoints surveyors and lawyers; the club assesses cover, liability and quantum, gives a letter of undertaking to lift an arrest under Admiralty Act s.5, then settles or defends, and is subrogated. Procedure: notify at once → admit nothing → preserve evidence → submit with documents → club assesses → security → settle or defend → recover.
Keywords
P&I clubmutualmember-ownedcalls not premiumsadvance callsupplementary callrelease calldeductibleentered tonnageclaims recordInternational Grouppooling agreementcollective reinsuranceoverspillprotectionindemnitycrew injuryrepatriationcollision liabilitythree-fourthsfixed and floating objectspollution liabilitywreck removalstowawaysquarantinefinescargo claimsgeneral averageloss of hirehull and machinerymaintained in classpay to be paiddirect actionblue cardCLC 1992Bunkers ConventionNairobi Wreck Removal ConventionMLC 2006correspondentsurveyorletter of undertakingsecurityarrestAdmiralty Act section 5subrogationMarine Insurance Act 1963ISM element 9bunker samplepreserve evidence
Critical numbers
  • Twelve clubs participate in the International Group pooling agreement (a figure that changes with mergers)
  • Three kinds of call: advance, supplementary and release
  • Hull policies classically cover three-fourths of collision liability, the club covering the remainder
  • Three layers above the member: club retention, the pool, then collective reinsurance and overspill
  • Admiralty Act 2017 section 4(1)(s) recognises 'insurance premium (including mutual insurance calls)' as a maritime claim
  • No monetary limit, pool retention or reinsurance attachment point is quoted - all are reset annually
Critical regulation
No convention or code governs P&I clubs; they are private mutual associations. The statutory frame that matters is the Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017: section 4(1) lists as maritime claims the very heads a club insures against — death and personal injury, loss or damage to goods, crew wages and repatriation, environmental damage and preventive measures, wreck removal — and at section 4(1)(s) expressly recognises “insurance premium (including mutual insurance calls)”. Section 5 supplies the arrest power that makes the club's letter of undertaking commercially necessary. Alongside it, the compulsory-insurance conventions — CLC 1992, Bunkers 2001, the Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention and the MLC, 2006 financial security standards — give claimants a direct action and require the certificate the club's blue card supports.
Major trap
Treating the club as an ordinary insurer and its cover as covering the ship. A club is a mutual owned by its members, funded by calls rather than premiums, and it insures liability to third parties — so hull and machinery damage, loss of hire and cargo the member owns are not club risks. A crankshaft failure is a hull claim; the grounding and oil spill it causes are P&I claims. The paired limb-(b) trap is putting cargo under “protection”: cargo liability is indemnity. And structurally, note that this sitting prints 5 + 5 + 6 where an earlier sitting printed 8 + 8 for the same first two limbs — delivering them at the old length starves the six-mark claims limb, which is the largest on the question.

Also on the platform: QP2504 Q5 - the Admiralty Act 2017, whose maritime claims and arrest power are what make a club letter of undertaking commercially necessary · QP2504 Q4 - the OPRC regime; pollution response costs are among the largest liabilities a club meets

Repeated — same wording Returns in the same words from June 2024 Q7. Also set at February 2025 Q7.

NO CLUB RULE BOOK, NO POOLING AGREEMENT AND NO INTERNATIONAL GROUP DOCUMENT IS HELD. Every statement about club rules, cover, calls and claims procedure is therefore carried at INDUSTRY GUIDANCE level; NO RULE NUMBER, NO MONETARY LIMIT, NO POOLING RETENTION FIGURE AND NO REINSURANCE ATTACHMENT POINT IS QUOTED anywhere in the answer, because none can be sourced. The Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 is held as an official India Code text and was read directly: section 4(1) lists as maritime claims the very heads a club insures against - (e) loss of life or personal injury, (f) loss or damage to goods, (o) crew wages and repatriation, (u) environmental damage and the cost of preventive and restoration measures, (v) wreck raising and removal, and (s) insurance premium including MUTUAL INSURANCE CALLS, which is the statute's own recognition of the club call. Section 5 gives the arrest power that makes club security commercially necessary, and section 11 the protection against wrongful arrest. Sources
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: true-source/08-industry-organisations/INDUSTRY_SOURCES.md, the corpus's own record - 'P&I / International Group: P&I = Protection (crew injury/death, collision liabilities to third parties, wreck removal, pollution beyond hull policies) & Indemnity (cargo claims, fines). Mutual clubs, member-owned, calls not premiums; 12 clubs pool large claims via the International Group pooling agreement + collective reinsurance. Differs from ordinary (fixed-premium, profit) insurance: mutuality, cover breadth, no fixed sum insured. Source: igpandi.org (official).' The file is expressly citation/index only, with no PDFs held.
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: the club is a MUTUAL - the members are the owners of the club and there are no external shareholders, so an underwriting surplus is retained for the members and a deficit is met by them. It is not a company selling a product to a customer; it is a group of shipowners sharing each other's third-party liabilities.
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: funding operates in tiers - an ADVANCE CALL levied on entry for the policy year, based on the member's entered tonnage, ship type, trade, crew nationality and claims record; SUPPLEMENTARY CALLS if the year's claims exceed the advance calls and investment income; and a RELEASE CALL payable by a member leaving, which buys out the exposure to future supplementary calls on the open years.
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: above the individual club sit two further layers - the INTERNATIONAL GROUP POOLING AGREEMENT, by which the participating clubs share claims above each club's own retention, and the GROUP'S COLLECTIVE REINSURANCE placed above the pool, with a further layer of overspill cover. The effect is that a very large single claim is met by the whole group rather than by one club.
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: PROTECTION covers liabilities connected with the operation of the ship other than cargo - crew personal injury, illness, death and repatriation; injury to and death of other persons on board and third parties; the proportion of collision liability not covered by the hull policy; damage to fixed and floating objects such as piers, buoys and cables; wreck removal; pollution liability beyond the hull cover; stowaways, refugees and persons rescued at sea; quarantine expenses; and fines.
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: INDEMNITY covers the member's liability to CARGO - loss, damage, shortage and contamination arising from breach of the contract of carriage - together with cargo's proportion of general average unrecoverable from cargo interests, and liabilities arising under approved charterparty and bill of lading terms.
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: the cover is subject to conditions and exclusions - it responds to LIABILITY rather than to the member's own loss, so hull and machinery damage, loss of hire and cargo the member owns are not club risks; and it is conditioned on the ship being CLASSED with an approved society and maintained in class, on flag and statutory certification being maintained, and on the member conducting the claim as the rules require.
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: the PAY TO BE PAID principle - the club indemnifies the member for a liability the member has discharged, so in principle the member must pay first and be reimbursed. It is relaxed in practice, and displaced by statute, for the compulsory-insurance regimes where a claimant has a direct action against the insurer.
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: claims handling runs through the club's CORRESPONDENT network - a local representative appointed in ports worldwide who attends on notification, appoints surveyors and lawyers, and protects the member's position on the spot; and through the club's own claims executives, who take the conduct of large claims.
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: the club issues LETTERS OF UNDERTAKING to lift or prevent an arrest, and BLUE CARDS evidencing compulsory insurance for the certificate regimes.
  • PRIMARY: Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 section 4(1), read from the held official India Code text - the heads of maritime claim include (e) loss of life or personal injury in direct connection with the operation of a vessel; (f) loss or damage to or in connection with any goods; (o) crew wages, repatriation costs and social insurance contributions; (u) damage or threat of damage to the environment and the cost of preventive and restoration measures; (v) wreck raising, removal, recovery, destruction or rendering harmless; and (s) INSURANCE PREMIUM, INCLUDING MUTUAL INSURANCE CALLS, in respect of the vessel
  • PRIMARY: Admiralty Act 2017 section 5 - arrest in rem to provide security against a maritime claim; section 11 - the court may require the arresting claimant to give an undertaking or security against wrongful or unjustified arrest. Together these are why club security has commercial value: the claim can arrest the ship, and the letter of undertaking substitutes for the ship as security
  • CONTEXT: compulsory insurance regimes for which the club issues the evidencing certificate - CLC 1992 for oil pollution from tankers, the Bunkers Convention 2001, the Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention 2007, and MLC, 2006 Standards A2.5.2 and A4.2.1 for repatriation and contractual claims
  • CONTEXT: the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 governs marine insurance in India at this sitting. The corpus records its subrogation section as REQUIRING VERIFICATION, so no section number is cited
  • INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED: QP2502-Q7 (February 2025), built - limbs (a) and (b) are word-for-word identical and their material transfers, re-delivered at 5 marks each instead of 8; limb (c) has no counterpart there and is fresh

Understand this first

This question looks like four unrelated tasks and is really one: it is checking whether you have read the circular it names. The systems list, the elements and the IT/OT distinction are all inside that one document, in its own terms. The idea worth taking away is that cyber risk was brought into shipping not as a new technical subject but as an extension of risk management the industry already does — which is exactly why a recommendatory circular sits alongside a resolution that puts the obligation inside the ISM Code. Nobody wrote a Cyber Code; they said you already have a safety management system, put this in it. On this paper there is one further thing, and it is unusual. The circular was revised on 4 April 2025 — inside this examination month — and the revision changes three of the four limbs. The source copy prints no examination day, so neither you nor anyone else can know which edition governed the candidate in the room. The professional answer to that is not to guess: it is to name the revision you are answering on, which is correct technique in any event and cannot be marked wrong either way.

Knowledge map
Maritime Cyber Risk Management
  1. framing
    1. 1The instrument, its status, and the edition question
      • the stem names MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3, Guidelines on Maritime Cyber Risk Management
      • approved JOINTLY by the Facilitation Committee and the Maritime Safety Committee in 2017, superseding the interim guidelines in MSC.1/Circ.1526
      • maritime cyber risk - a measure of the extent to which a technology asset could be threatened by a potential circumstance or event
  2. (A)
    1. 2The on-board systems exposed to cyber risk
      • the Guidelines say vulnerable systems COULD INCLUDE, BUT ARE NOT LIMITED TO - this is a floor, not a closed list
      • the stem quotes the Guidelines' own reasoning - the vulnerabilities created by ACCESSING, INTERCONNECTING OR NETWORKING these systems create the risk
      • BRIDGE SYSTEMS - navigation, safety and communication systems: ECDIS, radar and ARPA, GNSS receivers, AIS, VDR, the integrated navigation system, the autopilot
  3. (B)
    1. 3What cyber risk management means, and where it starts
      • THIS MATERIAL IS UNCHANGED BETWEEN THE EDITIONS, so it is the safe ground of limb (B) and comes first
      • annex 3.1 - cyber risk management is the process of IDENTIFYING, ANALYSING, ASSESSING AND COMMUNICATING a cyber-related risk
      • and then ACCEPTING, AVOIDING, TRANSFERRING OR MITIGATING it to an acceptable level
    2. 4The functional elements - and how many there are
      • annex 3.5 sets out the functional elements, and states that they are NOT SEQUENTIAL - all should be concurrent and continuous
      • UNDER Rev.2, of 7 June 2022, there are FIVE - IDENTIFY, PROTECT, DETECT, RESPOND, RECOVER
      • UNDER Rev.3, of 4 April 2025, there are SIX - GOVERN is added and placed FIRST
  4. (C)
    1. 5Why cyber security matters in the maritime sector
      • argue it from the Guidelines' own reasoning, not from general cyber-security talk
      • THE RISK HAS MOVED OUT OF THE PHYSICAL DOMAIN - a ship can now be harmed without anyone being near it
      • A BRIDGE OR PROPULSION COMPROMISE IS A SAFETY EVENT, not an IT inconvenience - loss of steering, propulsion or position is a collision or grounding risk
  5. (D)
    1. 6Information technology and operational technology
      • the Guidelines distinguish them by WHAT THE DATA IS FOR - not by the hardware
      • IT focuses on the USE OF DATA AS INFORMATION
      • OT focuses on the USE OF DATA TO CONTROL OR MONITOR PHYSICAL PROCESSES
  6. closing
    1. 7How a recommendatory circular becomes a real obligation
      • THREE LAYERS, and keeping them apart is worth a mark
      • MSC.428(98), adopted 16 June 2017, IS THE OBLIGATION - cyber risk must be appropriately addressed in the safety management system
      • the deadline was the FIRST ANNUAL VERIFICATION OF THE COMPANY'S DOCUMENT OF COMPLIANCE AFTER 1 JANUARY 2021 - long past at this sitting
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

framing

Memory cue. One document answers all four limbs — so name the circular and its revision first (1). Then the four limbs are systems (2), meaning and elements (3, 4), why it matters (5), IT versus OT (6). And the mark most candidates miss is the closing one: recommendatory guidance, mandatory ISM duty (7).

Remember7 route headings
Cover80 core points beneath them

Memorise the 7 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 80 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. The instrument, its status, and the edition question
  • the stem names MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3, Guidelines on Maritime Cyber Risk Management
  • approved JOINTLY by the Facilitation Committee and the Maritime Safety Committee in 2017, superseding the interim guidelines in MSC.1/Circ.1526
  • maritime cyber risk - a measure of the extent to which a technology asset could be threatened by a potential circumstance or event
  • which may result in shipping-related operational, safety or security failures as a consequence of information or systems being CORRUPTED, LOST OR COMPROMISED
  • note the three verbs - it is not only about attack; accident and failure are in scope, and AVAILABILITY counts
  • THE GUIDELINES ARE EXPRESSLY RECOMMENDATORY - section 7 below explains why that does not make them optional
  • THE EDITION MATTERS ON THIS PAPER MORE THAN ON ANY OTHER
  • Rev.2 is dated 7 June 2022; Rev.3 is dated 4 APRIL 2025 - inside this examination month
  • Rev.3 changes three of the four printed limbs - the systems list, the number of functional elements, and the IT/OT treatment
  • NAME THE REVISION YOU ARE ANSWERING ON - that is correct technique and it cannot be marked wrong
  • Rev.3 also introduces the organising term Computer Based System (CBS), covering both IT and OT systems on board
2. The on-board systems exposed to cyber risk
  • the Guidelines say vulnerable systems COULD INCLUDE, BUT ARE NOT LIMITED TO - this is a floor, not a closed list
  • the stem quotes the Guidelines' own reasoning - the vulnerabilities created by ACCESSING, INTERCONNECTING OR NETWORKING these systems create the risk
  • BRIDGE SYSTEMS - navigation, safety and communication systems: ECDIS, radar and ARPA, GNSS receivers, AIS, VDR, the integrated navigation system, the autopilot
  • CARGO HANDLING AND MANAGEMENT SYSTEMS - loading computer, ballast control, cargo control room, level and valve remote control, container reefer monitoring
  • PROPULSION, MACHINERY MANAGEMENT AND POWER CONTROL SYSTEMS - engine control room and bridge control, alarm and monitoring, power management, governor and PMS servers, remote vendor diagnostics
  • ACCESS CONTROL SYSTEMS - CCTV, bridge navigational watch alarm, ship security alert and shipboard security systems
  • PASSENGER SERVICING AND MANAGEMENT SYSTEMS - boarding, property management and passenger data systems
  • PASSENGER-FACING PUBLIC NETWORKS - guest wifi and entertainment, which are exposed by design
  • ADMINISTRATIVE AND CREW WELFARE SYSTEMS - ship administration, planned maintenance, purchasing, and crew internet and personal devices
  • COMMUNICATION SYSTEMS - satellite communications, VoIP, email and the shore link generally
  • under Rev.2 those are the EIGHT categories at annex 2.1.1; Rev.3 restructures the same ground into NINE at annex 2.2.1, rewriting the headings rather than simply appending one
  • so name the categories with a shipboard example each, and say which edition you are counting
3. What cyber risk management means, and where it starts
  • THIS MATERIAL IS UNCHANGED BETWEEN THE EDITIONS, so it is the safe ground of limb (B) and comes first
  • annex 3.1 - cyber risk management is the process of IDENTIFYING, ANALYSING, ASSESSING AND COMMUNICATING a cyber-related risk
  • and then ACCEPTING, AVOIDING, TRANSFERRING OR MITIGATING it to an acceptable level
  • note that this is the ordinary risk-management vocabulary the industry already uses - that is the whole design idea
  • annex 3.2 - THE GOAL IS OPERATIONAL RESILIENCE, not immunity. Nobody claims a ship can be made un-attackable
  • annex 3.3 - effective cyber risk management SHOULD START AT SENIOR MANAGEMENT LEVEL, not with the ETO
  • annex 3.4 - compare the CURRENT cyber-risk posture with the DESIRED posture, and produce a PRIORITISED plan to close the gap
  • the controls are of three kinds - MANAGEMENT, PROCEDURAL AND TECHNICAL - and a purely technical answer is an incomplete one
4. The functional elements - and how many there are
  • annex 3.5 sets out the functional elements, and states that they are NOT SEQUENTIAL - all should be concurrent and continuous
  • UNDER Rev.2, of 7 June 2022, there are FIVE - IDENTIFY, PROTECT, DETECT, RESPOND, RECOVER
  • UNDER Rev.3, of 4 April 2025, there are SIX - GOVERN is added and placed FIRST
  • GOVERN - designate a person or entity ACCOUNTABLE for planning, resourcing and executing cybersecurity activities, with the authority, support and expertise to do it
  • IDENTIFY - the systems, assets, data and capabilities which, if disrupted, pose risks to ship operations; know what you have before you defend it
  • PROTECT - risk control processes and measures, and contingency planning, to protect against a cyber event and ensure continuity
  • DETECT - activities to identify a cyber event in a timely manner
  • RESPOND - activities and plans to provide resilience and restore systems necessary for shipping operations or services impaired by a cyber event
  • RECOVER - measures to back up and restore cyber systems necessary for shipping operations impacted by a cyber event
  • annex 3.6 - together they constitute an ONGOING PROCESS WITH EFFECTIVE FEEDBACK MECHANISMS
  • annex 3.7 - awareness must be appropriate to each person's ROLE
  • so: five under Rev.2, six under Rev.3 with Govern first - name the edition and the answer stands either way
  • and whichever you give, DO NOT write them as a one-pass sequence - that is the commonest error on this limb
5. Why cyber security matters in the maritime sector
  • argue it from the Guidelines' own reasoning, not from general cyber-security talk
  • THE RISK HAS MOVED OUT OF THE PHYSICAL DOMAIN - a ship can now be harmed without anyone being near it
  • A BRIDGE OR PROPULSION COMPROMISE IS A SAFETY EVENT, not an IT inconvenience - loss of steering, propulsion or position is a collision or grounding risk
  • IT-TO-OT PROPAGATION - the classic route is a chart update on a memory stick or a remote diagnostic session, and an air-gapped ship is infected by removable media
  • BENIGN ACTIONS COUNT - the definition covers information or systems being corrupted, lost or compromised, so a mistaken software update is in scope as much as an attack
  • THE SHIP IS STRUCTURALLY HARD TO DEFEND - mixed vendor equipment of different ages, a small crew with no specialist, a thin and intermittent shore link, long asset lives
  • and equipment that cannot simply be patched, because a machinery control system cannot be rebooted mid-passage
  • TECHNICAL STANDARDS ALONE CANNOT KEEP UP - the threat changes faster than any standard can be amended, which is precisely why the response is risk management rather than a prescriptive rule
  • COMMERCIAL AND REGULATORY CONSEQUENCES - charterer and vetting expectations, insurance, port and terminal requirements, and the ISM consequence in section 7
  • THE SUPPLY CHAIN - third-party vendors, embedded systems and maintenance devices brought aboard are part of the ship's attack surface
6. Information technology and operational technology
  • the Guidelines distinguish them by WHAT THE DATA IS FOR - not by the hardware
  • IT focuses on the USE OF DATA AS INFORMATION
  • OT focuses on the USE OF DATA TO CONTROL OR MONITOR PHYSICAL PROCESSES
  • Rev.3 promotes both to FORMAL DEFINITIONS at annex 2.1 and supplies worked examples, which the limb expressly asks for
  • IT EXAMPLES - ship administration and business systems, planned maintenance and purchasing, email, crew welfare and internet, personnel records, electronic manuals and certificates
  • OT EXAMPLES - engine and machinery control and alarm systems, power management, ballast and cargo control, ECDIS and the integrated navigation system, steering gear control, refrigeration and inert gas control
  • CLASSIFY BY FUNCTION, NOT BY BOX - a loading computer is IT when it produces a stability report and OT when it drives valve and pump control
  • and an alarm and monitoring system is OT even though it controls nothing, because it monitors a physical process
  • THE PRIORITY INVERSION - ashore, CONFIDENTIALITY usually leads; on the plant side, SAFETY AND AVAILABILITY lead
  • which is why office IT practice cannot simply be applied to the engine room - you cannot take the main engine control offline for a patch cycle
  • CONVERGENCE IS THE REAL RISK - the two are no longer separate, and under the Rev.3 vocabulary both are Computer Based Systems
  • a chart update on a memory stick, a remote vendor diagnostic session, or a loading computer reading from the business network all cross the boundary
  • so NETWORK SEGREGATION is the primary control - Rev.3 states it as a duty at annex 2.2.2: OT segmented from IT, protected from Internet-facing systems, and given appropriate protection tools
7. How a recommendatory circular becomes a real obligation
  • THREE LAYERS, and keeping them apart is worth a mark
  • MSC.428(98), adopted 16 June 2017, IS THE OBLIGATION - cyber risk must be appropriately addressed in the safety management system
  • the deadline was the FIRST ANNUAL VERIFICATION OF THE COMPANY'S DOCUMENT OF COMPLIANCE AFTER 1 JANUARY 2021 - long past at this sitting
  • MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 IS THE RECOMMENDATORY GUIDANCE on how to do it
  • INDUSTRY AND CLASS MATERIAL IS THE DETAIL - the industry Guidelines on Cyber Security Onboard Ships, IACS recommendations, ISO/IEC 27001 and the NIST Framework, named precisely because the Organization does not intend to write it
  • and the Guidelines require the MOST CURRENT VERSION of whatever is used
  • SO THE POSITION IS: the GUIDANCE on how to manage cyber risk is recommendatory, but WHETHER it is managed at all is an ISM Code matter
  • verified at Document of Compliance and Safety Management Certificate audit, and capable of being written up as a non-conformity
  • and capable of becoming a PORT STATE CONTROL DEFICIENCY under the Procedures for Port State Control, since ISM compliance is inspectable
  • A SECOND LAYER APPLIES AT THIS SITTING - IACS unified requirements UR E26 (cyber resilience of ships) and UR E27 (cyber resilience of on-board systems and equipment)
  • they apply to ships CONTRACTED FOR CONSTRUCTION ON OR AFTER 1 JULY 2024 - nine months before this examination
  • so for newer tonnage cyber resilience is a CLASS requirement and not only an SMS one, and mentioning it shows exactly the currency awareness this subject rewards
  • in practice on board: procedures under ISM element 7, maintenance under element 10, non-conformities under element 9, and company review under element 12
Model written answer

1. The instrument, its status, and the edition question

The question names MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3, the Guidelines on Maritime Cyber Risk Management. The circular was approved jointly by the Facilitation Committee and the Maritime Safety Committee in 2017, superseding the interim guidelines in MSC.1/Circ.1526.

The Guidelines define maritime cyber risk as a measure of the extent to which a technology asset could be threatened by a potential circumstance or event, which may result in shipping-related operational, safety or security failures as a consequence of information or systems being corrupted, lost or compromised. Note those three verbs: the subject is not only attack. Accident, error and failure are in scope, and availability counts as much as confidentiality.

The Guidelines are expressly recommendatory. Section 7 below explains why that does not make them optional.

On this paper the edition matters more than on any other question. Rev.2 is dated 7 June 2022. Rev.3 is dated 4 April 2025 — inside this examination month — and it changes three of the four printed limbs: the vulnerable-systems list, the number of functional elements, and the treatment of IT and OT. The source copy prints no examination day, so which edition governed the candidate in the room cannot be known.

The professional response is not to guess but to name the revision you are answering on. That is correct technique in any event — this circular has been revised repeatedly and a great deal of published material still reflects the older text — and on this paper it makes the answer defensible on either side of 4 April. Both editions are therefore identified below and each proposition is attributed. Rev.3 also introduces the organising term Computer Based System (CBS), covering both IT and OT systems on board.

2. The on-board systems exposed to cyber risk

The Guidelines state that vulnerable systems “could include, but are not limited to” the categories listed — so this is a floor, not a closed list. The stem quotes the Guidelines’ own reasoning: it is the vulnerabilities created by accessing, interconnecting or networking these systems that create the risk.

  • Bridge systems — navigation, safety and communication equipment: ECDIS, radar and ARPA, GNSS receivers, AIS, VDR, the integrated navigation system and the autopilot.
  • Cargo handling and management systems — loading computer, ballast control, cargo control room, remote level and valve control, reefer container monitoring.
  • Propulsion, machinery management and power control systems — engine control room and bridge control, alarm and monitoring, power management, governors, planned maintenance servers and remote vendor diagnostic links.
  • Access control systems — CCTV, bridge navigational watch alarm system, ship security alert system and shipboard security arrangements.
  • Passenger servicing and management systems — boarding, property management and passenger data systems.
  • Passenger-facing public networks — guest wifi and entertainment systems, which are exposed by design.
  • Administrative and crew welfare systems — ship administration, planned maintenance, purchasing, and crew internet and personal devices.
  • Communication systems — satellite communications, VoIP, email and the shore link generally.

Those are the eight categories as Rev.2 sets them out at annex 2.1.1. Rev.3 restructures the same ground into nine at annex 2.2.1, rewriting the headings rather than simply appending a category. Name the categories with a shipboard example each — the marks here are in the naming — and say which edition you are counting.

3. What cyber risk management means, and where it starts

This material is unchanged between the editions, so it is the secure ground of this limb.

Cyber risk management is the process of identifying, analysing, assessing and communicating a cyber-related risk, and then accepting, avoiding, transferring or mitigating it to an acceptable level. That is the ordinary risk-management vocabulary the industry already uses — which is the whole design idea behind the Guidelines.

Three further propositions carry marks:

  1. The goal is operational resilience, not immunity. The Guidelines do not claim a ship can be made un-attackable; they aim at a ship that keeps operating, and recovers, when something goes wrong.
  2. Effective cyber risk management should start at senior management level — not with the electro-technical officer and not with the IT department. It is a management responsibility that flows down.
  3. Compare the current cyber-risk posture with the desired posture, and produce a prioritised plan to close the gap.

The controls are of three kinds — management, procedural and technical — and an answer that offers only firewalls and antivirus is an incomplete one.

4. The functional elements - and how many there are

The Guidelines set out the functional elements and state expressly that they are not sequential: all should be concurrent and continuous and incorporated appropriately in a risk-management framework.

Under Rev.2 (7 June 2022) there are five. Under Rev.3 (4 April 2025) there are six, Govern having been added and placed first.

ElementWhat it requiresEdition
GovernDesignate a person or entity accountable for planning, resourcing and executing cybersecurity activities, with the authority, support and expertise to do itRev.3 only — and placed first
IdentifyThe systems, assets, data and capabilities which, if disrupted, pose risks to ship operations — know what you have before you defend itBoth
ProtectRisk control processes and measures, and contingency planning, to protect against a cyber event and ensure continuityBoth
DetectActivities to identify a cyber event in a timely mannerBoth
RespondActivities and plans to provide resilience and restore systems necessary for shipping operations impaired by a cyber eventBoth
RecoverMeasures to back up and restore cyber systems necessary for shipping operations impacted by a cyber eventBoth

Together the elements constitute an ongoing process with effective feedback mechanisms, and awareness must be appropriate to each person’s role. Whichever count applies, the point that must be made is the same: do not write them as a one-pass sequence to be worked through once. That is the commonest error on this limb, and it is wrong under both editions.

5. Why cyber security matters in the maritime sector

Argue this from the Guidelines’ own reasoning rather than from general cyber-security commentary:

  • The risk has moved out of the physical domain. A ship can now be harmed by someone who is nowhere near it — a change in the nature of maritime risk, not merely an addition to it.
  • A bridge or propulsion compromise is a safety event, not an IT inconvenience. Loss of steering, propulsion, power or position is a collision, grounding or pollution risk.
  • IT-to-OT propagation is the classic route. A chart update carried aboard on a memory stick, or a remote diagnostic session opened to an engine vendor, crosses from the office side to the plant side. An air-gapped ship is infected by removable media.
  • Benign actions count. The definition covers information or systems being corrupted, lost or compromised — so a mistaken software update or a failed configuration change is in scope as much as a deliberate attack.
  • The ship is structurally hard to defend. Mixed vendor equipment of widely different ages; a small crew with no specialist; a thin and intermittent shore link; asset lives measured in decades; and control systems that cannot simply be patched, because a machinery control system cannot be rebooted mid-passage.
  • Technical standards alone cannot keep up. The threat changes faster than any standard can be amended — which is precisely why the response is risk management rather than a prescriptive technical rule.
  • The supply chain is part of the attack surface — third-party vendors, embedded systems and maintenance laptops brought aboard.
  • The consequences are commercial as well as operational — charterer and vetting expectations, insurance, port and terminal requirements, and the ISM consequence set out in section 7.

6. Information technology and operational technology

The Guidelines distinguish them by what the data is for, not by the hardware. IT focuses on the use of data as information; OT focuses on the use of data to control or monitor physical processes. Rev.3 promotes both to formal definitions at annex 2.1 and supplies worked examples, which is exactly what this limb asks for.

Information technology (IT)Operational technology (OT)
Purpose of the dataData as informationData to control or monitor a physical process
Shipboard examplesShip administration and business systems; planned maintenance and purchasing; email; crew welfare and internet; personnel records; electronic manuals and certificatesEngine and machinery control and alarm systems; power management; ballast and cargo control; ECDIS and the integrated navigation system; steering gear control; refrigeration and inert gas control
What is protected firstConfidentiality, then integrity, then availabilitySafety and availability first — the plant must keep running
Patching and downtimeRoutine, scheduled, tolerableDifficult — you cannot take main engine control offline for a patch cycle

Classify by function, not by box. A loading computer is IT when it produces a stability report and OT when it drives valve and pump control. An alarm and monitoring system is OT even though it controls nothing, because it monitors a physical process.

The examinable point is convergence. The two are no longer separate domains, and under the Rev.3 vocabulary both are Computer Based Systems. A chart update on a memory stick, a remote vendor diagnostic session, or a loading computer reading from the business network all cross the boundary. Network segregation is therefore the primary control — and Rev.3 states it as a duty at annex 2.2.2: OT systems should be segmented from IT systems, protected from Internet-facing systems, and provided with appropriate protection tools.

7. How a recommendatory circular becomes a real obligation

This is the highest-value content in the answer and it belongs to none of the four printed limbs. Three layers, and keeping them apart is worth a mark:

  1. The obligation is resolution MSC.428(98), adopted 16 June 2017: an approved safety management system should take account of cyber risk management in accordance with the objectives and functional requirements of the ISM Code, and Administrations were encouraged to ensure cyber risks are appropriately addressed in safety management systems no later than the first annual verification of the company’s Document of Compliance after 1 January 2021. That date is long past at this examination.
  2. The guidance on how to do it is MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3, which is recommendatory.
  3. The detail is industry and class material — the industry Guidelines on Cyber Security Onboard Ships, IACS recommendations, ISO/IEC 27001 and the NIST Framework — named precisely because the Organization does not intend to write it, and to be used in its most current version.

So the position is: the guidance on how to manage cyber risk is recommendatory, but whether it is managed at all is an ISM Code matter. It is verified at Document of Compliance and Safety Management Certificate audit, can be written up as a non-conformity, and can become a port State control deficiency, since ISM compliance is inspectable under the Procedures for Port State Control.

A second layer applies at this sitting, and mentioning it shows real currency. IACS unified requirements UR E26 (cyber resilience of ships) and UR E27 (cyber resilience of on-board systems and equipment) apply to ships contracted for construction on or after 1 July 2024 — nine months before this examination. For newer tonnage, cyber resilience is a class requirement as well as an SMS one.

On board, the work lives in the ordinary ISM machinery: procedures under element 7, maintenance and software management under element 10, non-conformities, incidents and near-misses under element 9, and company review and verification under element 12. That is the honest answer to “is it mandatory?” — the circular is not; the duty is.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks, four printed limbs, and the paper prints the total (16) but no limb split — so budget roughly four marks each and do not let limb (A) run away with the page. (A) is a list and the marks are in the naming, with a few words of shipboard example each. (B) is the heart of the question and is really two things — the definition and posture, and the functional elements — which is why the answer splits them; that split also puts the edition-independent material first. (C) is the only discursive limb and should be anchored in the Guidelines’ own reasoning rather than in general cyber-security talk. (D) is a definitional distinction and a table earns its marks faster than prose.

Section 7 belongs to none of the four printed limbs, and it is where the best marks are. The examiner who set this question knows that the circular is recommendatory; the candidate who explains how a recommendatory circular still binds has understood the architecture rather than memorised a list.

The edition boundary - and how to be right without knowing the date

This is the hardest single thing on this paper. MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3/Rev.3 is dated 4 April 2025 — inside this examination month. The source copy prints no examination day, so nobody can know which edition governed the candidate in the room.

And unlike the paper’s other in-month boundary, this one changes the substance of three limbs:

LimbRev.2 (7 June 2022)Rev.3 (4 April 2025)
(A) vulnerable systemsEight categories, annex 2.1.1Nine categories, annex 2.2.1 — a restructure, not an append
(B) functional elementsFive — Identify, Protect, Detect, Respond, RecoverSix — Govern added and placed first
(C) importanceUnchanged in substanceUnchanged in substance
(D) IT and OTLoose description at annex 2.1.2Formal definitions at annex 2.1, with examples

The answer to an unknowable date is not a guess and not a silent hedge. It is to name the revision you are answering on. That is correct examination technique whatever the paper — this circular has been revised repeatedly and a great deal of textbook and training material still reflects Rev.2 — and here it makes the answer defensible on both sides of 4 April. Write “answering on Rev.3 of 4 April 2025, which sets six functional elements; Rev.2, in force until that date, set five” and no examiner can mark it wrong.

The mnemonic to carry is G-IPDRR, with G for Govern understood as the April 2025 addition. Learn IPDRR alone and you are giving the previous edition’s answer without saying so.

The one structural idea behind the whole subject

Nobody wrote a Cyber Code. That is the examinable insight.

Cyber risk was brought into shipping as an extension of risk management the industry already does, not as a new technical discipline. So the response has two parts and they are deliberately different in kind: a recommendatory circular saying how, and a resolution putting the obligation inside the ISM Code saying that it must be done at all. The message was, in effect, you already have a safety management system — put this in it.

That is why the vocabulary of annex 3.1 is the ordinary risk vocabulary — identify, analyse, assess, communicate; accept, avoid, transfer, mitigate — and why the goal in annex 3.2 is operational resilience rather than immunity. Nobody is claiming a ship can be made un-attackable.

IT versus OT - classify by function, not by box

Almost every candidate can say “IT is computers and OT is machinery”. The Guidelines distinguish them by what the data is for: information, versus control or monitoring of a physical process. That formulation is better because it classifies the awkward cases correctly:

  • a loading computer is IT when it produces a stability report and OT when it drives valve and pump control — the same box, two classifications, depending on function;
  • an alarm and monitoring system is OT even though it controls nothing, because it monitors a physical process;
  • ECDIS is OT — it uses data to monitor and support control of navigation — and it is also connected for chart updating, which is exactly the convergence risk.

The follow-on point is the priority inversion: ashore, confidentiality usually leads; on the plant side, safety and availability lead. That is why office IT practice cannot simply be imported into the engine room — you cannot take main engine control offline for a monthly patch window, and a security control that risks availability may be worse than the threat it addresses.

Recommendatory guidance, mandatory duty

Three layers, and candidates routinely collapse them:

  1. MSC.428(98) — the obligation. Cyber risk must be appropriately addressed in the safety management system, by the first annual DOC verification after 1 January 2021.
  2. MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 — the recommendatory guidance on how.
  3. Industry and class material — the detail: the industry Guidelines on Cyber Security Onboard Ships, IACS recommendations, ISO/IEC 27001, NIST — to be used in the most current version.

So the honest answer to “is it mandatory?” is: the circular is not; the duty is. Verified at DOC and SMC audit, capable of a non-conformity, and capable of a port State control deficiency.

And at this sitting there is a fourth consideration worth a mark: IACS UR E26 and E27 apply to ships contracted for construction on or after 1 July 2024, so for newbuildings cyber resilience is a class requirement too.

Common mistakes

  • Giving a number of functional elements without naming the edition. Five under Rev.2, six under Rev.3 with Govern first — and on this paper the day is unknowable, so name the revision.
  • Writing the elements as a sequence to be worked through once. They are expressly not sequential — concurrent and continuous, with feedback.
  • Treating the systems list as closed. The Guidelines say systems could include, but are not limited to.
  • Saying the Guidelines are mandatory. They are recommendatory; MSC.428(98) makes the subject mandatory through the ISM Code.
  • Confusing MSC.428(98) with the circular. The resolution is the obligation; the circular is the guidance.
  • Distinguishing IT from OT by hardware rather than by what the data is for.
  • Saying an air-gapped ship is out of scope. Removable media are the classic propagation route, and benign actions are within the definition.
  • Treating cyber risk as an attack-only problem. Corrupted, lost or compromised covers accident and failure too.
  • Saying the goal is to make the ship secure or immune. The stated goal is operational resilience.
  • Making it the ETO’s problem. Annex 3.3 puts the start at senior management level, and Rev.3’s Govern element makes accountability explicit.
  • Omitting the convergence point in limb (D), which is where the real risk lives — and missing that Rev.3 states segregation as a duty.
  • Letting limb (A) consume the answer. It is worth about four marks and the marks are in the naming.

Examiner traps

  • “How many elements of cyber risk management are there?” Name the revision. Five under Rev.2 of 7 June 2022; six under Rev.3 of 4 April 2025, Govern first. Anyone who tells you a single number without an edition is quoting one text and not knowing it.
  • “Are the elements sequential?” No — concurrent and continuous, incorporated in a risk-management framework, with effective feedback.
  • “Is the ECDIS IT or OT?” OT — it uses data to monitor and support control of a physical process, navigation. It is also connected for chart updating, which is the convergence risk in one example.
  • “Your ship has no internet. Do the Guidelines apply?” Yes. Unintended consequences of benign actions are in scope, and removable media are the classic route. An air-gapped ship is infected by memory stick.
  • “Who owns cyber risk on board?” It starts at senior management level (annex 3.3), and under Rev.3 the Govern element requires a person or entity accountable, with authority, support and expertise. It is not the ETO’s private problem.
  • “Which IMO instrument makes this mandatory?” None makes the Guidelines mandatory. MSC.428(98) makes cyber risk an ISM Code matter, by the first annual DOC verification after 1 January 2021.
  • “Can a port State control officer raise it?” Yes — as an ISM deficiency, since ISM compliance is inspectable.
  • “Is there a class requirement?” Yes, for newer tonnage — IACS UR E26 and E27, ships contracted for construction on or after 1 July 2024.
  • “What is the primary technical control between IT and OT?” Network segregation — OT segmented from IT, protected from Internet-facing systems, with appropriate protection tools.
  • “Give the definition of maritime cyber risk.” A measure of the extent to which a technology asset could be threatened by a potential circumstance or event, which may result in operational, safety or security failures as a consequence of information or systems being corrupted, lost or compromised.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which circular contains the Guidelines, and which revision applies? MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 - name the revision you are answering on, and note that Rev.3 is dated 4 April 2025.
  • Which committees approved it? The Maritime Safety Committee and the Facilitation Committee, jointly.
  • What did it supersede? The interim guidelines in MSC.1/Circ.1526.
  • Name the functional elements. Identify, Protect, Detect, Respond, Recover - with Govern added and placed first under Rev.3.
  • Are they sequential? No - concurrent and continuous.
  • Name five vulnerable system categories. Bridge; cargo handling and management; propulsion, machinery management and power control; access control; communication systems - among others; the list is not closed.
  • Distinguish IT from OT. IT uses data as information; OT uses data to control or monitor a physical process.
  • Which resolution makes it mandatory, and by when? MSC.428(98) - the first annual DOC verification after 1 January 2021.
  • Which class requirements apply, and to which ships? IACS UR E26 and E27, to ships contracted for construction on or after 1 July 2024.
  • What is the goal of cyber risk management? Operational resilience, not immunity.

Memory framework

The whole question comes out of one document, so start by naming it — the circular and its revision. That single habit answers the hardest problem on this paper.

For limb (A), walk the ship from forward to aft and then ashore: BRIDGE → CARGO → MACHINERY → ACCESS → PASSENGER → PUBLIC → ADMIN → COMMS. Eight stops under Rev.2, restructured into nine under Rev.3.

For limb (B), two halves: the verbs — identify, analyse, assess, communicate; then accept, avoid, transfer, mitigate — and the elements: G-IPDRR, with G as the April 2025 addition.

For limb (D), one question decides every case: what is the data FOR? Information is IT; controlling or monitoring a physical process is OT.

And one sentence closes the answer: the guidance is recommendatory, the duty is not.

Regulation and source map

  • MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 — Guidelines on Maritime Cyber Risk Management. Approved jointly by MSC and FAL in 2017; superseded MSC.1/Circ.1526. Recommendatory.
  • Rev.2, 7 June 2022 — eight vulnerable-system categories at annex 2.1.1; five functional elements at annex 3.5; IT and OT described at annex 2.1.2.
  • Rev.3, 4 April 2025 — nine categories at annex 2.2.1; six functional elements with Govern first; formal IT and OT definitions at annex 2.1; segregation stated as a duty at annex 2.2.2; introduces Computer Based System (CBS).
  • Annex 1.1 — definition of maritime cyber risk. Annex 3.1 to 3.4 — the process, the resilience goal, the senior-management start, and current versus desired posture. Annex 3.6 and 3.7 — ongoing process with feedback; role-appropriate awareness.
  • Resolution MSC.428(98), 16 June 2017 — cyber risk to be addressed in the SMS in accordance with the ISM Code, by the first annual DOC verification after 1 January 2021.
  • ISM Code — element 1.2.2 (assess risks, establish safeguards), 7 (shipboard operations), 9 (non-conformities), 10 (maintenance), 12 (verification and review). Made mandatory by SOLAS chapter IX.
  • Resolution A.1185(33) — Procedures for Port State Control, 2023, the route by which an ISM deficiency is raised at the ship’s side.
  • IACS UR E26 and UR E27 — cyber resilience of ships, and of on-board systems and equipment; ships contracted for construction on or after 1 July 2024.
  • External material the Guidelines point to: the industry Guidelines on Cyber Security Onboard Ships, IACS recommendations, ISO/IEC 27001, the NIST Framework — in their most current version.

Uncertainty, currency and an unusually candid evidence note

The circular this question is built on is not held by the corpus in any revision. A full-text search of the True Source corpus for MSC-FAL returns only the Cyber-Risk instrument log. What the corpus does hold, and what was read directly, is resolution MSC.428(98) — the resolution that makes the subject mandatory. So the mandatory layer of this answer is primary and the guidance layer is not.

The Rev.2 content reaches this answer through QP2403-Q5, the stem-identical March 2024 paper, which is the adjudicated donor and which sits thirteen months before this sitting — earlier, and therefore incapable of importing later law. The Rev.3 content reaches it through QP2510-Q5, a stem-identical paper from October 2025, which is six months after this sitting.

That post-sitting paper was not used as the donor. The recurrence tool preferred it, because it ranks by the size of the temporal gap and is blind to its sign; the playbook is explicit that a later solved donor does not transfer later law backwards, so reused_from is the March 2024 paper. But its Rev.3 material was re-verified rather than discarded, for one specific reason: the boundary it establishes opens inside this sitting month rather than after it. A 4 April 2025 revision is a live possibility for an April 2025 candidate in a way that an October 2025 development never could be.

The consequence is stated plainly: the 4 April 2025 date is the load-bearing fact of this question and it is not primary-sourced. It is INTERNAL_REUSE_VERIFIED, carries a currency-check flag, and the corpus should acquire both revisions as a priority. The answer’s construction does not fail if the date is wrong, because it names both editions and attributes every proposition to one of them.

No paragraph of either revision is quoted verbatim. The annex references given — 2.1, 2.1.1, 2.2.1, 2.2.2, 3.1 to 3.7 — are the structural locations recorded in the built MIW papers and should be re-verified when the circular is acquired. The exact wording of the nine Rev.3 categories and of the Rev.3 IT and OT definitions is not reproduced; both are given in substance.

IACS UR E26 and E27 are not held either — the corpus’s class folder holds notes only. Their application date is stated and no requirement of either is asserted. No industry guideline edition is dated, and no incident, vessel, company or attack statistic is named: none is held and all would be time-sensitive.

Currency. MSC.428(98) is the stable anchor — its DOC deadline is long past, so the ISM obligation is settled whichever revision of the Guidelines applies. IACS UR E26 and E27 are operative for ships contracted on or after 1 July 2024, nine months before this sitting. Nothing after April 2025 is asserted, the 34th Assembly of December 2025 is absent, and no content unique to the October 2025 regulatory position has been carried across.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________framing The instrument, its status, and the edition question
  2. __________(A) The on-board systems exposed to cyber risk
  3. __________(B) What cyber risk management means, and where it starts
  4. __________(B) The functional elements - and how many there are
  5. __________(C) Why cyber security matters in the maritime sector
  6. __________(D) Information technology and operational technology
  7. __________closing How a recommendatory circular becomes a real obligation

Three layers - obligation, guidance, detail - and collapsing them is the commonest architectural error in this question.

Quick revision
15-second recall
MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3, Guidelines on Maritime Cyber Risk Management — approved jointly by MSC and FAL in 2017, superseding MSC.1/Circ.1526, and expressly recommendatory. NAME THE REVISION: Rev.2 of 7 June 2022 versus Rev.3 of 4 April 2025, which falls inside this sitting month. Maritime cyber risk = the extent to which a technology asset could be threatened, resulting in operational, safety or security failures from information or systems being corrupted, lost or compromised — so accident counts, not just attack. (A) Vulnerable systems, “could include but are not limited to”: bridge; cargo handling and management; propulsion, machinery management and power control; access control; passenger servicing; passenger-facing public networks; administrative and crew welfare; communications — eight under Rev.2, restructured to nine under Rev.3. (B) Cyber risk management = identify, analyse, assess, communicate; then accept, avoid, transfer or mitigate. Goal is operational resilience, not immunity; starts at senior management; compare current with desired posture. Elements: IPDRR under Rev.2 (five); G-IPDRR under Rev.3 (six), Govern first — and they are NOT sequential. (C) Risk has left the physical domain; bridge or propulsion compromise is a safety event; IT-to-OT propagation by memory stick; benign actions count; the ship is hard to defend and cannot be patched freely; standards cannot keep up. (D) IT = data as information; OT = data to control or monitor a physical process — classify by function, not by box; OT prioritises safety and availability; convergence is the real risk and segregation is the primary control. The circular is recommendatory but MSC.428(98) makes cyber risk an ISM Code duty, by the first annual DOC verification after 1 January 2021 — and IACS UR E26/E27 apply to ships contracted on or after 1 July 2024.

Critical numbers
  • MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3/Rev.2 is dated 7 June 2022; Rev.3 is dated 4 April 2025
  • Vulnerable system categories: eight under Rev.2, nine under Rev.3
  • Functional elements: five under Rev.2 (IPDRR), six under Rev.3 (Govern first)
  • MSC.428(98) adopted 16 June 2017
  • Cyber risk to be addressed in the SMS by the first annual DOC verification after 1 January 2021
  • IACS UR E26 and E27 apply to ships contracted for construction on or after 1 July 2024
  • The question prints a single (16) with four limbs and no printed limb split
Critical regulation
Resolution MSC.428(98), adopted 16 June 2017, is what makes this subject mandatory: an approved safety management system should take account of cyber risk management in accordance with the objectives and functional requirements of the ISM Code, cyber risks being appropriately addressed in safety management systems no later than the first annual verification of the company's Document of Compliance after 1 January 2021. MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 itself — in whichever revision governs — is recommendatory guidance on how, not an obligation in its own right.
Major trap
Giving a number of functional elements without naming the edition. MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3/Rev.3 is dated 4 April 2025 — inside this examination month — and it added a sixth element, GOVERN, placed first. Rev.2, in force until then, listed five: Identify, Protect, Detect, Respond, Recover. The source copy prints no examination day, so which edition governed cannot be known; the professional answer is to name the revision you are answering on, which cannot be marked wrong either way. The paired trap is treating the Guidelines as mandatory: they are expressly recommendatory, and it is MSC.428(98) that makes cyber risk an ISM Code duty.

Also on the platform: QP2504 Q7 - vetting inspection, whose risk-based scope has been extending toward cyber security · QP2504 Q2 - port State control under A.1185(33), the route by which an ISM deficiency arising from unaddressed cyber risk is raised at the ship's side

Repeated — same wording Returns in the same words from March 2024 Q5. Also set at October 2025 Q5.

Production metadata (review build only)
Reuse tier D · Verification MIXED PROVENANCE, DECLARED, AND THE CENTRAL INSTRUMENT IS NOT HELD. MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 IS NOT HELD BY THE TRUE SOURCE CORPUS IN ANY REVISION. A full-text search of the corpus for the circular number returns only the Cyber-Risk instrument log, which records the folder's single holding. What IS held, and was read directly, is resolution MSC.428(98), Maritime Cyber Risk Management in Safety Management Systems, adopted 16 June 2017 - the corpus record for the Cyber-Risk instrument (sourceId TS-CYBER) confirms it as held, and it is the resolution that makes the subject mandatory by requiring cyber risks to be appropriately addressed in safety management systems no later than the first annual verification of the company's Document of Compliance after 1 January 2021. THE GUIDELINES THEMSELVES ARE THEREFORE CARRIED AT AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY AND INTERNAL REUSE LEVEL. The Rev.2 content - the eight system categories, the five functional elements, the definition of maritime cyber risk, the recommendatory status and the IT/OT description - comes through QP2403-Q5, the built March 2024 paper, whose own verification record rests on the Rev.2 text. THE REV.3 CONTENT - the date of 4 April 2025, the approval by MSC 108 and FAL 49, the nine system categories, the sixth functional element GOVERN, the Computer Based System vocabulary and the formal IT/OT definitions - comes through QP2510-Q5, a POST-SITTING MIW paper whose verification record states that both editions were read in full. THAT IS INTERNAL_REUSE_VERIFIED, NOT P1, AND IT IS FLAGGED B_CURRENCY_CHECK. It is used here only because the boundary it establishes OPENS INSIDE THE SITTING MONTH rather than after it, which makes it a live possibility for this paper rather than a prohibited import - and because the answer is constructed to be correct whether or not it applies. NO PARAGRAPH OF EITHER REVISION IS QUOTED VERBATIM in this answer; annex references are given as the structural locations the two MIW records identify, and the wording throughout is the answer's own. The corpus should acquire MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 in both Rev.2 and Rev.3. IACS UR E26 and E27 are likewise not held - true-source/07-iacs-and-class holds notes only - and are carried at industry level with their application date stated and nothing else asserted.

Sources
  • PRIMARY: Resolution MSC.428(98), Maritime Cyber Risk Management in Safety Management Systems, adopted 16 June 2017, held in the True Source corpus as the sole holding of the Cyber-Risk instrument folder (sourceId TS-CYBER, constructionStatus 'held'). It affirms that an approved safety management system should take into account cyber risk management in accordance with the objectives and functional requirements of the ISM Code, and encourages Administrations to ensure that cyber risks are appropriately addressed in safety management systems NO LATER THAN THE FIRST ANNUAL VERIFICATION OF THE COMPANY'S DOCUMENT OF COMPLIANCE AFTER 1 JANUARY 2021
  • PRIMARY (structure): the ISM Code, made mandatory by SOLAS chapter IX - element 1.2.2 (assess all identified risks and establish appropriate safeguards), element 7 (procedures for shipboard operations), element 9 (reports and analysis of non-conformities), element 10 (maintenance of ship and equipment) and element 12 (company verification, review and evaluation) are the provisions through which cyber risk is actually managed and audited
  • PRIMARY (enforcement route): resolution A.1185(33), Procedures for Port State Control, 2023, held in the corpus - the machinery by which an ISM deficiency, including one arising from unaddressed cyber risk, is raised at the ship's side
  • AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY / INTERNAL REUSE: MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3, Guidelines on Maritime Cyber Risk Management, originally approved jointly by the Facilitation Committee and the Maritime Safety Committee in 2017, superseding the interim guidelines in MSC.1/Circ.1526. The Guidelines are expressly RECOMMENDATORY. Maritime cyber risk is defined as a measure of the extent to which a technology asset could be threatened by a potential circumstance or event, which may result in shipping-related operational, safety or security failures as a consequence of information or systems being CORRUPTED, LOST OR COMPROMISED
  • INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED (Rev.2 layer, from QP2403-Q5, March 2024): Rev.2 of 7 June 2022 lists EIGHT categories of vulnerable on-board system at annex 2.1.1 - bridge systems; cargo handling and management systems; propulsion, machinery management and power control systems; access control systems; passenger servicing and management systems; passenger-facing public networks; administrative and crew welfare systems; and communication systems. It sets FIVE functional elements at annex 3.5 - Identify, Protect, Detect, Respond, Recover - stated to be NOT SEQUENTIAL but concurrent and continuous. It describes IT as focusing on the use of data as information and OT as focusing on the use of data to control or monitor physical processes
  • INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED (Rev.3 layer, from QP2510-Q5, October 2025 - a POST-SITTING paper, used only because the boundary it establishes opens INSIDE this sitting month): MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3/Rev.3 is dated 4 APRIL 2025, approved by the Maritime Safety Committee at its 108th session (May 2024) and the Facilitation Committee at its forty-ninth session (March 2025). It grows the vulnerable-systems list to NINE categories at annex 2.2.1 in a restructure; adds a SIXTH functional element, GOVERN, placed FIRST; introduces the term Computer Based System (CBS) as the organising vocabulary, covering both IT and OT systems on board; promotes IT and OT to FORMAL DEFINITIONS at annex 2.1 with worked examples; and states network segregation of OT from IT as a duty at annex 2.2.2
  • AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY: annex 3.1 to 3.4 of the Guidelines - cyber risk management means identifying, analysing, assessing and communicating a cyber-related risk and accepting, avoiding, transferring or mitigating it to an acceptable level; the goal is OPERATIONAL RESILIENCE rather than immunity; effective cyber risk management should start at senior management level; and the approach compares the current with the desired cyber-risk posture and produces a prioritised plan
  • AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY: annex 3.6 and 3.7 - the elements together constitute an ongoing process with effective feedback mechanisms, and awareness must be appropriate to each person's role
  • AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY: the Guidelines point outside the Organization for the technical detail - to the industry Guidelines on Cyber Security Onboard Ships, IACS recommendations, ISO/IEC 27001 and the NIST Framework - and require the MOST CURRENT VERSION of whatever is used
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: IACS unified requirements UR E26 (cyber resilience of ships) and UR E27 (cyber resilience of on-board systems and equipment) apply to ships CONTRACTED FOR CONSTRUCTION ON OR AFTER 1 JULY 2024 - operative at this sitting for newbuildings. The corpus holds no IACS rulebook; 07-iacs-and-class holds notes only
  • INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED: QP2403-Q5 (March 2024), built and stem-identical, THIRTEEN MONTHS EARLIER than this sitting - the adjudicated donor, incapable of importing later law. QP2510-Q5 (October 2025), built and stem-identical but SIX MONTHS POST-SITTING - read for structure only, recorded in cross_links, never in reused_from
Unresolved
  • THE CENTRAL EVIDENCE GAP, AND IT IS THE MOST SERIOUS ON THE PAPER. MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 is NOT HELD by the True Source corpus in ANY revision. The circular's content in both Rev.2 and Rev.3 reaches this answer through built MIW papers, not through a held primary text. No paragraph of either revision is quoted verbatim; annex references are given as structural locations and the wording is the answer's own. The corpus should acquire both revisions as a priority.
  • THE REV.3 DATE OF 4 APRIL 2025 IS THE LOAD-BEARING FACT OF THIS QUESTION AND IT IS NOT PRIMARY-SOURCED. It comes from QP2510-Q5, a post-sitting MIW paper. Because the date falls INSIDE the sitting month and the examination day is unknown, the answer is constructed to be correct under either revision and no scoring proposition rests on which applies. If the date were later shown to be wrong, the construction still holds: the answer names both editions and attributes each correctly.
  • The exact wording of the nine Rev.3 system categories and of the Rev.3 IT and OT definitions is NOT reproduced. The categories are named at the level the two MIW records identify and the definitions are given in substance, because a verbatim reproduction cannot be sourced from a held text.
  • NO PARAGRAPH-LEVEL CITATION IS TREATED AS AUTHORITATIVE. The annex references given - 2.1, 2.1.1, 2.2.1, 2.2.2, 3.1 to 3.7 - are the structural locations recorded in the built MIW papers and should be re-verified against the circular when it is acquired.
  • IACS UR E26 and UR E27 are NOT held. Their application to ships contracted for construction on or after 1 July 2024 is stated; no requirement of either is quoted, and no class notation is named.
  • No industry guideline edition is dated. The Guidelines require the most current version of any external material to be used, and no edition of the industry Guidelines on Cyber Security Onboard Ships, IACS Rec 166, ISO/IEC 27001 or the NIST Framework is held or dated here.
  • No incident, vessel or company is named, and no statistic on maritime cyber attacks is given. None is held and all would be time-sensitive.
