April 2025 rapid revision

Every question in one pass. Generated from each question's Quick Revision block — open the full answer from the question number.

QTopicAnswer routeCritical rule / numberMajor trap
Q1 SOLAS Chapter XV and Industrial Personnel
16 marks		 What chapter XV is, and the problem it was written to solve → Application - the ships, the operations and the four trigger points → Industrial personnel - the definition, and the counting rule that catches candidates → The safety measures - what regulation 5 actually requires → Certification, survey and the role of the flag Administration SOLAS regulation XV/2.2 — industrial personnel shall not be treated or considered as passengers — read with regulation XV/5.1, which requires the ship to be certified as a cargo ship or high-speed cargo craft, to meet the requirements of the IP Code, and to be surveyed and certified as provided for in the Code in addition to regulations I/8, I/9 and I/10.
SOLAS chapter XV and the IP Code entered into force on 1 July 2024		 Reading “more than 12 industrial personnel” as a headcount of technicians. Regulation 2.3 makes it the aggregate of industrial personnel, special personnel and passengers, with passengers capped at 12 inside that aggregate — so 8 technicians, 3 special personnel and 4 passengers crosses the threshold. The paired trap is treating industrial personnel as passengers, which regulation 2.2 expressly forbids and which would defeat the entire purpose of the chapter.
Q2 Port State Control - Clear Grounds, Detention and Review
16 marks		 Clear grounds - the definition, and why the term matters → The circumstances that establish clear grounds → Immediate actions on board - what a Chief Engineer does in the first hours → Putting the disagreement on the record while the detention stands → Reviewing the detention - what IMO provides, and what the Panel is → The roles of the company, the flag State and class Resolution A.1185(33) paragraph 2.4.1 read with 1.7.2 and 2.5.1 — absent clear grounds an inspection is limited to verifying valid certificates and documentation and forming an impression of overall condition; clear grounds, being evidence of substantial non-correspondence or of unfamiliarity with essential shipboard procedures, unlock the more detailed inspection. Paragraph 2.3.11 then governs any dispute: the company has a right of appeal and the appeal does not suspend the detention.
A.1185(33) adopted 6 December 2023 by the 33rd IMO Assembly		 Believing that disputing a detention holds it in abeyance. Paragraph 2.3.11 states expressly that the appeal should not cause the detention to be suspended, so the ship stays where it is while the argument runs — which is why the correct conduct is to rectify and dispute in parallel rather than to refuse cooperation. The paired trap is looking for the Detention Review Panel inside A.1185(33): chapter 5 is headed 'Review Procedures' but reviews the SYSTEM through summarised deficiency reports and periodic committee evaluation, while the Panel that reviews an individual detention is a regional memorandum of understanding mechanism.
Q3 Underwater Radiated Noise from Ships
16 marks		 What underwater radiated noise is, and how it is described → The primary sources on a merchant ship → The effects on marine life → The international framework - and the fact that it is entirely recommendatory → Design measures → Operational and maintenance measures, and the Chief Engineer's role There is no mandatory instrument. The subject is governed by recommendatory IMO guidelines — MEPC.1/Circ.833 of 2014, superseded by the Revised guidelines MEPC.1/Circ.906 approved at MEPC 80 in 2023 — sitting beneath UNCLOS article 194 and the article 1(1)(4) definition of pollution as including the introduction of energy. MARPOL Annex VI contains nothing on underwater radiated noise, and the SEEMP required by its regulation 26 is simply the auditable document in which voluntary measures are recorded.
Source level is expressed in decibels referred to one micropascal at one metre		 Writing about underwater radiated noise as though it were a regulated emission — referring to noise limits, compliance and a certificate. At this sitting the regime is entirely recommendatory: there is no limit, no survey and no certificate, and MARPOL Annex VI contains no provision on it at all. The paired technical trap is naming machinery as the dominant source: machinery-borne noise dominates only below cavitation inception speed, and at normal service speed propeller cavitation swamps everything else.
Q4 OPRC Convention - Purpose, Flag State Duties and Documents
16 marks		 What OPRC is, and the casualty that produced it → Purpose and objectives - and what OPRC deliberately is not → The architecture - what the Convention obliges Parties to build → The responsibilities of the flag State → The documents required to be carried on board → How OPRC and MARPOL interlock MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 is the provision that actually reaches the ship. Regulation 37.1 requires every oil tanker of 150 gross tonnage and above and every other ship of 400 gross tonnage and above to carry a shipboard oil pollution emergency plan approved by the Administration; 37.2 requires it to be based on the Organization's guidelines, written in the working language of the master and officers, and to consist at least of the reporting procedure, the contact list, the description of immediate action and the shipboard co-ordination procedures and point of contact.
OPRC adopted 30 November 1990; entered into force 13 May 1995		 Believing that OPRC itself requires the ship to carry a SOPEP. OPRC binds States, not ships: article 3 obliges each Party to require ships flying its flag to have a plan “in accordance with provisions adopted by the Organization”, and those provisions are MARPOL Annex I regulation 37, which supplies the two different thresholds, the approval requirement, the working-language rule and the four contents. The paired trap is answering the question as though OPRC were about preventing pollution or about who pays for it — prevention is MARPOL and compensation is the CLC, FUND and Bunkers regime.
Q5 Admiralty Law in India and the 2017 Act
16 marks		 What admiralty law is, and what the 2017 Act was enacted to cure → Scope, application and the jurisdiction of the High Courts → Maritime claims, arrest in rem, liens and priority → Who assessors are, and how they are appointed → Qualifications, role, and how assessors assist the court Section 3 of the Admiralty Act 2017 vests jurisdiction over all maritime claims in the respective High Courts, exercisable over the waters up to and including their territorial waters, with a proviso allowing the Central Government to extend that limit. Section 4(1) recognises twenty-three heads of maritime claim, section 5 authorises arrest in rem to provide security for them, section 9 creates five maritime liens that survive a change of ownership, registration or flag, and section 10 ranks liens ahead of registered mortgages and of all other claims. For assessors, section 13(1) requires the Central Government to appoint by notification a list of assessors with prescribed qualifications and experience in admiralty and maritime matters, and section 13(2) provides that their appointment is no bar to the examination of expert witnesses by the parties.
Act No. 22 of 2017, dated 9 August 2017		 Treating the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 as the governing merchant shipping statute. It received assent on 18 August 2025 and commenced on 15 March 2026, repealing the 1958 Act by section 324(1) — almost a year after this sitting. The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 governs, and it matters directly because the Admiralty Act cross-refers to it at section 2(2) and disapplies its sections 150 and 151 at section 4(1)(o). The paired substantive trap is confusing a maritime claim with a maritime lien: there are twenty-three heads of claim in section 4 but only five liens in section 9, and it is the lien — not the claim — that follows the ship through a change of flag and ranks ahead of a registered mortgage.
Q6 CII Rating, AER and EEOI
16 marks		 Where CII sits in law, and where the scheme has reached → The mechanism - attained, required, Z and the reference line → The rating, and what actually attaches to it → AER - what it measures and why the regulation uses it → EEOI, and the critical comparison → Operational measures to improve the rating → Technical, fuel and calculation measures, and where they are administered MARPOL Annex VI regulation 28. Regulation 28.4 gives the required annual operational CII as (1 − Z/100) × CII reference; regulation 28.6 gives the A to E rating and fixes the middle point of rating level C at the required annual operational CII, the rating being determined by the Administration or any organization duly authorized by it; and regulations 28.7 to 28.9 require a plan of corrective actions in the SEEMP, submitted for verification no later than one month after reporting, for a ship rated D for three consecutive years or rated E.
Regulation 28 applies to ships of 5,000 gross tonnage and above		 Writing the answer in the scheme's first-year frame. At an April 2025 sitting, ratings for calendar year 2023 already exist and the 2024 attained CII fell due for reporting by 31 March 2025, so “no ship has yet been rated” is false; the reduction factor is 9 % for 2025, not 7 %; and the SEEMP guidance is the 2024 edition MEPC.395(82), not the 2022 one. The paired traps are treating EEOI as a regulatory requirement when G1 lists it for trial purposes only, and saying a single D rating triggers corrective action — it is D for three consecutive years or a single E, and at this sitting a three-year run is not yet arithmetically possible.
Q7 Vetting Inspections - Safety Culture and Emerging Trends
16 marks		 What vetting is, and what it is not → How the programme works - an information model, not an approval scheme → The instruments - and the ship/shore split → Significance in the maritime oil trade → Continuous improvement and safety culture - and the honest criticism → SIRE 2.0 - the position at this sitting → What SIRE 2.0 actually changes → Data analytics, remote inspection, and the limits There is no IMO instrument. Vetting is voluntary in law and enforced commercially, which is itself the examinable point. The statutory material that surrounds it is the ISM Code — made mandatory by SOLAS chapter IX, with element 9 providing the reports-and-analysis route by which a vetting observation becomes a recorded non-conformity with root-cause analysis and corrective action — and resolution A.1185(33), the Procedures for Port State Control, which supplies the statutory inspection regime against which vetting must be distinguished.
SIRE 2.0 phased implementation began in September 2024 and it is the operative programme at this April 2025 sitting		 Describing SIRE 2.0 as forthcoming, in beta or “in transition”. The identical question was set in January 2024, when that was correct; at this April 2025 sitting phased implementation has been running since September 2024 and SIRE 2.0 is the operative programme. Reproducing the earlier answer describes a superseded inspection regime as the present one and misses the largest advancement limb (b) is asking about. The paired conceptual trap is writing about “OCIMF approval” or “passing the SIRE inspection”: SIRE is an information programme, OCIMF approves and rejects nothing, and each recipient member applies its own acceptance criteria to the same report.
Q8 P&I Clubs - Funding, Cover and Claims Handling
16 marks		 What a P&I club is - the mutual → How the club collects funds → The risks covered under 'protection' → The risks covered under 'indemnity' - and the boundary of the cover → How the club handles a claim → The procedure for filing a claim → What the ship - and the Chief Engineer - contributes No convention or code governs P&I clubs; they are private mutual associations. The statutory frame that matters is the Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017: section 4(1) lists as maritime claims the very heads a club insures against — death and personal injury, loss or damage to goods, crew wages and repatriation, environmental damage and preventive measures, wreck removal — and at section 4(1)(s) expressly recognises “insurance premium (including mutual insurance calls)”. Section 5 supplies the arrest power that makes the club's letter of undertaking commercially necessary. Alongside it, the compulsory-insurance conventions — CLC 1992, Bunkers 2001, the Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention and the MLC, 2006 financial security standards — give claimants a direct action and require the certificate the club's blue card supports.
Twelve clubs participate in the International Group pooling agreement (a figure that changes with mergers)		 Treating the club as an ordinary insurer and its cover as covering the ship. A club is a mutual owned by its members, funded by calls rather than premiums, and it insures liability to third parties — so hull and machinery damage, loss of hire and cargo the member owns are not club risks. A crankshaft failure is a hull claim; the grounding and oil spill it causes are P&I claims. The paired limb-(b) trap is putting cargo under “protection”: cargo liability is indemnity. And structurally, note that this sitting prints 5 + 5 + 6 where an earlier sitting printed 8 + 8 for the same first two limbs — delivering them at the old length starves the six-mark claims limb, which is the largest on the question.
Q9 Maritime Cyber Risk Management
16 marks		 The instrument, its status, and the edition question → The on-board systems exposed to cyber risk → What cyber risk management means, and where it starts → The functional elements - and how many there are → Why cyber security matters in the maritime sector → Information technology and operational technology → How a recommendatory circular becomes a real obligation Resolution MSC.428(98), adopted 16 June 2017, is what makes this subject mandatory: an approved safety management system should take account of cyber risk management in accordance with the objectives and functional requirements of the ISM Code, cyber risks being appropriately addressed in safety management systems no later than the first annual verification of the company's Document of Compliance after 1 January 2021. MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 itself — in whichever revision governs — is recommendatory guidance on how, not an obligation in its own right.
MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3/Rev.2 is dated 7 June 2022; Rev.3 is dated 4 April 2025		 Giving a number of functional elements without naming the edition. MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3/Rev.3 is dated 4 April 2025 — inside this examination month — and it added a sixth element, GOVERN, placed first. Rev.2, in force until then, listed five: Identify, Protect, Detect, Respond, Recover. The source copy prints no examination day, so which edition governed cannot be known; the professional answer is to name the revision you are answering on, which cannot be marked wrong either way. The paired trap is treating the Guidelines as mandatory: they are expressly recommendatory, and it is MSC.428(98) that makes cyber risk an ISM Code duty.
